8 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
somewhat later period of redaction. The overall text of the JA as we have it
now can be dated to the fifth or sixth centuries ce. There are therefore several
discernable layers in the history of the text.
To demonstrate the insight that can be gained from examining these different
layers we may return to the jātaka outlined earlier. in the story of the monkey
gardeners, on the level of the Jātaka-pāli all we have is the verse:
assistance from a fool does not lead to happiness:
a fool fails, just like the monkey gardener.
Thus there is an aphorism, with reference to an event or story that supports it.
There is no discernable Buddhist content. in the story of the past we have the
story to which the verse relates. We see that a wise man spoke the verse as a
comment on the situation, though the comment is addressed to the monkeys,
despite being about them. The wise man plays no part in the action himself. in
the story of the present we are told that this particular jātaka – the ārāmadūsaka-
jātaka – was told by the Buddha after he was informed of a similar set of events
involving a village lad and the garden of a wealthy landowner. at the end of the
jātaka, in the identification of the births, we hear that the village lad was the king
of the monkeys in those days, and the Buddha was the wise man. The Buddha
told the story of the past to show that this is not the first time the village lad has
spoiled a park. There is therefore no discernable Buddhist content in the story
itself, and the Buddha and Bodhisatta are regulated to the sidelines, yet their
presence is enough to identify the story as a jātaka.
as this brief example shows, studies of the formal aspects of the JA provide
an invaluable springboard for work on the ideological impact of the jātaka genre
more broadly. However, such studies should not be seen as the end of the road,
for an understanding of the history or structure of a text is very different to an
understanding of the history or pattern of a text’s influence on a community or
religious tradition. Whilst studies of the formal aspects of the JA have laid the
foundation for further work, it is clear that a definition of jātakas according to
their inclusion in certain texts, or their peculiar structure, will give us at best
only a partial understanding of the significance of the genre.
aRe JĀTaKaS BUDDHiST?
This may seem like a silly question. of course jātakas are Buddhist: they are
ubiquitous in Buddhist texts and Buddhist contexts. However, there has been
a tendency to treat jātakas as somehow non-Buddhist or pre-Buddhist. The
main interest in the JA when it became available to Western scholars in the
late nineteenth century was in its worth as a historical document. The first
scholar to thoroughly examine the text, T.W. Rhys-Davids, described it as ‘full of
information on the daily habits and customs and beliefs of the people of india,

What is a Jātaka? 9
and on every variety of the numerous questions that arise as to their economic
and social conditions’,19 and throughout the past century many scholars have
seen the collection primarily as ‘a storehouse of information about life and
society in ancient india’,20 despite the impossibility of dating the text accurately.
The treatment of the JA as a historical document discouraged any study of the
text’s literary qualities. The literary value of the jātakas was in any case seen
as minimal, thanks to the large amount of repetition and omission, as well as
the sometimes crude content.21 in any case they are stories, and therefore ‘low
culture’, thus the only other interest in them was as folklore, albeit as ‘the most
reliable, the most complete, and the most ancient collection of folklore now
extant in any literature in the world’.22 indeed, for Rhys-Davids, the fact that
jātakas are ancient folklore is what gives them real historical value, since they
provide insight into more ‘primitive’ cultures, and form ‘a priceless record of the
childhood of our race’.23
Such views are so obviously outdated that it is easy to forget that they are
still influential today, albeit in a more subtle form. The definition of jātakas
as common folktales remains particularly prevalent. The trouble is that a
comparativist approach to jātakas that examines their parallels in, for example,
aesopic fables or indian epic, encourages the idea that the JA is made up of
common story stock. Concomitant with this can be a lack of attention to the
effects of placing a story within a specific collection or text. The argument
might be that whilst Buddhists claim that a jātaka illustrates the biography of the
Bodhisatta, we should not expect to find any Buddhist content, since the story
is in fact – for example – Greek. Such an argument leaves jātakas with no unique
19 T.W. Rhys-Davids, Buddhist India (london, 1903), p. 189.
20 Benoychandra Sen, Studies in the Buddhist Jātakas: Tradition and Polity (Calcutta,
1974), p. i. Perhaps the greatest work done on this basis is Ratilal N. mehta, Pre-Buddhist
India: A Political, Administrative, Economic, Social and Geographical Survey of Ancient India Based
Mainly on the Jātaka Stories (Bombay, 1939), though there are also smaller studies on this
theme. However, as Rhys-Davids himself pointed out (Buddhist India, p. 189ff.), if we are
going to use jātaka stories as historical documents we must first determine what period
they refer to. The many layers and levels of redaction of the JA make it impossible to date
the incidental details with any degree of accuracy. Thus even if we were able to defend
such studies from recent scholarly attacks against treating Buddhist texts as documentary
evidence, their contributions are speculative at best.
21 The stories of the JA are often repeated more than once, or associated
simultaneously with different verses or stories of the present. one example is the story of
the greedy crow and pigeon, recounted in JA 42, 274, 375 and 395 with different verses. in
JA 275 only verses are given, with a reference back to the preceding jātaka for the story.
22 Rhys-Davids, Buddhist India, p. 208.
23 T.W. Rhys-Davids, Buddhist Birth Stories (london, 1880), p. lxxix.

10 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
qualities to distinguish them from other indian or indeed world narratives, and
suggests that they contain no distinctively Buddhist morals or doctrines.24
That this is a misleading position can be demonstrated by a comparison with
other settings. For example, in morgan’s study of proverbs, fables, gnomai and
exempla in the early Roman empire, she points out that such genres require
some sort of moral authority, hence, for example, the gradual compilation of
legends surrounding the person of aesop. She argues that popular genres are in
many ways definitive of a society:
it is in the nature of ethics across societies to have much in common … at
the same time, no feature more sharply distinguishes societies than the moral
authorities they invoke … Roman ethics can be felt by Romans to be profoundly
Roman, not because they are unique, but because they appeal to Roman gods,
heroes and stories.25
With this in mind we might say that the same story told by the Buddha and
by aesop is not, in fact, the same story. Thus, even if jātakas have parallels in
other collections, their setting as jātakas must be examined, and their influence
as a genre remains significant. Rather than dismiss the stories as common tales,
we can ask how and why they were transformed into jātakas and established as
Buddhist stories.
it is impossible to treat jātakas as non-Buddhist or even pre-Buddhist for the
simple reason that these stories are now – and have been for over 2,000 years – a
popular part of Buddhism. The confusion here is between the often non-Buddhist
origins of the stories and their present Buddhist textual and societal context. Real
danger lies in assuming that no change has occurred during the appropriation
24 The general expectation seems to be that folklore is moralistic, but not in any
sense that is specific to the community that preserves the tale. For example in Jones’
study of the values of the JA in comparison with the Pāli canon, he provides a very
thorough survey of the JA and makes a number of interesting observations about themes
such as kamma and rebirth, non-harm, friendship and women. However, he states that:
‘We should not expect the Jātaka stories to attempt any systematic or comprehensive
ethical teaching, though we should expect them to be, like folk tales in general, moralistic
in tone.’ John G. Jones, Tales and Teachings of the Buddha: The Jataka Stories in Relation to
the Pāli Canon (london, 1979) p. 57. He then goes on to highlight jātakas in which the
Bodhisatta harms, or even kills, other beings, and behaves less than chivalrously with
regard to women, leaving us wondering what morals he expects the jātakas, as folktales,
to contain. in any case Jones is doubtful about the Buddhist nature of jātakas, and in
particular views jātakas as fundamentally at odds with Buddhism because they talk
of a person progressing through many lives (contradicting the doctrine that there is
nothing that is an attā – a self). He is also keen to point out the historical impossibilities
of the stories, such as the fact that the Bodhisatta is said to be born 395 times while
Brahmadatta was ruling in Benares.
25 Teresa morgan, Popular Morality in the Early Roman Empire (Cambridge, 2007), p. 231.

What is a Jātaka? 11
of a story, whereas in fact even if the story itself remains untouched, making a
story into a jātaka instantly provides a Buddhist framework. That some stories
(and indeed verses) may predate Buddhism does not mean that early Buddhists
used jātakas in anything other than a wholeheartedly Buddhist context, or that
the JA as we have it now is in any way non-Buddhist. Thus jātakas deserve to be
studied seriously for their Buddhist elements, not merely as folktales or sources
of information about ancient india.
aRe JĀTaKaS SERIOUSLy BUDDHiST?
even if we accept that the presence of a story in the JA and the presence of the
Buddha and Bodhisatta in the story make a jātaka Buddhist, does that necessarily
mean that jātakas contain anything seriously Buddhist? in other words, are jātakas
simply moral stories purely for the instruction of the laity? Do they contain
anything of interest in relation to the development of Buddhist philosophy or
practice?
it is easy to discredit the idea that jātakas as stories purely for the laity. as
Gombrich notes, village culture in Theravāda countries is often dominated by
the monastery temple, which provides depictions of jātakas on the walls, and
sermons that can border on storytelling or even drama. The subjects for both the
art and the sermons are chosen by the monks, so one assumes that the appeal
of jātaka stories must be just as strong in monastic education as amongst the
laity.26 There is evidence to suggest this was also the case in the past: Schopen,
for example, notes that a large proportion of the donors at stūpa sites (where
jātaka illustrations are popular) were monks and nuns.27 in any case, monastic
education, as many anthropologists have observed, is not always very different
to the education of a layman. it is thus clearly a mistake to assume that jātaka
stories will have no use in a monastic context, and although it is surely less
objectionable to refer to ‘popular’ rather than ‘lay’ Buddhism in relation to this
issue, as meiland argues,28 this might still misleadingly imply a lack of doctrinal
or philosophical content.
There is further evidence within the texts and stories themselves that jātakas
have relevance to the monastic community. For a start, each story in the JA is said
to be told by the Buddha, who is head of the monastic order, and the audience
inside the frame story is monastic in more than 80 per cent of cases. in addition,
26 margaret Cone and Richard F. Gombrich, The Perfect Generosity of Prince Vessantara
(oxford, 1977), p. xxvi.
27 Gregory Schopen, Bones, Stones, and Buddhist Monks: Collected Papers on the
Archaeology, Epigraphy, and Texts of Monastic Buddhism in India (Honolulu, 1997), chapters 3,
5, 9 and 11.
28 Justin meiland, Buddhist Values in the Pāli Jātakas, with Particular Reference to the
Theme of Renunciation (oxford University D.Phil. Thesis, 2004), p. 15.

12 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
the issues covered are frequently of monastic relevance, including discussions
of renunciation, how to resist the temptation of returning to one’s wife, and
how to ensure peace and harmony in the saṅgha. There is plenty of evidence to
suggest that jātakas appeal(ed) to the laity too, not least the crowds that gather
around for a dramatic performance in present-day Southeast asia. it is often
noted that many of the stories focus upon giving, which is an ideal required
of the laity if the monastic community is to survive, and therefore an obvious
theme for sermons. However, as ohnuma has recently shown in her study of gift-
of-the-body jātakas in the indian Buddhist tradition, the rationale behind the
representation of giving in such stories is clearly more complex than the simple
ethical teaching that generosity is good.29
it is clear that jātakas in fact contain teachings aimed at many audiences that
work on many different levels, and that defining jātakas as simple moral stories
for the edification of the laity is misrepresentative. Stories do, of course, provide
us with material quite different to explicitly philosophical and doctrinal texts,
but the evidence they provide is no less important for a full understanding of
Buddhism. as Steven Collins notes:
it is, surely, no more than common sense to recognise that people react to
problems, ideas and events by telling stories about them, or by understanding
them in terms of already-known stories, as well as – and sometimes at the
same time as – by thinking logically or scientifically about them; and that what
counts as a good story is not the same as what counts as a good argument, and
vice versa.30
Collins’ division between ‘narrative’ and ‘systematic’ thought, set out in his
study of felicitous states in Buddhism,31 provides a neat framework for studying
jātaka stories as narratives, but without dismissing them as ‘just stories’. i will be
returning to his arguments in Chapter 6.
That jātaka stories do indeed contain something very seriously Buddhist
is declared by many of the texts themselves. in the biographical preface to
the JA as well as in later jātaka collections we are told that jātakas illustrate the
Bodhisatta’s long path to buddhahood, in which he acquired the 10 perfections
or pāramitās. one cannot get more seriously Buddhist than an illustration of the
path to buddhahood. However, this rationale is not reflected in the core of the JA:
in the majority of the stories it is difficult to tell which perfection might be being
acquired, and indeed the Bodhisatta’s behaviour is often far from perfect. many of
the stories contain no explicitly Buddhist teaching. in addition, the stories are not
ordered chronologically, as one might expect them to be if they illustrate a path.
29 ohnuma, Head, Eyes, Flesh, and Blood.
30 Steven Collins, Nirvana and Other Buddhist Felicities: Utopias of the Pali Imaginaire
(Cambridge, 1998), p. 121.
31 He defines these terms in Collins, Nirvana, p. 121ff.

What is a Jātaka? 13
This definition of jātakas – that they illustrate the gradual perfection of the
Bodhisatta – will form the focus of my study. as is clear already, this is only one
aspect of jātakas, and only one possible way of defining them. Such a definition
has wide implications for Buddhist history, literature and doctrine, since if the
jātakas illustrate the Bodhisatta’s acquisition of the perfections required for
buddhahood, it stands to reason that they are biographical, and time-specific.
History and chronology become important, and the Bodhisatta is linked to other
buddhas and bodhisattas, including – potentially – an aspiring bodhisatta in the
audience. Such preoccupations, however, at times sit uneasily with the stories’
origins, allowing us to see the development of these ideas over time.
a DiVeRSe aND CHaNGiNG GeNRe
among all the definitions of what a jātaka is, there is little discussion of the
possible changes in the genre, or the possible variety within it. The dangers of
this situation can be demonstrated by a jātaka story. in the Kiṃsukopama-jātaka
( JA 248), four princes ask their charioteer to show them the Kiṃsuka (‘What’s-
it’) tree. He takes each of them to the tree in turn:
He seated the eldest son in the chariot and took him to the forest. Saying ‘This
is the Kiṃsuka’ he showed him the tree at the time there was just a trunk. The
next he showed at the time it was showing young foliage, the next at the time
of flowering, and the next at the time of fruiting. later the four brothers were
sitting together and raised the question, ‘What sort of tree is the Kiṃsuka?’
one said ‘it is like a burnt pillar,’ the second ‘it is like a Banyan tree,’ the third
‘it is like a piece of flesh’ and the fourth ‘it is like an acacia tree.’32
Perplexed at each other’s answers they go to their father, the King of Varanasi
(who is the Bodhisatta), to settle the matter. He speaks the first stanza of the
jātaka:
all have seen the Kiṃsuka; why now do you doubt?
Nobody asked the charioteer about all the conditions!33
We see in the story of the present ( paccuppannavatthu) that this jātaka is told by
the Buddha to illustrate that different meditation methods suit different people,
but that they all lead to the same nibbāna. it reads:
‘all have seen the Kiṃsuka…’ This story was told by the Teacher while he
was dwelling in Jetavana, about the simile of the Kiṃsuka tree. Four monks
32 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 265.
33 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 266.

14 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
approached the Tathāgata and requested meditation subjects. The Teacher
gave them each a meditation subject and having taken them they each went to
their day-time and night-time abodes. one of them attained arahatship after
contemplating the six sense-bases, another the five aggregates, another the
four great elements, and the other the eighteen bases. They told the Teacher
about the characteristics of each of their attainments. Then a thought occurred
to one monk and he asked the Teacher, ‘Nibbāna is of one kind for all these
meditation subjects. How can arahatship be attained by all these methods?’
The Teacher replied, ‘monks, isn’t this variation like that of the brothers
who saw the Kiṃsuka tree?’ ‘Bhante, explain the meaning to us,’ the monks
entreated, and so he told a story of the past.34
Keeping to the JA’s tradition of having a story for every purpose, and sometimes
several paccuppannavatthus for one jātaka (and vice versa), it is possible to re-
write the paccuppannavatthu for this story, in order that it instead illustrates the
state of scholarship on the jātakas:35
‘all have seen the Kiṃsuka…’ This story was told by the Teacher while he was
dwelling in Jetavana, about the simile of the Kiṃsuka tree. Four scholars were
discussing the nature of the jātaka stories. one had seen jātakas with parallels in
other story collections outside Buddhism, and saw them as folktales. another
noticed the many incidental details and said they were indian narratives of
predominantly historical value. a third had been listening to sermons and
marvelled at the way the stories were used to inculcate Buddhist ethics in
the laity. The fourth had been reading the Jātaka-nidānakathā and Cariyāpiṭaka
and had learnt from these that jātakas are biographical and illustrate the
acquisition of the perfections by the Bodhisatta. They each explained their
positions to the Teacher. The Teacher said, ‘Scholars, isn’t this variation like
that of the brothers who saw the Kiṃsuka tree?’
Though perhaps a little facetious, this exercise clearly demonstrates that
there are several possible definitions of jātakas precisely because of the variety
inherent in the genre.
The Kiṃsuka tree – Butea Monosperma or Butea Frondosa – is appreciated for
the magnificent changes in its appearance during the year. after appearing
totally barren for up to two months its gnarled branches burst forth into flesh-
coloured flowers, which vanish to be replaced with large leaves. The leaves have
a ritual function for Hindus, but also serve as plates, tobacco wrappers and
packing materials. The gum and seeds both have medicinal uses, the shoots and
roots are used to make rope, the flowers provide orange dye, and the wood is
34 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 265.
35 my thanks go to Sarah Shaw for pointing out this very helpful parallel.

What is a Jātaka? 15
used to make sacrificial utensils as well as for building.36 Thus the diversity of
the Kiṃsuka tree, in both appearance and function, is celebrated and admired;
not so with the jātakas. The different interpretations of jātakas are often seen as
at best irrelevant to one another, and at worst contradictory or incompatible:
how can jātakas be both non-Buddhist folktales and Buddhist moral stories, for
the common man and the aspiring bodhisatta, simple moral tales about kamma
that nonetheless demonstrate the gradual path to buddhahood? The key to
understanding and appreciating this diversity is that jātakas – like the Kiṃsuka
tree – are not unchanging. individual stories developed and changed over time,
as did the very idea of what a jātaka is and is for, and although both the stories
and their rationale were eventually cemented together in textual form, they
remain diverse in the practices and contexts of living Buddhism.
The diversity of the JA can be celebrated as it allows interaction with a range
of ideas and people. even the apparently haphazard nature of the collection can
be seen as an advantage. That the stories are often repeated elsewhere in the
text, sometimes with alternative endings, gives the text a sense of being flexible
and open ended. There is a similar case in relation to the verses, for the same
verses are sometimes used in several stories, or the same story is told in relation
to different verses. Several verses also have parallels in other texts both within
and outside Buddhism, and so the recitation of a verse or story immediately
brings to mind a variety of contexts and applications.37 The variety – and even
the untidiness – within the jātaka collections can therefore be celebrated in the
same way as the variety of the Kiṃsuka tree.
The Kiṃsukopama-jātaka reminds one of the story related in chapter six of
the Udāna, where some blind men are each shown part of an elephant and then
asked by the king to describe what an elephant is like:
Those blind people who had been shown the head of the elephant replied, ‘an
elephant, your majesty, is just like a water jar.’ Those blind people who had
been shown the ear of the elephant replied, ‘an elephant, your majesty, is just
like a winnowing basket.’ Those blind people who had been shown the tusk of
the elephant replied, ‘an elephant, your majesty, is just like a ploughshare.’
Those blind people who had been shown the trunk replied, ‘an elephant, your
majesty, is just like a plough pole.’ Those blind people who had been shown the
body replied, ‘an elephant, your majesty, is just like a storeroom.’ Those blind
people who had been shown the foot replied, ‘an elephant, your majesty, is just
like a post.’ Those blind people who had been shown the hindquarters replied,
‘an elephant, your majesty, is just like a mortar.’ Those blind people who had
36 S.P. ambasta (ed.), The Useful Plants of India (New Delhi, 1986), pp. 91–2; Dietrich
Brandis, Indian Trees (london, 1906), p. 230; Shakti m. Gupta, Plant Myths and Traditions in
India (2nd edn, New Delhi, 1991), pp. 23–5.
37 For an interesting discussion of this see Kum Kum Roy, ‘Justice in the Jatakas’,
Social Scientist, 24/4/6 (1996): 23–40.

16 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
been shown the tail replied, ‘an elephant, your majesty, is just like a pestle.’
Those blind people who had been shown the tuft at the end of the tail replied,
‘an elephant, your majesty, is just like a broom.’38
in subtle contrast to the jātaka, this story relies upon the diversity in parts of
a whole, rather than changes over time. This too can be applied to the jātakas,
where not only is there great change over time, but also great variety in form
and function within a given time (or text). This is particularly true of the JA,
which is often assumed to be a united whole (on account of its united frame)
when in fact there is great variety within it.
These two stories complement one another and remind us that when we look
at the jātaka genre, we must be aware of the diversity both within individual
texts and across time. in a sense, the story of blind men and the elephant forms
the basis for Chapter 2, where i investigate the diversity found within the JA. The
Kiṃsuka-jātaka informs Chapters 3 and 4, which trace developments in the JA and
the jātaka genre more broadly. These chapters then feed in to a discussion of wider
developments in Theravāda doctrine and practice. Rather than see the mutative
and diverse nature of the jātakas as a problem, i prefer to use observations of the
changes and variety to better understand the development of early Buddhism.
The image of the tree provides us with another useful parallel, namely the
different ways in which it might be viewed. Not only can we see it at different
times, or see only parts of it, we might also have different ways of looking.
Some people (descriptive botanists) might be particularly interested in the
structural make-up of the tree, or want to study the roots or ascertain its age;
others (aesthetes) appreciate its beauty and complexity for artistic reasons; and
yet others (applied botanists) look at the uses made of it. To appreciate a tree
in its entirety requires several approaches; similarly, when studying jātakas it
is necessary to use a variety of methodologies in order to build up a complete
picture.
in the study of jātakas, it is textualists who have dominated, looking at the
roots in non-Buddhist folklore, counting the rings to ascertain the age, and
examining the ways in which the different parts fit together. They have shown
limited interest in the purposes or uses of jātakas. Now that the basic forms and
sources of jātaka texts have been established, it is necessary to build on this
work by moving towards more thematic, historical and doctrinal studies that
appreciate the diversity of the genre. Such studies rely upon moving away from
purely textual studies, into such arenas as art history and anthropology.
i am of course not alone in my insistence that we move away from exclusively
textual studies. as Schopen has famously pointed out, in the study of indian
Buddhism texts have been unduly prioritised over the inscriptional and artistic
evidence preserved in asia, despite the latter being in some cases older and
38 John D. ireland (trans.), The Udāna: Inspired Utterances of the Buddha (Kandy, 1990),
pp. 93–4 (section 6.4).

What is a Jātaka? 17
more reliable than the texts. There has also been a prioritisation of doctrinal and
philosophical texts over and above those with a higher ritual or mythological
content, and ‘canonical’ texts over and above popular works. Such texts have
been taken as key to understanding the history of Buddhism, and have been
viewed as descriptive documents, even though they are often clearly normative.
Such attitudes are now often termed ‘Protestant Buddhism’, since, as Schopen
argues, the idea that ‘true’ or ‘correct’ religion lies in doctrinal texts can be
traced to the influence of sixteenth-century Protestant polemics on Western
intellectual history, and consequently on the attitudes of Western scholars of
Buddhism.39
as well as prompting studies of Buddhism through its art, archaeology and
epigraphy (the latter forming the focus for his own work), Schopen’s critique has
encouraged new approaches to texts that attempt to counter the bias of earlier
scholarship. These include Blackburn’s notion of the ‘practical canon’, which she
contrasts to the ‘formal canon’ so often the focus of scholars.40 She advocates an
examination of what texts were used by whom in specific circumstances, and
argues that such an approach can help scholars to better understand Buddhist
communities within historical contexts.41 Blackburn also insists that Buddhist
texts should not be viewed as a passive witness to what the Buddha taught,
but rather as part of an active process of the self-representation of a particular
community in a particular historical context. as Hegarty puts it in relation to
the Mahābhārata, a text can be ‘“work” in the sense of both noun and verb, that is
to say it is both “a literary work” and “work” in the sense of the way in which the
text demands creative response to its discourse from its audience’.42 Hegarty’s
description certainly applies to the JA, which has had a dynamic relationship
with other texts and ideas about buddhahood within certain communities,
making it possible (or even necessary) to talk of the text as if it were a subject as
39 See in particular his 1991 article on ‘archaeology and Protestant Presuppositions
in the Study of indian Buddhism’, reprinted as chapter 1 in Schopen, Bones, Stones, and
Buddhist Monks.
40 Blackburn defines the practical canon as ‘the units of text actually employed in
the practices of collecting manuscripts, copying them, reading them, commenting on
them, listening to them, and preaching sermons based upon them that are understood
by their users as part of a tipiṭaka-based tradition’. anne m. Blackburn, ‘looking for
the Vinaya: monastic Discipline in the Practical Canons of the Theravāda’, Journal of the
International Association of Buddhist Studies, 22/2 (1999): 281–309, p. 284. This she contrasts
with the more general definition by Collins (Nirvana, p. 78) as ‘works in actual use at any
given places and times’.
41 Blackburn, ‘looking for the Vinaya’, especially pp. 284–5.
42 James m. Hegarty, A Fire of Tongues: Narrative Patterning in the Sanskrit Mahābhārata
(manchester University Ph.D. Thesis, 2004), p. 20.

18 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
well as an object.43 alternatively we might return to the comparison with a tree
and say that, in a sense, the JA – and indeed the jātaka genre more generally – is
a living thing.
if the jātaka genre is a living creative process, what ‘work’ might jātaka
illustrations be doing? it is easiest to view them simply as illustrations of a
narrative, retelling a story through images. However, recent studies of the uses
of jātaka images, as well as of the uses of jātaka stories in the consecration of
Buddha images, suggest that image and story are linked in a more complex
way than merely as source and illustration.44 Understanding the relationship
between jātaka texts and developing ideas about the Buddha and buddhahood
sheds further light on the role of jātaka images. art-historical analyses of jātakas
in turn may hold the key to an understanding of the purposes and uses of jātakas
in general.
if we are looking for uses of jātakas, we might reasonably turn to ethnographic
work for elucidation, and piecing together brief mentions of the genre in various
works it becomes clear that jātakas have many uses, not all of which depend
upon their narrative content. as well as being used in sermons and schoolbooks,
they are also recited in legal disputes, ritual contexts and during significant
life events. The diversity of jātakas can be seen by their relevance to each of
Spiro’s (contested) divisions: ‘nibbānic’ (which he understands to be a normative
soteriological system, and that most frequently understood by Westerners
to be Buddhism), ‘kammatic’ (which he understands to be a non-normative
soteriological system, which relies upon either postponing the quest for nibbāna
whilst focusing on attaining a better rebirth, or reclassifying nibbāna as a
heavenly state and aiming for it through meritorious actions), and ‘apotropaic’
(which he understands to be a non-soteriological framework concerned with
matters in this life, such as health and the weather).45 The portrayal in jātakas of
personal soteriological quests, karmic consequences and apotropaic methods,
demonstrates that trying to link jātakas to a single Buddhist attitude or situation
is impossible.
43 We might, as Hegarty does, quote lévi-Strauss in support of proceeding ‘as if the
thinking process were taking place in the myths, in their reflection upon themselves
and their interrelation’ (from The Raw and the Cooked, quoted in Hegarty, A Fire of Tongues,
p. 23). Viewing a text in this way is perhaps particularly useful when it has no single
author or single date of composition, making talk of authorial intent next to impossible.
44 i am thinking especially of the work of two authors, which i will discuss at length
in Chapter 7: Robert l. Brown, ‘Narrative as icon: The Jātaka Stories in ancient indian
and Southeast asian architecture’ in Juliane Schober (ed.), Sacred Biography in the Buddhist
Traditions of South and Southeast Asia (Honolulu, 1997), pp. 64–109; Donald K. Swearer,
Becoming the Buddha: The Ritual of Image Consecration in Thailand (Princeton, 2004).
45 melford e. Spiro, Buddhism and Society: A Great Tradition and its Burmese Vicissitudes
(2nd edn, Berkeley, 1980).

What is a Jātaka? 19
The aspect of jātakas that becomes most clear in ethnographic and art-
historical studies is their extreme popularity in Sri lanka and Southeast asia
through to the present day. as Carrithers notes in relation to Sri lanka: ‘The
Jātakas are at once older than the Pali canon, and the basic substance of sermons
in modern Ceylon; they are preserved in Pali, but form the basic matter of most of
Sinhalese literature; and they have been woven into the daily life of the Sinhalese,
through sermons, poetry, art and music, for more than two thousand years.’46
Bode speaks of ‘the immortal Jātaka book’ and states that ‘[t]o understand the
literature, “serious” or popular, of Burma we cannot know the Pali Jātaka too
well’.47 Clearly the popularity of jātakas in these countries is strongly linked to
jātaka texts. Therefore, although i will be returning to ethnographic sources in
Chapter 7, i must begin my exploration of the ideological relationship between
the Buddha and his jātaka stories by becoming better acquainted with the
Pāli jātaka book, the JA. once i have established the contribution of that text
to emerging understandings of the generic conventions of jātaka stories i will
move on to an examination of the genre’s impact on Theravāda doctrine and
practice.
46 michael Carrithers, The Forest Monks of Sri Lanka (Delhi, 1983), p. 90.
47 mabel Haynes Bode, The Pali Literature of Burma (london, 1909), p. 3 and p. 82.

This page has been left blank intentionally

Chapter 2
The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories
in the dedicatory verses at the very beginning of the Jātakatthavaṇṇanā ( JA) we
hear that the jātakas ‘illuminate the unimaginable magnificence of the deeds of
a Great man’ who ‘brought to fruition over a long time the endless conditions
for bodhi’.1 The view that jātakas are about the acquisition of the many qualities
required for buddhahood over a long series of lives is shared by many scholars
and Buddhists alike. Under the influence of such a definition we might expect
to see the Bodhisatta performing exclusively positive deeds in his jātaka stories.
However, even a cursory glance at the contents of the JA reveals that the
Bodhisatta is a more complex character than this. When we consider that the
presence of the Bodhisatta is an essential feature of jātakas, it is surprising how
little attention has been paid to his character. The most obvious place to start in
my examination of the biographical dimensions of the jātaka genre is therefore
the characterisation of the Bodhisatta within his jātaka stories.
Great variety lies in the roles the Bodhisatta plays in jātaka stories. in many
cases he behaves as a good Buddhist should, demonstrating virtues and acquiring
the perfections ( pāramitās). However, his character is not restricted by Buddhist
concerns, and sometimes his ‘good’ behaviour is more a demonstration of worldly
wisdom, even at the expense of other (Buddhist or ‘moral’) qualities. in several
stories he plays little or no part in the action, but merely observes or pronounces
the moral. He may also assault people, kill, steal, commit sexual impropriety,
gamble and get drunk. it is said in the prose of the Hārita-jātaka ( JA 431, discussed
below) that the Bodhisatta can break all the other precepts but never lie, yet we
even find examples of the Bodhisatta deceiving people. Thus the Bodhisatta’s
behaviour is not always in accordance with the ‘endless conditions for bodhi’
that the compiler leads us to expect. The purpose of this chapter is to examine
these various roles, and present some possible explanations for the great variety
and apparent widespread lack of biographical concern. most examples will be
drawn from the JA, since it is this text that presents itself as a biography, and
where possible quotations will be given from the verses of this text, since these
are considered to be the canonical core. Supplementary examples from other
texts will be given where they better illustrate the variety of characters played
by the Bodhisatta in his jātaka stories.
1 Verses 10 and 5, respectively. For a full translation see the opening to Chapter 3.

22 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
GooD
i shall begin my investigation with those stories in which the Bodhisatta behaves
in a ‘good’ way. in such stories the Bodhisatta is variously a morally upright or
particularly sagacious hero, a compassionate saviour of others, a teacher, or a
wise observer. in some, the text points out the particular pāramitās (perfections)
he is acquiring, and draws our attention thus to the way in which jātakas form a
prelude to the final life as Gotama Bodhisatta and Gotama Buddha.
Hero or Saviour?
Scholars and practitioners have long debated the appropriate attitude that
Buddhists should have towards the Buddha, and indeed other buddhas and
bodhisatt(v)as. is the Buddha an ideal that should be emulated or rather
a compassionate figure to be propitiated? is the Buddhist path the sole
responsibility of each individual, or might we reasonably expect a little
assistance? Such debates are most pertinent in mahāyāna Buddhism, but the
division is alive also in Theravāda societies. in relation to the Buddha, whether
he is a focus for emulation or propitiation is a central issue in discussions of
image and relic worship. To investigate attitudes towards the Bodhisatta, we
might turn to jātakas, where he is portrayed sometimes as hero and sometimes
as saviour.
in several jātakas the Bodhisatta is identified with a great hero of old, and
perhaps the most striking of these is the Dasaratha-jātaka ( JA 461) where he is
identified as Rāma and enacts his own version of an episode from the Rāmāyaṇa.
Several other jātakas portray the Bodhisatta as the head of a group, either of
merchants, animals or birds, leading them to safety. in his saviour role, the
most obvious examples are those jātakas where he is the god Sakka, who very
often in Buddhist tales provides support for Buddhism and righteous people.
Sakka is a frequent character in the JA, where on more than 20 occasions he is
identified with the Bodhisatta.2 in many of these stories Sakka teaches a moral
lesson through his special divine powers, but in others he offers assistance: in
the Bhadraghaṭa-jātaka ( JA 291) he gives a wishing-cup to a poor man who was his
son in a previous birth; in the Kaccāni-jātaka ( JA 417) he saves a mother who has
been cast out by her son.
2 Sakka’s other main role is to test the Bodhisatta’s resolve, for example he often
takes on a disguise and asks the Bodhisatta for some extremely difficult gift, either in
order to aid the Bodhisatta’s practice of the perfections, or as a way of preserving his
own position which might be challenged by a being as great as the Bodhisatta. For a good
discussion of these ambiguities see Justin meiland, Buddhist Values in the Pāli Jātakas, with
Particular Reference to the Theme of Renunciation (oxford University D.Phil. Thesis, 2004),
chapter 2, and Reiko ohnuma, Head, Eyes, Flesh, and Blood: Giving Away the Body in Indian
Buddhist Literature (New York, 2007), p. 64ff.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 23
The characters of hero and saviour are perhaps best illustrated by the
Valāhassa-jataka ( JA 196) and the Telapatta-jātaka ( JA 96), which are combined in
some Sanskrit texts to form one story. The Valāhassa-jātaka tells of how a merchant
and his caravan are shipwrecked on an island and seduced by the yakkhinīs (man-
eating demonesses) who live there. The caravan leader becomes suspicious and
makes for the shore with half of his merchants. on the shore, as luck would
have it, there happens to be a magical horse, who carries the merchants home.
Those who foolishly stay behind are eaten.3 The Telapatta-jātaka tells of a prince
who makes his way to Takkasilā across a wilderness populated by yakkhinīs. His
five companions each fall by the wayside, tempted by each of the five senses,
and the prince is tracked by a yakkhinī who pretends to be his wife. When she
has no luck with him, she seduces the king, and summons her friends to feast
on the royal family. The prince expels the yakkhinīs and is made king. in the
Mūlasarvāstivāda-vinaya and the Divyāvadāna these two jātakas are combined to
form a single narrative. in the Valāhassa-jātaka the Bodhisatta is the saviour horse;
in the Telapatta-jātaka he is the prince who resists the charms of the yakkhinīs.
Consequently the combined story has two options for the identification of the
Bodhisatta: heroic merchant or saviour horse. He is, as it happens, identified
as the former.4 Thus the same cycle of stories, which all illustrate the danger
of lust, has a flexible attitude towards its identifications of the Bodhisatta. is
the Buddha-to-be a sagacious heroic leader (to be emulated) or a compassionate
saviour (to be propitiated)? The answer we find in the jātakas is both.
Teaching and Example
in many jātaka stories the Bodhisatta has no opportunity to demonstrate his
heroism or compassion, but rather shows his good judgement. often these jātakas
show the Bodhisatta teaching through wise observation or by his own actions.
an example of the latter is the Dummedha-jātaka ( JA 50), in which the Bodhisatta
is a prince who makes a big show of worshipping a particular tree. When he
becomes king he says he must make an offering to the tree-deity (rukkhadevatā)
3 The Valāhassa-jātaka is a poorly abbreviated form of another telling, and thus
is barely coherent in its narrative. an example of how the story may have been told
when the verses were first composed can be found in the story of 500 merchants in the
Mahāvastu, where the verses are also paralleled. a full discussion of the different versions
can be found in Naomi appleton, Seduced by Saṃsāra, Saved by a Flying Horse: A Study of the
Aśvarāja and Siṃhala Stories (Cardiff University m.Phil. Thesis, 2004) and a shorter analysis
in Naomi appleton, ‘The Story of the Horse-King and the merchant Siṃhala in Buddhist
Texts’, Buddhist Studies Review, 23/2 (2006): 187–201.
4 in mahāyāna versions of the story the horse becomes identified with
avalokiteśvara and the heroism of the Bodhisatta is played down, demonstrating another
level of flexibility. For a full discussion see appleton, Seduced by Samsāra or ‘The Story of
the Horse-King’.

24 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
who brought him such good fortune, and that this offering must be 1,000 people
who indulge in animal sacrifice. Suddenly nobody in his kingdom will perform
animal sacrifice. Such jātakas are not always so Buddhist in emphasis, but
rather show the Bodhisatta being ‘good’ in a mundane sense, by being worldly
wise or quick-witted. in the Gagga-jātaka ( JA 155) a brahmin called Gagga and
his son, the Bodhisatta, have to stay in a hut haunted by a yakkha who is able
to devour anyone who doesn’t say ‘long life to you’ when someone sneezes.
Hoping to make a meal of the two travellers he makes the brahmin sneeze, but
the Bodhisatta realises the situation and dutifully says ‘long life’, prompting
his father to reply, thereby guaranteeing their immunity. He then reforms the
yakkha, to everyone’s amazement. This story is in no way inherently Buddhist,
but nor does it contradict Buddhist ideals.
an example that shows the Bodhisatta as a teacher or wise commentator
may be seen in the Matakabhatta-jātaka ( JA 18), which tells of a goat that laughs
then weeps as he is being prepared as a sacrifice. Being asked why, he explains
that he is happy that this is finally his last of 500 births where his head must be
chopped off as punishment for sacrificing animals. However, he is upset at the
thought that the brahmin who is about to slaughter him will have to suffer the
same fate. The brahmin sets the goat free and does his best to protect him, but
his head is cut off by a falling rock which has been struck by a thunderbolt. The
Bodhisatta is a tree-deity who explains:
if beings knew this was the suffering of the origin of birth,
no living being would kill a living being, for the murderer laments.5
in these stories, as well as the many others where the Bodhisatta’s role is to give
advice, pass judgement or pronounce the moral, it is the Bodhisatta’s wisdom and
insight, rather than his actions, that matter, and the audience can be instructed
by the actions of other protagonists.
often the Bodhisatta’s role is merely to give good advice, for example in the
Cullakaseṭṭhi-jātaka ( JA 4), which tells of how the Bodhisatta finds a dead mouse,
and interprets the omens, saying a sensible man should pick up the mouse and
start a business and keep a wife. a young man overhears, and through a long and
convoluted series of actions he gains a fortune. He goes to thank the Bodhisatta
and marries his daughter, completing his rise to fortune and happiness. The
verse summarises that:
With the smallest capital, a wise and discerning man,
advances himself – just like blowing on a small fire.6
5 Trans. from V. Fausbøll (ed.), The Jātaka together with its commentary being tales of the
anterior births of Gotama Buddha (6 vols, london, 1877–1896), vol. 1, p. 168.
6 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 1, p. 122.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 25
Perhaps a little surprisingly, the wise man in this case is not the Bodhisatta, who
has only kick-started the narrative with some good advice. Sometimes even
his advice is ignored, for example in the Ruhaka-jātaka ( JA 191) the Bodhisatta
is Brahmadatta (King of Varanasi) and has a chaplain called Ruhaka, who is
estranged from his wife. The king calls him to the palace to try to reconcile
them, saying:
Ruhaka! a broken bowstring can be connected again.
let this old wife be reconciled. Don’t let anger overcome you.
Ruhaka replies:
as long as there is hemp and craftsmen,
i will make a new bowstring. enough of the old wife!7
and he finds himself a new wife.
The Perfections
at the other end of the spectrum to those stories in which the Bodhisatta gives
advice to his inattentive companions in saṃsāra, are those in which he takes
centre stage as an aspiring buddha. Such stories show how the Bodhisatta acquired
the skills and virtues required for buddhahood, in particular the 10 perfections
( pāramīs or pāramitās): giving (dāna), morality (sīla), renunciation (nekkhamma),
wisdom ( paññā), energy (viriya), forbearance (khanti), truth (sacca), determination
(adhiṭṭhāna), loving kindness (mettā) and equanimity (upekkhā). The most famous
of these stories, both within and outside asia, is the Vessantara-jātaka ( JA 547),
which according to Theravāda tradition relates Gotama’s last-but-one birth.
in it, the Bodhisatta gives away his wife and children in the perfection of his
generosity. other jātakas can be found that take the other perfections to similar
extremes, such as the Khantivādi-jātaka ( JA 313), which tells of the teacher of
forbearance who allows his hands and feet and ears and nose to be lopped off by
an angry king without lifting a finger in his own defence. Nekkhamma is perhaps
the quality most frequently addressed, with many tales extolling the benefits of
renunciation, although it – like the other perfections – is rarely mentioned by
name in the jātakas themselves. according to later traditions, the final 10 jātakas
of the JA illustrate the 10 perfections being acquired in their highest form by the
Bodhisatta in his final 10 births.
7 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, pp. 114–15. There is a pun here on the word
jiyā which can mean bowstring or wife. Where Fausbøll has marūdvāsu, l.S. Cousins has
suggested to me the alternative of maruvāsu; maruvā (Skt mūrvā) is a plant in the hemp
family, which may have been used to make bowstrings.

26 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
although stories about the pāramitās are influential, the jātakas in the JA tend
not to address them explicitly. The perfections are, in fact, a late-canonical and
commentarial preoccupation, as can be seen by the strong emphasis on them
in the JA’s dedicatory verses, the Jātaka-nidānakathā, and the Cariyāpiṭaka, all of
which will be discussed in the next chapter. To acknowledge that the perfections
are a late addition to the jātakas of the JA is not to deny the important role
that they play in the history of the genre. However, since i will be discussing
the pāramitās in greater detail in later chapters, we need not consider them at
length here.
Bad
Despite the pervasive idea that jātakas demonstrate the perfections, the Bodhisatta
sometimes acts badly, both within a Buddhist framework and according to what
we might consider universal standards of morality. We may now move on to an
examination of the different types of such jātakas: first where the behaviour is
accepted as bad and resolved within the jātaka, either in the story of the past
itself, or in the frame story through a karmic consequence; and second where
the immoral behaviour is apparently condoned within the narrative.
Villainy and Kamma
let me begin with a mild example: in the Litta-jātaka ( JA 91) the Bodhisatta
poisons a gambling dice in order to catch a cheat. This is a neat illustration of the
importance of two aspects of morality, namely intention and result; according to
Buddhist expositions, it is the former (cetanā) that ultimately defines an action
as good or bad, skilful or unskilful. in this case we can assume that the Bodhisatta
had good intentions in the long term (to prevent the cheat from accruing further
bad kamma), but in the short term the intention was to cause physical suffering,
and the result of the poison was that the cheat ‘fainted, his eyes rolled around,
and doubling over [in pain] he fell’.8 after proving his point the Bodhisatta did
cure the man, as well as tell him not to cheat again, but there is no mention of
whether the cheat reformed or not. if only we knew that he had been cured of
his bad behaviour and therefore prevented from a bad rebirth we could be happy
that at least the longer-term results, as well as the intention, were good.
a slightly more problematic example is the Lomahaṃsa-jātaka ( JA 94), in
which the Bodhisatta lives a life as a naked ascetic, which in Buddhist terms is
a false path stemming from ‘wrong view’, and one of the 10 paths of unskilful
action enumerated in many Buddhist commentarial texts. He is not allowed to
take this path to its natural karmic consequences, however, for at the end of his
life (and of the story) he sees a vision of hell as he lies dying, and he realises the
8 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 1, p. 380.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 27
futility of austerities just in time to make it to heaven. a similar example is the
Mudulakkhaṇa-jātaka ( JA 66) in which the Bodhisatta is an ascetic who loses his
ability to levitate after seeing the queen in a state of undress.9 The king gives the
queen to the tormented ascetic, and she cures him of any lust by acting the part
of a demanding, nagging wife. Realising his folly, the ascetic returns the queen to
her husband and retreats to the Himalayas to continue his practices out of harm’s
way. as in the previous example, the message here is that it is acceptable for the
Bodhisatta to behave foolishly, but not for an entire life, for then his karmic
fruits would be inevitable. once again, therefore, the Bodhisatta’s problematic
behaviour is resolved within the course of the jātaka’s story of the past.
in other cases the Bodhisatta’s problematic behaviour is resolved by the story
of the present, which shows the karmic consequences of his actions and thereby
warns the audience not to emulate them. many jātakas are said to have been
related by the Buddha to illustrate the workings of kamma. often this involves
the past actions and present tendencies or situations of his monks or patrons,
for example the Keḷisīla-jātaka ( JA 202), which is told to explain how someone so
learned and accomplished as the elder monk lakuṇṭaka can have the physically
insignificant form of a dwarf. The explanation of course involves a combination
of good and bad deeds in a previous life, leading to mixed fruits in the present.
according to the definition proposed in the previous chapter, this is an apadāna
– a story of karmicly significant deeds – and several of these are included in
the JA and other jātaka texts. However, stories about the Buddha’s bad or mixed
kamma are not considered in the JA, as he is considered to have passed beyond
the realm of kamma (by becoming awakened) by the time he tells the jātakas.
However, if we leave the JA for a moment and enter another Theravāda text,
the Apadāna, we find a set of 12 jātakas in the Pubbakammapiloti that illustrate
past bad actions of the Buddha. The text seeks to explain the suffering that the
Buddha experiences after his awakening, for even he is not free from bodily
illness, slander and attacks from adversaries. The slander is from two women
who, working for heretics, claim that they have been sleeping with the Buddha.
The assaults are caused by the Buddha’s cousin Devadatta, who hires assassins,
and urges a mad elephant to attack. The events in these two categories cause little
actual suffering, as the Buddha shows great forbearance at the slander, and is not
injured by the assassin or the elephant (which he calms with his great mettā –
loving kindness). only a splinter from a rock aimed at crushing him causes injury
to his foot, after two mountains intervene to prevent this from being any more
serious. The Buddha experiences no sorrow or anxiety, and so, as Walters points
out, these events merely highlight his greatness.10 even the attack of dysentery
and the backache and headaches recounted in the suttas seem not to bother the
9 This also happens in JA 251 and 431.
10 Jonathan S. Walters, ‘The Buddha’s Bad Karma: a Problem in the History of
Theravāda Buddhism’, Numen, 37/1 (1990): 70–95, p. 74.

28 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Buddha. However, this does not remove the problem of the reasons behind these
events, which according to the Buddhist paradigm are likely to be karmic.
in the Apadāna, we discover that 12 bad actions resulted in these 12 sufferings
experienced in the Buddha’s final birth (in addition to much suffering in the
intervening births). For example, the Bodhisatta’s slander of a buddha many eons
in the past resulted in him being slandered when he became Buddha. likewise
the Bodhisatta once crushed his half-brother with a rock, which resulted in
him being attacked in the same manner by his cousin. This is a controversial
explanation, as is demonstrated by the outright denial of any karmic cause for
the Buddha’s suffering found in the Milindapañha, the Dhammapadaṭṭhakathā and
the JA.11 The unease which is demonstrated by Theravāda responses to these
stories suggests the possibility that they originated in an alternative school
of Buddhism, and this is supported by the presence of similar stories in the
Mūlasarvāstivāda vinaya and elsewhere.12
Putting up a robust defence of the tradition, the commentary on the
Pubbakammapiloti elaborates the stories and provides further explanation,
pointing out that previous buddhas suffered too, and that the stories provide an
example to ordinary Buddhists who find the perfection of the Buddha daunting,
and wish for reassurance that even they can get on the right path.13 The fact
that these actions occur in the distant past allows the tradition to examine the
Buddha’s flaws without impinging too greatly on his final perfection.
This is really a doctrinal issue to do with the nature of awakening and
residual kamma. These jātakas are told in clear awareness that the Bodhisatta’s
actions are bad, and there is no attempt to justify his behaviour or encourage
others to emulate it; thus the morality of the jātakas is not in question, even if
the doctrinal implications are. Similarly, those jātakas in which the Bodhisatta
behaves in a morally dubious way, but realises his error and reforms within the
story of the past, do not attempt to hold up the Bodhisatta’s behaviour as a good
example. Rather, all these stories show that being on the path to awakening does
not require perfection at all times.
11 Walters, ‘The Buddha’s Bad Karma’, p. 79ff.
12 Similar explanations in the form of jātaka stories – with some variations – are
found in the Anavataptagāthā of the Bhaiṣajyavastu of the Mūlasarvāstivāda vinaya, and the
Daśakarmaplutyavadāna of Kṣemendra’s eleventh-century Bodhisattvāvadāna Kalpalatā,
as well as in the Upāyakauśalya Sūtra, which nonetheless insists that the suffering of
the Buddha was not real, but only skill-in-means. For a discussion of the parallels see
Sally mellick Cutler, ‘Still Suffering after all These aeons: The Continuing effects of the
Buddha’s Bad Karma’ in Peter Connolly and Sue Hamilton (eds), Indian Insights: Buddhism,
Brahminism and Bhakti (london, 1997), pp. 63–82.
13 Walters, ‘The Buddha’s Bad Karma’, p. 88.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 29
Ethics and Wisdom
What about those stories in which the Bodhisatta behaves in a morally dubious
manner, yet this is nonetheless internally presented as acceptable? often this
immoral behaviour is a direct contravention of one of the five precepts ( pañca-
sīlāni), which are accepted by the laity as well as forming the basis for more
elaborate monastic regulations. These are against harming living beings, taking
what is not given, committing sexual misconduct, speaking falsely, and taking
intoxicating substances. These precepts are occasionally transgressed by the
Bodhisatta in his jātaka stories, which therefore portray him going against both
explicitly Buddhist values and what might be considered universal moral values.14
Breaking the precepts is also viewed as the opposite of certain positive qualities
that the Bodhisatta (if he is an example of a good Buddhist) should be cultivating:
kindness and compassion, generosity and renunciation, contentment, preserving
and understanding the truth, and mindfulness respectively.15 Furthermore, sīla is
one of the 10 perfections that the Bodhisatta is said to be developing in his jātaka
stories. Thus those jātakas where the Bodhisatta breaks the precepts require
some justification.
in the Hārita-jātaka ( JA 431) the Bodhisatta considers lying to the king after
having an affair with his wife:
He thought, ‘This king would believe me if i were to say that i haven’t enjoyed
her, but there is no support in this world equal to the truth. Those who have
forsaken the truth may sit at the foot of the bodhi-tree but they are unable to
attain bodhi, so i ought to just speak the truth.’ For in some situations a Bodhisatta
destroys life, takes what is not given, commits sexual misconduct, and drinks
liquor, but he does not lie, attended by deception that breaks with reality.16
14 Jones lists 11 jātakas where the Bodhisatta is involved in harming or killing ( JA 93,
128, 129, 152, 178, 233, 238, 246, 315, 319, 384; p. 61), though three of these actually concern
the issue of vegetarianism (246, 315, 319) rather than killing per se, two contain no actual
action on the Bodhisatta’s part (129, 238), and one of the others is accidental (178). He
also records stories ‘in which the bodhisatta proves vulnerable to sexual temptation and
is admitted to have violated the precept regarding sexuality’ ( JA 66, 251, 263, 431, 507;
p. 87) as well as those in which he steals ( JA 168, 279, 318; p. 136). Jones also provides a
useful discussion of the lists of precepts and virtues discussed in the jātakas, including JA
459 which explicitly addresses the deleterious effects of breaking each of the five precepts
(p. 57ff.). John G. Jones, Tales and Teachings of the Buddha: The Jātaka Stories in Relation to the
Pāli Canon (london, 1979).
15 Peter Harvey, An Introduction to Buddhist Ethics: Foundations, Values and Issues
(Cambridge, 2000), p. 68.
16 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 3, p. 499.

30 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
However, this declaration that a Bodhisatta cannot lie is not supported by all
the jātakas of the JA: for example in the Vānara-jātaka ( JA 342) the Bodhisatta is a
monkey who is befriended by a crocodile. The crocodile’s wife wants to eat the
monkey’s heart, and so the crocodile tricks the monkey into riding on his back.
on the point of being drowned, the monkey tells the crocodile that his heart is
hanging in a tree, and offers to go and fetch it, thereby ensuring his escape back
to dry land.
one explanation for such jātakas is that ‘bad’ behaviour is inevitable within a
long biography of the Buddha, as it is within the long biography of any human.
This explanation is in accord with the Apadāna stories just examined, but without
clear evidence of the karmic consequences. in search of this evidence, we might
argue that the fact that the Bodhisatta is regularly born in the animal realm in
his jātaka stories demonstrates that he cannot have been flawless in his actions.
However, stories in which the Bodhisatta transgresses precepts are included
in Theravāda Buddhist sources without any such justification or apology, and
furthermore they are present in texts that are traditionally used as sources for
ethical guidance. There are, it seems to me, two possible reasons for this: first,
that the morals we might expect to see are subjugated to higher ideals such as
the power of truth and worldly wisdom; and second, that jātakas are outside the
Buddhist moral framework and should not be looked to for ethical guidance. i
will address these in turn.
one example of a jātaka in which the Bodhisatta’s seemingly immoral actions
are presented without apology is the Aṇḍabhūta-jātaka ( JA 62). in this story the
Bodhisatta is a king with a passion for gambling. He always wins, because with
every roll of his dice he repeats a verse:
all rivers wind, all forests are made of wood;
all women, given opportunity, do no good.17
Because this verse is true, the dice land in his favour time after time until his
opponent is driven to a rather extreme response. He has a baby girl brought up
in the confines of a tower, with only female servants and companions. When
she is grown, he adds the line ‘except my girl’ to the end of the Bodhisatta’s
verse, turning the luck his way. To switch the luck back again, the Bodhisatta
pays someone to break into the lodgings of this young woman and seduce her.
The moral of the story, summed up in the verses, is of course that one should fear
woman, since even if you were to carry her about with you at all times you could
never be sure of her.
in this jātaka, two problematic actions of the Bodhisatta go without comment
and are thus implicitly condoned. First, he gambles, which though not explicitly
forbidden, is seen by many Buddhist traditions as a transgression of the second
17 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 1, p. 289. This verse is part of the commentary
– it is not one of the canonical verses.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 31
precept – against taking what is not given.18 We saw in an earlier jātaka that the
Bodhisatta has had occasion to punish cheats, yet in this story he himself gambles
in the knowledge that he is sure to win, which could be seen as akin to cheating.
Second, his treatment of women is problematic. i am not here talking about the
verse he uses, for the position of women in the jātakas is deeply ambiguous.19
a mixture of popular folk-wisdom and a Buddhist desire to reduce attachment
to sense pleasures could easily produce such a sentiment, which in any case is
primarily for comic effect. What i refer to is his payment of a young man to
seduce the captive girl, which must surely qualify as a transgression of the third
precept – against sexual misconduct. even though the Bodhisatta himself does
not perform the deed, he encourages it; indeed he both intends and causes it.
Despite all the dubious actions, it is possible that moral instruction has not
been abandoned or ignored in this jātaka, but that it has instead been directed at
particular ideals at the expense of others. The Bodhisatta wins because he makes
a statement of truth – what is elsewhere called a saccakiriya (‘act of truth’). These,
in jātakas as elsewhere, lead to great benefit to the truth-teller. For example, in a
more famous jātaka of the JA (Vaṭṭaka-jātaka, JA 35) the Bodhisatta is a young quail
who is left defenceless, facing a forest fire. He utters a verse about his situation
and the power of the truth holds back the fire. Similarly, in our jātaka, the truth
of the verse ensures the success of the Bodhisatta in a situation where the laws
of nature should prevail.
as we saw in the Hārita-jātaka, lying is seen as worse than breaking the other
precepts, thus although the Bodhisatta can break any of the other precepts, he
(reputedly) cannot lie, ‘attended by deception that breaks with reality’. This latter
phrase indicates that lying is seen here as being fundamentally at odds with the
truth underlying reality, in other words the dhamma. The Cetiya-jātaka ( JA 422)
contains similar sentiments in a set of verses that the Bodhisatta recounts to a
king whose persistent lying is causing him to gradually sink into the earth:
injured Truth (dhamma) truly harms; not injured it harms nobody;
Therefore one shouldn’t harm the Truth, lest harmed Truth harms you.
The gods leave one who speaks falsely,
His mouth is rotten, and he falls from the heavenly realms,
He who answers falsely when asked an intelligible question.
18 Harvey, An Introduction to Buddhist Ethics, pp. 70–71.
19 For example a cluster of five jātakas in the Itthi-vagga that could be labelled
‘misogynous’ ( JA 61–5) is followed by a jātaka in which a woman saves a hermit from
giving in to lust (66) and a story of a woman whose affection for her husband, son and
brother, and her wisdom in being able to choose between them, is praised (67). For a full
treatment of the place of women in Theravāda jātakas see Naomi appleton, ‘Temptress on
the Path: Women as objects and Subjects in Buddhist Jātaka Stories’ in Pamela anderson
(ed.), New Topics in Feminist Philosophy of Religion: Contestations and Transcendence Incarnate

32 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
o King, if you speak the truth you will be as you were before;
if you speak a lie, you will sink further into the earth of Ceti.
it rains at the wrong time and fails to rain when it should,
For one who answers falsely when asked an intelligible question.20
Similar notions about the priority of truthfulness are found elsewhere, for
example in Dhammapada verse 176:
The person who speaks falsely, transgressing the one Truth (dhamma),
abandoning the other world, there is no evil he cannot do.21
as all of these verses suggest, speaking a lie is going against the dhamma, which
is both the Truth underlying the universe, and the Buddhist teachings that can
save beings from the sufferings of rebirth and redeath. Transgressing the dhamma
by lying demonstrates that one is ignorant of the truth, and is destined for much
future suffering. Telling the truth, even for a morally ambiguous purpose, could
therefore be seen as proof of the Bodhisatta’s awareness of the wider truths that
will later characterise his attainments.
if speaking the truth can be seen as a higher ideal than other ethical principles,
this vindicates the Aṇḍabhūta-jātaka to some extent, for the focus is placed upon
the power of the truth. However, the focus is much more clearly on the danger
of women, and here we find another justification. it is said that this jātaka was
told by the Buddha to a monk who was in danger of breaking his vows of celibacy.
in other words it is designed to preserve an ethical and religious ideal in the
present, even if this happens at the expense of ethics in the past. This is at least
partial justification for the presence of such a story in this jātaka collection.
my second set of examples are those in which the Bodhisatta is born as a
robber, which happens twice in the JA. in the Satapatta-jātaka ( JA 279) the
Bodhisatta is a robber who correctly interprets omens for a passing youth before
letting him pass with his money intact. The intention here is clearly to show him
being a good robber, and indeed we are even told in the commentary that his
robber-nature is not his fault:
Bodhisattas, even though they are great beings, sometimes take the belongings
of others, because of taking birth as a miscreant; they say this is because of a
fault in the constellations.22
20 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 3, pp. 456–8, omitting intervening prose. The
verses go on to state that a liar will only have female children, and that his children will
flee from him.
21 Trans. from o. von Hinüber and K.R. Norman (eds), Dhammapada (oxford, 2003),
p. 50.
22 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 389.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 33
in contrast, in the Kaṇavera-jātaka ( JA 318) the Bodhisatta is a very successful
robber who is saved from execution by a courtesan who has taken a fancy to him.
Too quick-witted for her (and, one might think, a little ungrateful), he knocks
her unconscious and steals her jewels. He refuses to be tempted back by her, as
he suspects she would eventually betray him as she has betrayed others. This
story thus serves as a lesson about the unreliability of women and once again
encourages a wayward monk to stop thinking of returning to his wife. Clearly
this jātaka shows the Bodhisatta assaulting and stealing, yet he is still portrayed
as in the right. This, it seems, has a lot to do with his worldly wisdom and quick-
wittedness, as well as his detachment from the charms of the most sought-after
courtesan in the city.
an even more shocking jātaka about a Bodhisatta-robber is found in the
Mūlasarvāstivāda-vinaya.23 in it, the Bodhisatta steals from the king and rapes the
princess, leaving her pregnant with his child. Years later, when the son correctly
identifies his father in a crowd, the Bodhisatta is presented with the princess’
hand in marriage as a reward from the king for being so clever. The jātaka is told
by the Buddha to illustrate the idea that a son has an innate ability to recognise
his father, as Rāhula did with his father the Buddha, despite the latter creating
several illusory bodies. in this example, the Bodhisatta clearly transgresses two
of the precepts – those against stealing and sexual misconduct. However, once
again the focus is elsewhere: the robber is praised and rewarded for his cleverness,
and so the story is really about worldly wit and wisdom. This may appear to
contradict Buddhist wisdom, since it allows the transgression of Buddhist
precepts. However, on closer inspection, worldly wisdom could be seen as a part
of the Buddhist ideal of wisdom, which is in many cases seen as the highest of all
ideals in Buddhism. it is contained in the lists of perfections aspired to in both
indian (including mahāyāna) and Theravāda texts, and is one of the acquisitions
required for buddhahood and therefore pursued by the Bodhisatta during many
of his births. if we see worldly wisdom as part of the Buddhist concept of wisdom
then once again our only problem is that certain other morals are being ignored
or transgressed in order to demonstrate its power.
The hierarchy in which certain qualities lie is a doctrinal issue as well as a
moral one. Some analyses of the Buddhist path see ethical conduct (sīla) and
wisdom ( paññā) as consecutive parts of the path, thus suggesting that ethics are
irrelevant to the ultimate soteriological goal. in other words good actions are the
preoccupation of the laity and are all about attaining a better rebirth, whereas
nibbāna itself transcends moral values. By this type of analysis, it is perhaps
possible to justify tales showing the power of wisdom over and above the calls of
morality. However, such a view of the Buddhist path has been widely criticised
by both scholars and practitioners, who insist that ethical conduct and wisdom
23 Discussed in John S. Strong, ‘a Family Quest: The Buddha, Yaśodharā, and Rāhula
in the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya’ in Juliane Schober (ed.), Sacred Biography in the Buddhist
Traditions of South and Southeast Asia (Honolulu, 1997), pp. 113–28, pp. 120–21.

34 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
go hand-in-hand throughout the Buddhist path, and that awakened beings are
not beyond good conduct, but only beyond attachment to it and also beyond
its karmic results (since they are free from rebirth). although i agree with this
latter analysis, whichever thesis we accept, two rather awkward facts remain:
first, jātakas are stories of a Bodhisatta who is at times far from awakening, thus
he is arguably not yet at the stage of the path in which ethical conduct could
be abandoned, even if this were his ultimate goal; second, jātakas are used in
Buddhist countries as a source of ethical guidance, and thus we cannot be blamed
for expecting to find morals within them.
Yet of course even this latter statement is problematic, for we can already
see that jātakas are very variable, and that many were probably not composed as
jātakas but rather adapted from existing stories; this early history of jātakas will
be examined in the next chapter. Jātakas could in any case be viewed as falling
outside the Buddhist moral framework, since most of the stories take place
in a time when no Buddha sāsana (teaching) exists, and many revolve around
the actions of animals. as a duggati, the realm of animals is one in which the
attainment of enlightenment is thought impossible, thus it is rare – though
not unfeasible – to consider animals capable of actions conducive to progress
on the Buddhist path.24 Perhaps most importantly, jātakas in many cases are
primarily good stories, and, as Caroline Rhys-Davids puts it in relation to the
JA, ‘the compiler was keener in storymongering than in piety’.25 We should not,
therefore, always look to jātakas for moral instruction. This does not mean that
we should not consider the stories to be Buddhist, but only that they cannot be
viewed as straightforwardly exemplifying Buddhist morals.
one further example that clearly illustrates these concerns is the Biḷāra-
jātaka ( JA 128), in which the Bodhisatta is a big, wise rat living in the forest with
hundreds of ratty followers. a wily jackal pretending to be an ascetic is visited
by them daily, and every day the jackal eats the last to leave. eventually the
Bodhisatta notices and makes sure he is last to leave. When the jackal pounces
he utters a verse about the evil of pretending to be religious then leaps at the
jackal’s throat and kills him, after which:
The company of rats returned and ate the jackal with a crunch crunch crunch.
or rather, i have heard, the first that came got meat, but those that were
behind got none.26
24 This position is borne out by the fact that jātaka stories do not describe animals
meditating or attaining any stage of the Buddhist path, though they do describe animals
making positive moral choices.
25 C.a.F. Rhys-Davids (trans.), Stories of the Buddha (london, 1929), p. xix.
26 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 1, p. 461. This story has a parallel in the
Mahābhārata, where the villain is a cat. as an example of the frequent inconsistency
between the prose and verse portions of the JA, the jātaka verse in fact refers to a cat, as
does the title of the jātaka, but the prose story is about a jackal.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 35
although the Bodhisatta is clearly the hero of this tale, he is not averse to killing
and eating another creature (described in a pretty gruesome manner), albeit
in self-defence. as a rat unaware of Buddhist teachings, his defeat of the jackal
merely serves to demonstrate both his heroism and his concern for the welfare
of his companions. in addition, the story is also found in many other sources in
asia, showing that as a good story it transcends the jātaka genre.
Foolish Bodhisatta
one way in which non-Buddhist or other ‘immoral’ actions are condoned within
the jātakas is through showing the protagonist succeeding because of or despite
his actions. Sometimes, as above, this ‘immoral’ protagonist is the Bodhisatta, and
in other examples it is another protagonist who gets the better of the Bodhisatta
through behaving badly; such stories can make the Bodhisatta appear to be a
fool. one example is the Dūbhiyamakkaṭa-jātaka ( JA 174), in which the Bodhisatta
is a brahmin who draws some water from a well and gives some to a thirsty
monkey. When the monkey has drunk the water, he starts pulling faces at the
Bodhisatta, who chastises him for his ingratitude. The monkey replies:
Who has ever seen or heard of a well-behaved monkey?
Now i will shit on you, for such is our nature!27
He defecates on the Bodhisatta’s head and leaves.
a similar set of events occurs in the Mahisa-jātaka (278), where the Bodhisatta
is a buffalo tormented by a monkey who repeatedly defecates on his back.
He refuses to kill the monkey, taking comfort in the fact that sooner or later
somebody else will, and then he will be free from both the pestering and the
guilt of taking a life:
Proud of his treatment of me, he will do the same to others;
They will kill him, and i will be free.28
Therefore in both these cases we can at least be certain that the monkey will get
the fruit of his action, even if the punishment is not meted out by the Bodhisatta
himself. We might, however, be forgiven for wondering about the karmic
consequences of the Bodhisatta wishing for another creature to kill the monkey.
admittedly, reflecting on the fact that everyone is heir to their own kamma is
said by both Buddhaghosa and Dhammapāla to be the basis or ‘proximate cause’
for the cultivation of upekkhā.29 in a sense this story is an illustration of that idea,
27 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 71.
28 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 386.
29 Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli (trans.), The Path of Purification (Visuddhimagga) by Bhadantācariya
Buddhaghosa (5th edn, Kandy, 1991), p. 311; D.l. Barua (ed.), Achariya Dhammapāla’s
