though these great commentators do not of course recommend taking comfort
from the fact that others will not be so equanimous.
Whatever their moral implications, these two stories leave us (perhaps a
little reluctantly) laughing at the Bodhisatta. He is too kind for his own good,
not smart enough to know what monkeys are like, and covered in excrement on
more than one occasion.
iNSiGNiFiCaNT
i have so far examined jātakas where the Bodhisatta is identified as either a good
or bad character, and sought some explanations for the latter. However, perhaps
even more surprising than identifying the Bodhisatta as a villain, is identifying
him – in his own jātaka stories – as an indifferent or unnecessary character. We
have already encountered a few examples where he merely comments on the
actions of others, or gives advice which may or may not be heeded. in addition
there are some jātakas in which he plays no part at all, and in fact enters the
jātaka only in the samodhāna, where he is identified most often as a tree-deity
who was watching the whole scene, or maybe some passer-by. of all the jātakas
of the JA, it is those in which the Bodhisatta doesn’t feature at all that are most
difficult to reconcile with the JA commentator’s idea of what jātakas are. in this
final section of the chapter i will examine these examples and ask whether the
presence of such jātakas in the JA can be explained by the origins of the stories.
in contrast to the rest of the this chapter i will therefore be starting to examine
the stories in their historical context, in addition to completing my survey of
the Bodhisatta’s characters. This historical focus will prepare us for the next
chapter.
one example of where the Bodhisatta merely observes in silence is the
Gūthapāna-jātaka ( JA 227), which illustrates the dangers of intoxication, whether
by alcohol or lust. it tells of a dung beetle who gets drunk and challenges a
rutting elephant to a duel:
a hero comes together in battle with a strong, fierce hero.
Come, elephant! Turn back! Why do you fearfully run away?
let the people of aṅga and magadha witness our heroism!
The elephant replies:
i will not strike you with my foot, nor with my tusks nor with my trunk.
i will strike you with shit: let the putrid be killed by the putrid.30
Paramatthadīpanī, Being the Commentary on the Cariyā-Piṭaka (london, 1939), p. 281.
30 Trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 2, p. 211. The latter verse was deemed rude
enough to be translated into latin by W.H.D. Rouse for Cowell’s 1895 translation.

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 37
and he kills the dung beetle. Thus intoxication can lead to death, or killing
another living being. in this story the Bodhisatta is a tree-deity who watches
the whole event, but does not even have the opportunity to comment, since
both verses are used up by the main characters. in fact, his presence is not even
mentioned in the atīta-vatthu; only the final identification of the birth mentions
that he was watching. The Bodhisatta is a silent devatā in nine other jātakas,31
and in eight more he merely makes a small comment which has no impact on
the events of the story.32
The Gūthapāna-jātaka is one of many in which merlin Peris sees Greek
influence, drawing our attention in this case to two aesopic fable motifs which
he believes may have inspired the story we find in our jātaka.33 Peris is by no
means the first to have noticed the many parallels between jātakas and Greek
fables, but he is in my view the first to argue persuasively that the influence was
from Greece to india, rather than (or in addition to) vice versa.34 Peris points out
that before the JA, the jātakas in the Nikāyas always portrayed the Bodhisatta
as a human, and none of the stories had any Greek parallels; yet by the time of
the Bhārhut illustrations there were vastly more stories, many identifying the
Bodhisatta with an animal, and many with motifs that emulate Greek sources.
This development is seen in full in the JA, and the period of time during which
the development took place coincides with the arrival of alexander’s huge army
into Northwest india. Peris goes on to present a thorough argument for Greek
influence based upon both a study of internal evidence in parallel stories, and
an investigation of the chronology and history of opportunity in the interactions
between india and Greece. The parallels with Greek myth provide some of the
most convincing evidence for the priority of Greek sources, since not only are
some of the myths centuries older than Buddhism, but parallels are also found
in Herodotus’ historical writings, into which it would arguably be difficult to
incorporate fables from afar. Peris concludes that the influence of Greek motifs
upon many jātakas cannot be denied, though he is quick to emphasise that
such stories ‘constitute but a small fraction of the considerable wealth of story
material that these birth stories of the Buddha contain’.35
it is difficult to resist being pulled into a form of civilisational essentialism in
such arguments, and it is of course problematic to speak of ‘Greek’ and ‘indian’
31 JA 102, 147, 205, 209, 297, 298, 361, 400, 437.
32 JA 13, 18, 38, 139, 283, 294, 295, 492.
33 merlin Peris, Greek Story Motifs in the Jatakas (Colombo, 2004), pp. 198–200.
34 Scholars who have argued the priority of indian stories include T.W. Rhys-Davids,
a. Wagener, otto Keller and J. Hertel; those prefering Greek influence include Benfey,
albrecht Weber, W. Gunion Rutherford and several other reputed classicists; scholars
who have merrily sat upon the fence or argued for coincidence include max müller, e.B.
Cowell, a.B. Keith and Joseph Jacobs. See chapter one of Peris, Greek Story Motifs, for a
thorough discussion of the arguments on all sides.
35 Peris, Greek Story Motifs, p. 354.

38 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
in these contexts without lengthy qualification. However, we need not become
entangled in the arguments here, since it is at least clear that some of the jātakas
of the JA were not composed as jātakas, but were reworked from existing sources.
The origins of such jātakas are of interest to us only in that they highlight the fact
that there must have been a process of absorption, during which a character is
identified with the Bodhisatta and the story is put in the mouth of the Buddha. it
is reasonable to suggest that if a story has been composed without the character
of the Bodhisatta in mind, it might at times be difficult to see which role to assign
him, and he might end up identified merely as a silent or insignificant witness.
Thus identifications of the Bodhisatta with characters of no importance, as well
as with morally dubious or at the very least non-Buddhist characters, might be
explained by the importing of a story into the jātaka genre. However, though
this might explain the origins of the stories, it does not excuse the fact that such
stories are later claimed to be part of the Buddha’s biography. i will return to
this issue later.
Greek fables are not the only types of story to have been transformed into
jātakas; many other stories appear to have been appropriated by the JA to further
its own ends. These include those stories which are really apadānas, stories about
karmicly significant deeds and their fruit. Such stories may indeed be about the
Buddha’s karmic fruit, both positive (as in several jātakas, especially those about
his acquisition of perfections) and negative (as in the Pubbakammapiloti discussed
above). However, often apadānas are about the karmic deeds of the Buddha’s
followers, such as in the Keḷisīla-jātaka ( JA 202) mentioned earlier, which explains
the mixed karmic fruits of lakuṇṭaka Thera; in this story the Bodhisatta is Sakka,
and teaches the king (lakuṇṭaka) to be better behaved. in some stories the
difference of the past is used to inspire a change in the present, for example the
Vaṇṇupatha-jātaka ( JA 2) tells of the past perseverance of a man who is currently
a faint-hearted monk. in addition, a very common format is a parallel story in
the past; in other words the actions of a person in the paccuppannavatthu are
repeated in the atītavatthu, with the introduction ‘this is not the first time that
so and so has done such and such’. examples of this include stories about the
idiocy of the elder Udāyi (see JA 5, Taṇḍulanāli-jātaka), also called lāḷudāyi (see
JA 123, Naṅgalīsa-jātaka), as well as the numerous stories that relate Devadatta’s
attempts to kill the Bodhisatta and split the saṅgha. in all such stories the focus
is naturally upon a character other than the Bodhisatta.
The stories that illustrate parallel actions in the past do not really fit our
definition of apadāna. For Peris, such stories are more accurately described as
fables:
The practice of the Buddha, when any event of importance has taken place, to
refer to a similar event involving the same people in a past life is, from a karmic
point of view, neither here nor there. What the jataka achieves, however, is
that, by the detachment of the participants of the past life up to the point of
the samodhana, to make his hearers view their behaviour objectively, critically

The Bodhisatta in Jātaka Stories 39
– and sometimes even humorously, and thus self-evaluate their actions of the
present … the function is thus truly that of fable, holding up the former to the
latter as a mirror in which the moral assessment that the speaker wishes to
make can be the more clearly brought home.36
The fable thus contrasts with the apadāna, where it is a karmic consequence that
is illustrated. Both fable and apadāna are present in the JA, and neither requires
the Bodhisatta to play a significant role. once again, we have an explanation
for the minor character of the Bodhisatta in some of his jātakas, yet once again,
this cannot explain the fact that such stories are said to be part of the Buddha’s
biography, during which he traverses the long path to buddhahood.
CoNClUSioN
as is now clear, it is rather an oversimplification to say that the jātakas of the
JA illustrate the magnificence of the Bodhisatta as he strives for the conditions
of buddhahood. in fact, there are many different types of story contained in
the JA and related texts, as several scholars have noted, prompting them to
formulate typologies of various kinds. Since my focus is upon the Bodhisatta, i
have distinguished between those stories with positive, negative and indifferent
characterisations. Within this discussion i have examined the nature of apadānas
(illustrating the karmicly significant deeds of either the Buddha or one of his
followers) and fables (illustrating a moral point as a reflection of the present
situation). The importance of both these types of story in the teaching methods
of the Buddha can help to explain their presence in the jātaka collection, where
they nevertheless challenge the overall rationale for the collection. in addition, i
have hinted at the history of the collection, which might explain the presence of
stories that challenge or merely ignore Buddhist moral and religious values. This
whistle-stop tour through some of the jātakas has therefore helped to illustrate
the variety that makes it so surprising that the JA presents itself as an exemplary
biography.
if we cannot find evidence within individual stories that jātakas demonstrate
the Bodhisatta’s long path to perfection, we might ask where this idea comes
from, and how it is reconciled with the varied characterisation of the Bodhisatta.
To see this we must examine the history of how and why the stories were told,
collected and presented. This will be the subject of the next chapter.
36 Peris, Greek Story Motifs, p. 45.

Chapter 3
The Creation of a Jātaka Genre
in the dedicatory verses at the beginning of the Jātakatthavaṇṇanā ( JA) the fifth-
century commentator1 gives us his idea of what a jātaka is:
Having bowed at the feet of the great sage, the world-protector, by whom
measureless benefit was made during thousands of millions of births, and
having made homage to the Dhamma, and honoured the Saṅgha that is the
vessel of all honour, destroying all adversity through the power of the merit
that results from the veneration of the three jewels, [1–3]
The jātakas, beginning with the Appaṇṇaka, taught formerly by the splendid
great sage, relating to various tasks, in which the teacher, the leader, desirous
of carrying the world across, brought to fruition over a long time the endless
conditions for bodhi, all those which were chanted together under the name
‘Jātaka’ when the collections of Dhamma were being made, [4–6]
Having been approached and entreated by the elder atthadassin, who wishes
for the perpetuation of this chronicle of the Buddha,2 who shuns society and
always lives with his co-monks, and indeed by the learned Buddhamitta of
calm mind, who is well-versed in methods, and from the mahiṃsāsaka lineage,
and also by the monk Buddhadeva of pure intellect, [7–9]
i will speak the commentary on the Jātaka that illuminates the unimaginable
magnificence of the deeds of a Great man, depending upon the approaches of the
mahāvihāra inhabitants.3 may the virtuous grasp the virtue while i speak. [10–11]4

likely to have been a Sri lankan monk working shortly after the time of Buddhaghosa,
following the tradition established by that great commentator. a thorough discussion is

42 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
according to this commentator, therefore, jātakas are those which were chanted
as such during the early compilation of the Buddha’s discourses. They were
related by the Buddha, and show how he encountered many tasks and spent
many lives perfecting the conditions required for awakening.
How do the stories i have just examined fit into this overall rationale? Having
taken a swift tour through the great diversity found in individual jātaka stories,
and the variety in the characters which the Bodhisatta plays in stories that became
associated with his long biography, i must now turn my attention to jātaka texts,
which try in their own way to unify the stories through some sort of biographical
association. in contrast to the predominantly synchronic view of the stories
found in the previous chapter, i will here be examining the developments of the
jātaka genre through history. Here i will address the changing role of jātakas and
the emergence of a developed jātaka genre that accompanied the formation of
the Theravāda scriptures. This is not a mere textual history, but rather a history
of the relationship between the Buddha/Bodhisatta and his jātaka stories.
as i discussed in Chapter 1, scholars have tended to view jātakas as a static
genre, rather than one that changes in appearance and use over time. as a
consequence, few scholars have seriously examined the history of Theravāda
jātakas, and fewer still with any attention to their ideological content. T.W. Rhys-
Davids, in a quest to ascertain the period to which the cultural and political
evidence contained in the JA referred, considered the comparative age of the
different parts of the JA. He argued that the original and canonical jātaka work
consisted only of verses, and that the JA consists of a commentary to those
verses; this view is now widely accepted. Rhys-Davids also traced stories in sutta
and vinaya that have parallels in the JA, pointing out that such stories ‘usually
give us neither framework nor verses. in them the Buddha, in his previous birth,
is never identified with an animal, or even with an ordinary man. He is identified
only with some famous sage of bygone times’.5 These comments, though deeply
interesting in regard to the developing rationale of the jātaka genre, are passed
over without further notice by him, on the way to conclusions about the value
of jātakas as folklore.
Rhys-Davids’ observations form part of the evidence for De’s Significance and
Importance of Jātakas. Briefly put, De contends that jātakas must be studied away
from their association with the Bodhisatta, since this was a late association and
not part of their initial rationale. in fact, jātakas were originally only the verses
now found in the Jātakapāli, and these contain nothing distinctively Buddhist.
in pursuit of his conclusion that jātakas demonstrate ‘the fundamental unity of
the two great religions – Hinduism and Buddhism’6 De is guilty of the occasional
in square brackets are the verse numbers; the nature of the passage makes it difficult to
render verse-by-verse.
5 T.W. Rhys-Davids, Buddhist India (london, 1903), p. 207.
6 Gokuldas De, Significance and Importance of Jātakas (Calcutta, 1951), p. xx.

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 43
(possibly wilful) misunderstanding of Buddhist doctrine,7 and his strongly
polemical tone is matched by a confused and ill-formed argument. For all De’s
faults, however, he must be praised for raising several issues that have been
otherwise largely ignored in the study of jātakas. Not only does he highlight the
worth of tracing the development of jātakas from pre-Buddhist times through
to the relatively late JA collection, he also demonstrates that the Bodhisatta
ideal (and the pāramitā doctrine that goes hand-in-hand with it) is only a late
association with the genre. He insists that the state of jātakas before the great
jātaka collection must be examined if we are to appreciate the true value of these
stories. although i cannot agree with De’s polemic, and frequently find fault in
his arguments, his contribution to the study of jātakas is significant because of
this insistence.
in contrast to De’s work, von Hinüber’s Entstehung und Aufbau der Jātaka-
Sammlung is a meticulous analysis of the structure and history of the JA. Von
Hinüber argues that the earliest form of jātaka in the Theravāda canon is a story
told by the Buddha entirely in prose; such jātakas are found in the Sutta Piṭaka. in
the Vinaya Piṭaka, stories appear with moral verses, though these contain nothing
specifically Buddhist and are rarely incorporated into the stories themselves.
once we reach the JA, the prose has lost its canonical status, since a text without
fixed form (as in ākhyāna8) cannot be included in the canon. The verses therefore
become the backbone of the collection, and are seen as the canonical core,
despite their late arrival in the genre.9
Von Hinüber restricts himself to a philological textual approach, and is
uninterested in the ideological developments that form the focus of my own
study. Thus, although i will draw upon von Hinüber’s work in support of my own
arguments, my purpose is rather different from his. and although my purpose in
some ways overlaps with De, insofar as he wants to ascertain how and when the
Bodhisatta ideal became associated with jātakas, his influence on my arguments
is minimal. i wish to take a new approach that focuses upon the religious
ideological content of the different forms of jātaka, tracing any developments
through the texts. my approach is founded upon certain strands of modern genre

theory, inasmuch as i hold that the jātaka genre constantly evolved and became
slightly redefined by every text composed within it. Thus jātaka texts have much
to reveal about the development of the jātaka genre towards an association with
Buddha- and Bodhisatta-biography.
JĀTaKaS BeFoRe JĀTaKa TexTS
Before jātakas were brought together into dedicated texts such as the Jātaka-
pāli, JA and Cariyāpiṭaka, we find several embedded in sutta and vinaya material.
Such stories show signs of a stronger association with the Buddha than with the
Bodhisatta. These ‘embedded jātakas’ must therefore be viewed in the light of
Theravāda traditions about both the memory of past births, and the categorisation
of the scriptures. only after such an investigation can we productively begin our
examination of the Theravāda jātaka collections.
Recollecting Lives: The Doctrinal Foundation of Jātaka Stories
arguably, the very concept of a jātaka is inextricably tied up in the Buddha’s
biography, and can be traced back to the moment of the Buddha’s awakening.
according to several well-known passages in the Sutta Piṭaka, the Bodhisatta
had three different realisations during each of the three watches of the night in
which he became awakened. in the first watch of the night he saw his previous
births:
i recollected my manifold past lives, that is to say, one birth, two, three, four,
five births, ten, twenty, thirty, forty, fifty births, a hundred births, a thousand
births, many ages of world contraction, many ages of world expansion, many
ages of world contraction and expansion: ‘i was there so-named, of such a race,
with such an appearance, such food, such experience of pleasure and pain,
such a life term … and passing away thence i reappeared here’ – thus with
details and particulars i recollected my manifold past life. This was the first
true knowledge attained by me in the first watch of the night.10
This part of his awakening experience might be seen as the beginning of the
idea of a jātaka story. Yet at this stage there is no mention of the reasons behind
his progression from life to life; instead we are just left with a sense of the
tiring infinitude of the process. However, in the second watch of the night the
Bodhisatta saw the effects of actions:
10 Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli (trans.), The Life of the Buddha According to the Pali Canon (3rd
edn, Kandy, 1992), pp. 23–4, from Majjhima Nikāya 36.

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 45
i saw beings passing away and reappearing, inferior and superior, fair and ugly,
happy and unhappy in their destinations. i understood how beings pass on
according to their actions: ‘These worthy beings who were ill-conducted in
body, speech and mind, revilers of noble-ones, wrong in their views, giving
effect to wrong view in their actions, on the dissolution of the body, after
death, have reappeared in states of privation, in an unhappy destination, in
perdition, even in hell; but these worthy beings who were well-conducted in
body, speech and mind, not revilers of noble-ones, right in their views, giving
effect to right view in their actions, on the dissolution of the body, after death,
have reappeared in a happy destination, even in a heavenly world.’ … This was
the second true knowledge attained by me in the second watch of the night.11
This might be seen as the beginning of the concept of an apadāna story. Having
realised, in the third watch of the night, the knowledge of the destruction of the
defilements, the Bodhisatta became Buddha, and proceeded to use stories about
previous births as part of his teachings.
The ability of the Buddha to recollect his own lives is certainly necessary for
his ability to tell jātakas. a recurrent formula in the JA describes the Buddha’s
telling of the stories of the past as ‘making clear that which was concealed [from
his audience] by rebirth’,12 implying that the Buddha’s vision gave him this
special ability to narrate jātakas. However, the ability to see one’s past births,
though mentioned in most relations of the night of awakening, is not limited to
awakened beings. Recollection of past lives, or past abodes (P pubbenivāsānussati;
Skt pūrvanivāsānusmṛti) is one of the six higher knowledges (P abhiññā; Skt abhijñā),
only the last of which – the knowledge of the destruction of the defilements
– is limited to enlightened beings. The other five, including the memory of past
abodes, are attainable through intense meditation, and thus are not even limited
to Buddhist practitioners, though Buddhaghosa remarks that non-Buddhists
can only remember as far back as 40 eons, since their understanding is so
weak.13 indeed the Pāli canon contains past birth stories related by the Buddha’s
followers.14 in addition, according to the Jātaka-nidānakathā the Bodhisatta
attains the five higher knowledges before he even makes his first aspiration to
11 Ñāṇamoli, The Life of the Buddha, p. 24.
12 bhavantarena paṭicchannakāraṇaṃ pākaṭam akāsi – JA stories 1–13 are introduced
in this way, after which the formula is dropped, though the commentator states that it
should nonetheless be assumed to be part of the experience of each story.
13 Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli (trans.), The Path of Purification (Visuddhimagga) by Bhadantācariya
Buddhaghosa (5th edn, Kandy, 1991), p. 407; Visuddhimagga xiii 16. The Buddha can see
millions of eons, and the varying types of followers varying degrees in between.

50) of the Majjhima Nikāya.

buddhahood as Sumedha at the feet of Dīpaṃkara Buddha.15 His recollection
of past births during the night of his awakening cannot therefore be seen as a
defining feature of his enlightenment.
an obvious question might be why the memory of past abodes was included
in accounts of the Buddha’s awakening, if it is in fact a relatively unimpressive
attainment. Possible answers to this have been examined by lopez, who suggests
that it is memory’s close link to personal identity that makes its inclusion in
the account of awakening necessary, as a contrast to (and in tension with) the
realisation of anattā, for ‘the bodhisatta sees the past and present order of the
world in the first two watches of the night. But when he sees that the ordered
world has no essence, he is awakened’.16 as such, it is necessary to demonstrate
the continuity and identity in past births – a realisation common with the
Brahmanical tradition – in order to transcend this in the exclusively Buddhist
realisation of conditionality that forms awakening.
if the memory of former lives is not a uniquely Buddhist phenomenon, why is
Buddhism unique in having such a vibrant tradition of narrative recollections of
the past births of its founder?17 it is possible that this account of the awakening
boosted the importance of recollecting births, thus inspiring the rising numbers
of jātaka stories.18 it may also have influenced the association between stories
of past births and the Buddha’s ability to see deep into the past. Yet if the
Buddha can see the births of others, and selected others can see their own as
well as his, we have to ask how the jātaka genre became defined as a story of
the past life of the Buddha related by himself (albeit sometimes later repeated
by others). it is not self-evident that this is what a jātaka necessarily is; rather it
is a definition developed and propagated by the texts. We have here a doctrinal
(and mythological or hagiographical) justification for the Buddha’s ability to tell
jātakas, but the strong association of jātakas with Buddha and Bodhisatta is a
development that requires further explanation.

Jātakaṭṭhakathā (oxford, 1990), p. 8. This aspiration, confirmed by a prediction by the
buddha of the time, traditionally marks the beginning of the Bodhisatta’s journey towards
buddhahood. See Chapter 5 for a full discussion.
16 Donald S. lopez Jr., ‘memories of the Buddha’ in Janet Gyatso (ed.), In the Mirror of

preserve more stories of the past births of unawakened beings, and these are used in quite
a different way to equivalent stories in Buddhist traditions. Clearly each tradition has

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 47
Embedded Jātakas
it will of course aid my historical analysis to look closely at the earliest examples
of jātaka stories, as preserved in sutta and vinaya material. Three stories
found in the JA (1, 168 and 371) are found in sutta and vinaya texts without an
identification of the Buddha with any of the characters, and without the jātaka
verses.19 Seven further stories ( JA 28, 31, 37, 91, 136, 253 and 309) are found with
no identification but containing the same verses as are preserved in the JA.20 it is
clear from these examples that stories later identified as jātakas were not always
associated in their earlier form with the person of the Bodhisatta. Nor indeed
were they necessarily associated with the Buddha’s teaching career: two stories
in the Pāyāsi Sutta of the Dīgha Nikāya which are later identified as jātakas ( JA 1
and 91) are narrated by the disciple Kassapa, after the Buddha’s parinibbāna.
Two further stories from the JA are found in sutta texts, and this time they
are identified as stories about a previous birth of the Buddha. in the Majjhima
Nikāya we find the story of King makhādeva, who renounced his throne after
the discovery of a grey hair. This story parallels those preserved in JA 9 and
541, the Makhādeva-jātaka and Nimi-jātaka, though none of the jātaka verses are

of the jātaka story, which refers directly to this passage for its context.

48 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
found in the sutta version.21 in the Dīgha Nikāya is the story of mahāsudassana,
a wealthy king from the glorious past of Kusinārā. This story forms JA 95, the
Mahāsudassana-jātaka, where the same verse is also found.22 like the other
parallel stories to the JA found in sutta and vinaya material, these two stories
are introduced with bhūtapubbaṃ ‘formerly’, in contrast to the JA, where stories
are introduced with the term atīte ‘in the past’. None of the stories use the word
‘jātaka’ or ‘bodhisatta’, so it is perhaps unfair to consider even these two stories
as examples of the jātaka genre at this stage. However, these stories must have
influenced what later became standard for the genre, and so they are worthy of
a little investigation.
in both of these stories the Bodhisatta is identified as a king, but despite
the limited characterisations the material reveals much about attitudes towards
Buddha and Bodhisatta. The story of King mahāsudassana is told after Ānanda
laments that the Tathāgata should die in a great city, not a little insignificant
town like Kusinārā. The story’s elaborate description of the great wealth
belonging to the past king of Kusinārā (the Bodhisatta) not only lends status
to the place where the Buddha is about to die, but also serves as a final lesson
to Ānanda on the nature of impermanence. Thus Ānanda is at once comforted
by the knowledge that his teacher is not about to end his life in a place of no
significance, whilst also being reminded that the sort of significance that comes
from riches is ultimately unsatisfactory. in yet another layer of meaning, we see
that the Bodhisatta once had all the riches a person could ever conceive of, yet
remains insignificant in comparison with the Buddha.
Such a comparison between Buddha and Bodhisatta is clearer still in the
story of King makhādeva, who renounces the world at the sight of his first grey
hair, and entreats his son to continue this practice. He, and each of a long line
of descendents, thus attain the Brahma realm. The last in the line is King Nimi,
who in the JA is also identified with the Bodhisatta. in the sutta, however, only
makhādeva is the Bodhisatta, and he thus institutes a good practice which is
followed by many others. But, the Buddha concludes, that practice only led
to rebirth in the Brahma realm, not to nibbāna. in contrast, he says, the good
practice instituted by the Buddha – the eightfold path – leads to much greater
things.
This situation is echoed in some of the stories which identify themselves
as being about the Buddha’s past births but are nevertheless not found in the
JA. one such story is in the Kūṭadanta Sutta of the Dīgha Nikāya,23 which tells of
how an advisor to King Vijita arranged a great (bloodless) sacrifice for him. The
audience for this story includes the brahmin Kūṭadanta, who at its conclusion
asks whether the Buddha himself witnessed the events. The Buddha replies

22 DN 17 Mahāsudassana (Rhys-Davids and Carpenter, Dīgha-nikāya, vol. 2, p. 169ff.).

that he was at that time the king’s advisor, and then gives various instructions
about how to perform even better sacrifices than that performed by King Vijita;
such ‘sacrifices’ (yañña) range from giving gifts specifically to renunciants, to
undertaking Buddhist meditative practices. Thus, we learn, the Bodhisatta does
well, but the Buddha does better.
in the Pacetana Sutta of the Aṅguttara Nikāya24 the Bodhisatta is wheelwright
to King Pacetana, and makes two new wheels ready for a battle. one wheel he
makes in six months less six days; this wheel is magnificent. The other he makes
in the remaining six days, and because it is crooked and flawed it is inferior. The
Buddha sums up the birth and says:
at that time, monks, i was skilled in regard to crooked wood, wood with flaws
and faults. Now, monks, i – arhat and Fully enlightened one – am skilled with
regard to the crookedness, faults and flaws of the body, the crookedness, faults
and flaws of speech, the crookedness, faults and flaws of the mind.25
again, we have a direct contrast being made between the mundane skills of the
Bodhisatta and the religious skills of the Buddha. Similarly, in the Mahāgovinda
Sutta of the Dīgha Nikāya,26 the Bodhisatta is identified as a brahmin named
Jotipāla who is minister to a king, and is followed into renunciation by the king
and several hundred others, all of whom progress as a result to birth as gods
or gandhabbas (spirits). again, however, the Bodhisatta’s fault is highlighted,
for, as the Buddha points out, progress towards the heavens is not as good as
progress towards nibbāna; thus the teachings of Jotipāla Bodhisatta are nothing
in comparison with those of Gotama Buddha.
Rather than glorifying the progress of the Bodhisatta, these early jātakas
put such Bodhisattas in direct contrast to the Buddha – their achievements are
great, but the Buddha’s are on a different scale altogether. The difference is not
highlighted in order to demonstrate the failings of the Bodhisatta, however;
on the contrary one gets the impression the Bodhisatta did all that he could,
bearing in mind the limitations of being in a world without Buddhism. Such a
limitation is highlighted most explicitly by the story of Brahmin Velāma in the
Aṅguttara Nikāya,27 in which the Bodhisatta gives great gifts which are elaborately
described. The Buddha, however, points out that ‘though the householder
Brahmin Velāma gave very great gifts, had he fed a single person of right view
there would have been much greater fruit’.28 The Buddha goes on to explain that
the gift would have been much greater had there been worthy recipients (in
24 AN 3, 15 Pacetana (Richard morris and edmund Hardy (eds), Aṅguttara-nikāya (5
vols, london, 1885–1900), vol. 1, p. 110ff.).
25 Trans. from morris and Hardy, Aṅguttara-nikāya, vol. 1, p. 112.
26 DN 19 Mahāgovinda (Rhys-Davids and Carpenter, Dīgha-nikāya, vol. 2, p. 220ff.).
27 AN 9, 20 Velāma (morris and Hardy, Aṅguttara-nikāya, vol. 4, p. 393ff.).
28 Trans. from morris and Hardy, Aṅguttara-nikāya, vol. 4, p. 394.

50 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
ascending order of worth: people of right view, once-returners, non-returners,
arahats, paccekabuddhas and tathāgatas) or had the donor resolved to keep the
precepts or conceived a thought of impermanence. Such things, we are implicitly
told, were not possible for the Bodhisatta when he was the Brahmin Velāma. We
are thus made to appreciate how much better the situation is with Buddhism in
the world.
The contrast between a past without Buddhism and a present with Buddhism,
as drawn in such stories, is instructive in that it allows the audience to see how
much benefit the Buddha has brought to the world. it is interesting to compare
this focus with ohnuma’s distinction between the ‘ethos of the jātaka’ (perfection,
necessary in a buddha-less world) and ‘ethos of the avadāna’ (devotion, enough
in a world with Buddhism), mentioned earlier.29 The situation in our context is
slightly different from that examined by ohnuma, since the Bodhisatta’s reaction
to being stuck in a buddha-less world is not to perform extreme acts of self-
sacrifice or other demonstrations of the pāramitās (as in ohnuma’s examples),
but rather to just do the best he can within the non-Buddhist framework. His
actions, positive though they may be, are shown to be insignificant in relation to
the magnificent acts of the Buddha. it is almost as if we are being told that the
Bodhisatta is not Buddhist. Such stories must therefore date to before any fully
formed doctrine of the bodhisatta path.
in contrast to such stories, in the Ghaṭīkāra Sutta of the Majjhima Nikāya30
we find a story of the Bodhisatta’s actions in the time of another buddha. Here
Gotama Buddha identifies himself with another brahmin named Jotipāla, who
dismisses several times his friend Ghaṭīkāra the potter’s suggestion that they
visit Kassapa Buddha, saying there is no point in visiting a ‘little bald ascetic’
(muṇḍaka samaṇaka). He finally relents, hears a teaching, and goes forth into
Kassapa Buddha’s order. The story then focuses upon the benefits felt by his
companion the potter as a result of supporting Kassapa Buddha and his monks.
There is no mention of Jotipāla’s subsequent achievements in the Buddhist
order, and so attention is focused upon his initial reviling of Kassapa Buddha,
though the consequences of this are not commented on in the story. in the
Pubbakammapiloti of the Apadāna this episode is given as the karmic cause of
Gotama Buddha’s six years of suffering during severe austerities.31 in this latter
text, therefore, Jotipāla’s less-than-exemplary behaviour is highlighted in order
to demonstrate that the sufferings of the Buddha’s final life can be explained by
his misbehaviour in the past.
looking at jātakas as they might have been before the compilation of jātaka
texts has several benefits. First, they remind us that the JA is not an exhaustive
29 Reiko ohnuma, Head, Eyes, Flesh, and Blood: Giving Away the Body in Indian Buddhist
Literature (New York, 2007), as discussed in Chapter 1.
30 mN 81 Ghatīkāra (Trenckner and Chalmers, Majjhima-nikāya, vol. 2, 1, p. 45ff.).
31 See Chapter 2 for a discussion of the significance of this text in relation to jātakas
and the Bodhisatta’s ability to misbehave.

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 51
collection. Second, they demonstrate that some stories only became identified
as jātakas with their inclusion in jātaka texts. Third, they show that where the
Buddha is identified as one of the characters in these early stories, this is almost
always done in order to point out the contrast in value between Bodhisatta and
Buddha. The idea that jātakas demonstrate the Bodhisatta’s gradual path to
perfection is not found in such texts; rather the stories show how special the
Buddha is, and what impact he has had on the world through his presence and
teachings. The jātaka genre thus has roots in some of the earlier texts, yet in quite
a different form, both structurally and stylistically (as von Hinüber has shown),
and ideologically. Now that we are clear about the foundations upon which the
later texts rest, we may move on to an examination of the developments in the
genre.
Recollecting Texts: The Emergence of a Genre
The Theravāda canon was formed at a time when the preservation of scripture
was done by learned monastics who would memorise portions of it. Thus the
early ‘councils’ of Buddhism, where the scriptures were assembled, are referred
to as the saṃgīti, or ‘singing together’. one can only speculate about the nature
of the earliest scriptures, since there is little evidence about which texts formed
a part at this time. The earliest reliably datable evidence we have for Buddhist
literature is an asokan inscription from the third century bce, which contains
a list of Buddhist scriptures, although the exact identification of the texts
mentioned is still under debate.32 Textual evidence is unreliable by comparison,
but one classification of scripture that is considered to be very old is that into nine
limbs (navaṅga): sutta (discourses), geyya (recitation), veyyākaraṇa (expositions),
gāthā (verses), udāna (utterances of joy), itivuttaka (‘thus said’s), jātaka (birth
stories), abbhutadhamma (marvellous phenomena) and vedalla (analyses).33 it is
quite clear that this list does not refer to individual texts, but rather to ‘types’ of
text (we might even, in the present context, say ‘genres’). ‘Jātaka’ forms one of
these types, while ‘apadāna’ is not mentioned at this stage, suggesting that this
had not yet emerged as a genre in its own right, and is perhaps counted under
‘jātaka’; as we have seen, many apadānas are included in the JA.
32 Calcutta-Bairat minor rock edict: e. Hultzsch, Inscriptions of Aśoka (New Delhi,
1991), pp. 172–4. There is no mention of jātakas.
33 See K.R. Norman, Pāli Literature (Wiesbaden, 1983), pp. 15–16 for instances of this
list. These lists occur in some of the early portions of the canon, although the earliest
lists only contain only three or four limbs, and no jātakas. in most Sanskrit materials we
find lists of 12, which include avadāna. For an overview of the lists and their adoption by
different schools see etienne lamotte, History of Indian Buddhism from the Origins to the Śaka
Era, trans. from French by Sara Boin-Webb (louvain la Neuve, 1988), p. 143ff.

52 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
The preservers and reciters of the Theravāda scriptures – the bhāṇakas
(and bhāṇikās)34 – specialised in different sections of sutta material, and thus
we find mention in Pāli commentaries of bhāṇaka traditions for the Dīgha,
Majjhima, Saṃyutta and Aṅguttara Nikāyas, including some references to disputes
between the members of different traditions. in the Milindapañha we find
‘Jātakabhāṇakā Dīghabhāṇakā Majjhimabhāṇakā Saṃyuttabhāṇakā Anguttarabhāṇakā
Khuddakabhāṇakā’35 as part of a list of inhabitants of the Blessed one’s ‘city of
dhamma’, in an elaborate metaphor that compares the Buddha to an architect of
a city. This brings the list to six, and it is noteworthy that this text refers to Jātaka-
bhāṇakas as separate from the Khuddaka-bhāṇakas, a reasonable practicality
bearing in mind the size of the JA. mori points out that in fact many lists omit
the Khuddaka-bhāṇakas even when they mention Jātaka-bhāṇakas, suggesting
that some form of jātaka collection may predate the compilation of the Khuddaka
Nikāya.36
in the Samantapāsādikā (Buddhaghosa’s commentary on the vinaya), the
requirements for bhāṇakas are laid out. Here we learn that bhāṇakas need not
know the entire nikāya, thus Majjhima-bhāṇakas should know at least the first
50 discourses, and Dīgha-bhāṇakas should know the Mahāvagga. Jātaka-bhāṇakas
should know ‘the jātaka with its commentary’ (sāṭṭhakathaṃ jātakaṃ), as well as,
according to the mahāpaccarī commentary, the Dhammapada with its stories
(dhammapadam pi saha vatthunā).37 That some expected Jātaka-bhāṇakas to learn
stories from the Dhammapada commentary as well as the jātaka stories of the JA,
suggests that these particular bhāṇakas might double up as storytellers; indeed
their specialism could hardly be anything else. The important factor here must
therefore be the illuminating quality of stories, rather than any association with
the Bodhisatta.
Such references can only confirm that jātakas were subject to their own
bhāṇaka tradition from before the commentarial period (and, therefore, before
the JA as we have it now). in relation to the earlier history of jātakas, adikaram
records references to a Jātaka-bhāṇaka existing at the time of the Buddha, another
preaching stories to a king in the first century ce, and another referred to as
a Mahā-jātaka-bhāṇaka who specialised in reciting the Vessantara-jātaka. These
references are also rather late (around the fifth century ce), but they do suggest
34 i am heavily reliant here on the thorough survey of materials relating to the
bhāṇakas of various types found in e.W. adikaram, Early History of Buddhism in Ceylon
(Dehiwala, Sri lanka, 1946), chapter 3, as well as S. mori, ‘The origin and History of
the Bhānaka Tradition’ in Y. Karunadasa (ed.), ānanda: Papers on Buddhism and Indology
(Colombo, 1990), pp. 123–9.
35 V. Trenckner (ed.), The Milindapañho (london, 1962), pp. 341–2.

on the Vinaya Piṭaka, vol. 4 (london, 1934), p. 789.

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 53
the possibility of a sustained tradition from early times.38 This is supported by
a first-century ce epigraphic reference to a Sri lankan donor monk who was
a Jātaka-bhāṇaka.39 That jātakas were a recognised genre from as early as the
second century bce is in any case attested by the presence of the word ‘jātaka’
in the labels for the jātaka reliefs at Bhārhut, though the precise meaning of the
term at this time cannot be known.
in summary, the early history of the jātaka genre is difficult to trace, though
we can see from the division of scriptures, the bhāṇaka traditions, and the
inscriptions at Bhārhut, that ‘jātaka’ is considered to be a distinct genre from
at least a few centuries after the time of the Buddha. in addition, the idea of
telling stories of past births is conceptually linked to the story of the Buddha’s
awakening, and stories that demonstrate his abilities to recall past lives are
found in both sutta and vinaya materials. However, there is no evidence at this
stage that the genre had any relation to the path of the Bodhisatta, indeed a
tendency to demonstrate the great abilities of the Buddha, or perhaps just
a desire to tell good stories, is more prominent. The emergence of a defined
jātaka collection seems to have been a gradual process, perhaps linked to the oral
preservation of stories and the continued use of such stories in sermons. at some
point this random assortment of jātakas, or rather the verses relating to them,
was collected together into one text – the Jātakapāli.
the JāTAKAPāLI and the KHUDDAKA NIKāyA
The first jātaka text to emerge in the Theravāda tradition was the Jātakapāli,40 a
collection of verses which now forms the tenth section of the Khuddaka Nikāya of
the Theravāda canon. it is not clear at what stage the text was compiled, though
some of the verses are found in other parts of the canon as well as in texts from
other schools, and the first pāda of the Jātakapāli verse of the Aṇḍabhūta-jātaka
( JA 62) is found inscribed as its title at Bhārhut. Gombrich has suggested that the
verses of the Vessantara-jātaka are no later than the third century bce.41 Warder
has used evidence from the metre of all of the Jātakapāli verses to argue that they
were composed at different times during the long period of the compilation of
the canon. He concludes that some are as early as the Suttanipāta, or even earlier,
38 Respectively Papañcasūdanī, Mahāvaṃsa, Manorathapūranī – adikaram, Early History,
pp. 30–31.
39 S. Paranavitana, Inscriptions of Ceylon, vol. 2/2 (Colombo, 2001), no. 171a
(pp. 270–271): Jātaka-baṇa[ka] Naka-teraha patagaḍi – ‘The step of the elder Nāga, the reciter
of Jātakas’. i am grateful to Peter Skilling for bringing this reference to my attention.
40 i use this term rather than simply Jātaka in order to make it clear that i am not
referring to the JA, which is often called Jātaka by scholars.
41 Richard F. Gombrich, ‘The Vessantara Jātaka, The Rāmāyaṇa and the Dasaratha
Jātaka’, Journal of the American Oriental Society, 105/3 (1985): 427–37, p. 428.

54 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
whereas many others probably belong to a particularly creative period in the
third century bce.42 Though Warder’s methods have been disputed, his findings
are consistent with the other (scanty) evidence we have for the compilatory
history of the Jātakapāli.
The Jātakapāli clearly belongs to the early period of Buddhism when texts
were part of an oral tradition. like parts of the Veda as well as several other
texts in the Pāli canon, the Jātakapāli was organised on a nipāta system, thus the
ekanipāta contains verses which individually relate to a jātaka, the dukanipāta
contains pairs of verses that relate to a jātaka, and so on. The final nipāta
contains long, verse-filled narratives. Within the first eight nipātas the stories
are organised into chapters, and these are either themed or named after the first
story contained within them. even where no theme is stated there appears to be
some sort of organisational principle, as simple as clustering all the stories about
birds together, or all the stories with verses on a similar structural pattern. in
this way it has a certain order, but this order is primarily for mnemonic reasons,
rather than being the conscious ordering of a literary composition.
it is difficult to establish a unifying principle for the verses in the Jātakapāli
that resulted in their ending up in the same text. arguably we should examine
them alongside their stories, in which case we could merely accept the traditional
explanation that the verses were placed in the text because of their presence in
a jātaka. i see no great reason to dispute this explanation, although it might be
worth adding the alternative that in some cases at least verses may have been
composed to accompany an existing story in order to facilitate its inclusion in
the collection. in either case one might then ask why the whole story was not
included in the Jātakapāli, but here we need only refer to the ākhyāna theory
discussed in Chapter 1, and suggest that although the stories came first, the
verses were fixed earlier than the stories, and thus it was only the verses that
acquired canonical status. The verses thus became the way of remembering a
story, itself too cumbersome and flexible to be remembered verbatim.
evidence that the jātaka was considered to be more than just the verse is
provided by the uddāna (‘summary’) verses. Such verses are found throughout
the Pāli canon, every 10 chapters or so, and contain a list of the contents that
have just preceded. The uddāna verses of the Jātakapāli occur every 10 jātakas, and
mention each jātaka separately, even where such a jātaka is only a single verse.
This is in contrast to the uddāna verses of other canonical texts, where chapters
are referred to, not their individual contents. evidence from the uddāna verses
cannot be relied upon too heavily, since their dating is always in question,43 but
they do add to a picture that associates the verses of the Jātakapāli inextricably
42 a.K. Warder, Pali Metre: A Contribution to the History of Indian Literature (london,
1967), p. 97 and the table on p. 225.
43 Such verses are found in some early manuscripts and also in the Sanskrit tradition,
but dating is nonetheless problematic. in any case, even if we can date the practice there

with the stories found later in the JA. However, the fact that the stories were
not fixed, and not included in the canon, implies that the verses deserve to be
examined separately, much as the Dhammapada is studied both in its own right
as well as with its commentary.
in the case of the Dhammapada and its commentary the two texts are each
well known but are used in different ways. in contrast, the Jātakapāli and JA are
considered inextricable, and this is usually explained as the result of the verses
being incomprehensible without the stories. in actual fact, it is not so much that
the verses are incomprehensible, as incomplete, since they often refer directly
to a specific example found in the prose story. This situation is well illustrated
by a cluster of jātakas about foolishness. The relevant verses of the Jātakapāli
(vv. 41–50) are as follows:
He who doesn’t listen to the counsel
of those friends who care about his welfare
laments, like mittaka hanging on to the goat’s foot. [41]
He who doesn’t listen to the counsel
of those friends who care about his welfare
dwells as if, having not heeded the pigeon’s words,
he is in the hand of the enemy. [42]
He who doesn’t listen to the counsel
of those friends who care about his welfare
is destroyed like Veḷuka’s father. [43]
an intelligent enemy is better,
not indeed a stupid friend.
Deaf-and-dumb, thinking ‘i would strike the mosquito’
the son cleaved his father’s head. [44]
Better a wise foe than a foolish friend.
See worthless Rohiṇi; having killed her mother she laments. [45]
assistance from a fool is not conducive to happiness:
a fool fails, just like the monkey gardener. [46]
assistance from a fool is not conducive to happiness:
a fool fails, as in the case of Koṇḍañña and his liquor. [47]
He who is misguided in his efforts to help, suffers:
the Cetans killed Vedabbha, and they all met with ruin. [48]
The luck of honoured stars misses the fool
luck is luck’s star; what can stars do? [49]
With a thousand fools, the sacrifice entreated by me
now indeed i will perform, for unrighteous people are plentiful. [50]44
also vary between the Burmese tradition (represented by the VRi edition, used here) and
other Southeast asian manuscript traditions.

in most of these verses the story is referred to as a support for what is being said,
but the moral itself is spelled out fully in the verse – often half the verse is the
moral, and the other half a reference to a specific set of events that demonstrate
this. one could compare what the Dhammapada has to say on the same subject, in
some verses from chapter five, Bālavagga (‘chapter on the fool’):
long is the night to one who is alert; long a mile to one who is tired;
long is saṃsāra for fools who do not comprehend the true doctrine. [60]
if a wanderer fails to meet his equal or better,
he should surely journey alone; there is no companionship with fools. [61]
Thinking ‘i have sons, i have wealth’ the fool suffers.
He has not even his own self, how then sons or wealth? [62]
The fool who is aware of his foolishness is therefore like a wise man.
But a fool who thinks he is wise is called a fool indeed. [63]
a fool who, as long as he lives, attends to a wise man,
he doesn’t know the doctrine, as the spoon doesn’t know the soup’s flavour. [64]
if a discerning person attends to a wise man even for a second,
he swiftly knows the doctrine, like the tongue knows the soup’s flavour. [65]45
in this case the verses make no reference to anything outside themselves, and
where examples are required they are included in the verses, and take the form
of images rather than stories. Thus the Dhammapada-aṭṭhakathā has an easier job
than the JA, since it only needs to supply the context for the utterance of the
verse by the Buddha, or a story which further backs up the content of the verses.
This comparison is not limited to the Dhammapada: von Hinüber contrasts the
Jātakapāli with all other canonical verse collections, since it is the only text which
is dependent upon its commentary.46
in addition to the tendency of the Jātakapāli to be reliant upon its
accompanying stories, the verses have rather a more worldly focus than texts
such as the Dhammapada. Though all the verses just quoted are about folly, the
idea of who is a fool is rather different in each text: in the Jātakapāli, a fool is
someone who doesn’t listen to his friends, or a gardener who pulls up plants to
check from their roots how much water they might require; in the Dhammapada
a fool is someone who doesn’t heed or understand Buddhist teachings. only in

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 57
Jātakapāli verses 49 and 50 do we see a difference in style (these verses are pretty
incomprehensible without the stories), accompanied by some Buddhist notions.
The story that accompanies verse 49 in the JA tells of a family who consult
the stars before allowing a marriage to take place, and by holding back on an
inauspicious day miss out on the bride.47 The next story tells of a prince who
makes a show of worshipping a tree-deity until he becomes king, then orders a
sacrifice to be made to the tree of 1,000 unrighteous people – such a declaration
unsurprisingly leads to better behaviour amongst his subjects.48 Both stories
(and indeed verses) contain a mocking portrayal of popular practices and to
that extent are Buddhist. The commentary of the latter story as we have it now
explicitly expounds some Buddhist principles, though there is no reason to
believe this was an original part of the story.
The style of the Jātakapāli changes further as the text proceeds, and a
particularly strong contrast is found in the later (longer) stories, where the
verses can form ballads or epics, and rely much less upon the accompanying
prose for their elucidation. There is even an isolated case of canonical prose (in
the Kuṇāla-jātaka49). Such variation makes the style of the Jātakapāli difficult to
pin down, and the ideology is similarly mixed, though generally – as we have
seen – concerned with secular affairs. The word ‘bodhisatta’ is present in the
verse samodhānas of three jātakas of the JA, but it is not clear whether or not
these verses were considered to be part of the Jātakapāli.50 The term ‘bodhisatta’
is not found in any other Jātakapāli verse, and the term ‘pāramī’ is found only in
two verses of the Vessantara-jātaka.51 in any case, since the Jātakapāli is ordered for
mnemonic reasons, there can be no preoccupation at this time with the jātakas
as chronological parts of the Buddha’s biography. The creation of the Jātakapāli
may have altered the jātaka genre to give it a set structure (canonical verse and
flexible prose accompaniment) and its own dedicated text and bhāṇaka tradition,
but the ideology found in the JA is not yet formalised.
Before moving on to look at the changes brought by the JA, it is worth pausing
to consider the relevance of the location of the Jātakapāli in the Khuddaka Nikāya.
This nikāya is later than the other four, and is considered to be a collection of
‘miscellaneous’ texts. Several of the Khuddaka texts are strongly associated with
the Buddha’s early disciples, rather than with the Buddha himself, for example

the Thera-therī-gāthā and Apadāna explicitly identify themselves as authored by
followers of the Buddha.52 it is thus possible that at the time of the Jātakapāli
the jātakas were not all considered to be buddhavacana; as we saw earlier,
such a notion is not conceptually necessary, though it becomes an important
characteristic of the jātaka genre by the time of the JA and Cariyāpiṭaka. The
Apadāna, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka also demonstrate a preoccupation with
buddhology (the doctrine of past and future buddhas and the development of
the bodhisatta ideal), whilst the Vimānavatthu and Petavatthu join the Apadāna
in focusing on kamma theory. Such preoccupations feature prominently in our
jātakas by the time of the JA, and will be further explored in later chapters.
observations about how well the Jātakapāli fits with the character of the
Khuddaka Nikāya as a whole are made more interesting by the history of confusion
– or explicit association – between the Khuddaka Nikāya and jātakas. abeynayake
notes the mistake made by Buddhaghosa and later scholars of identifying
the navaṅga with actual texts, rather than styles or genres, and suggests the
possibility that ‘some of the Khuddaka texts came into existence as a result of
an attempt to compile texts as examples for certain “limbs”’.53 as we noted
above, an early form of jātaka collection may well predate the Khuddaka Nikāya,
and so it may have formed a model for the composition of other texts for each
of the aṅgas. as a genre that was early noted, and thus legitimated, the jātaka
text was clearly an important part of the nikāya, and in some senses may even
have been considered synonymous with it. There is, for example, a passage in
the Samantapāsādikā where the decline of the dhamma is outlined, and the texts
disappear one by one. Here, the first four nikāyas are mentioned, along with the
fifth: ‘Jātakanikāya’.54
the JāTAKATTHAVAṇṇANā
The Jātakapāli forms the backbone to the better-known JA, which takes the form
of a commentary upon the canonical verses. We saw in the previous chapter
that the stories of the past are very variable in their form and content, but the
JA gives them a set structure – in the simplest form merely story of the present,
story of the past, and identification of the births – which lends the collection a
greater unity. The stories of the present also serve to authenticate the stories
of the past and place them in a biography of the Buddha, and this is enhanced
further by the addition of the biographical preface known as the Nidānakathā.
52 This is not unique to the Khuddaka Nikāya: some of the suttas of the four earlier
nikāyas are also spoken by close disciples of the Buddha.
53 oliver abeynayake, A Textual and Historical Analysis of the Khuddaka Nikāya (Colombo,
1984), p. 22.
54 abeynayake, Textual and Historical Analysis, p. 55.

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 59
This text, above all others, is the one which moulded the jātaka genre towards
biography.
although the JA as we have it now is a composition from around the fifth
century ce, and all parts of it except the verses are considered to be by the same
author, it is based upon much older material. There has been much debate about
what type of commentary to the Jātakapāli was present before that which now
forms part of the JA. Since most of the verses of the Jātakapāli are incomplete
without the stories that accompany them, we have to assume that there has
always been some sort of commentary, though it is not clear whether this was
preserved orally or in writing, in Sinhala or Pāli. Several jātakas are also paralleled
in other schools, indicating that they may be part of an old common tradition,
and yet others are found in other Theravāda canonical texts, whether in the
old sutta and vinaya texts examined above, or the late-canonical Cariyāpiṭaka,
which preserves some narrative details found in the prose of the JA as well as
several verses from the Jātakapāli. in addition, several jātakas appear in artistic
portrayals, such as the reliefs at Bhārhut, which date to the second century bce
and depict some jātakas that are only found in the JA, and some events that are
only found in the prose of this text.
Regarding the order in which the different parts were composed and compiled,
the most likely chronology is that the stories existed first (though they may not
always have been jātakas), then verses were composed to accompany them, then
the verses were preserved as part of the canon while the story itself continued to
develop, evolve, and in some cases become lost. Thus although the stories of the
past may be considered to be an old layer, we have to be wary of possible changes
and interpolations. even more problematic is the age of the stories of the present,
though again some are found also in other parts of the canon, and the practice of
framing all the Buddha’s discourses in a situation during his teaching career is
completely standard. The Nidānakathā causes yet more problems of dating, as we
will see below. However, for reasons which will become clear, and with my focus
firmly on the changing ideology of the jātaka genre, i am going to assume for the
time being that a text approximating the form of the JA was in existence before
the closing of the Theravāda scriptures. i am therefore going to treat the JA, at
least tentatively, as the second stage in the development of jātaka texts.
Towards a Unified Structure
one of the most striking features of the jātakas in the JA is their structure, and
this is clearly an attempt to unify a collection that contains a large variety of
stories. We might see this process as a way of establishing a well-defined jātaka
genre, and indeed the structure is imitated by the Cariyāpiṭaka, Apadāna, and
later Paññāsa-jātaka collections. i have already suggested that the atīta-vatthu in
some form was in existence before the verses, and that the verses were probably
composed to give some solidity to the ever-growing body of stories, as well as
to provide a means of remembering them. The samodhāna allows the crucial

60 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
link between present and past to be made, and the relevance of each jātaka to
be explained. The frequently repeated formulaic ending, telling of the sermon
on the noble truths and the attainments of the hearers, adds yet more unity to
the collection and by its repetitive nature suggests an oral tradition. The link
to a story of the present is the most complex factor in all this, and has various
ideological implications that demand our attention.
Though it is clear that the stories of the past must always have been integral
to the jātakas, the same is not necessarily true of the stories of the present. in
some cases a jātaka has clearly been associated with a specific story of the present
since early times, and these may therefore have a frame that contains echoes
of vinaya, biography or sutta. one example is the Gagga-jātaka ( JA 155) related
in the previous chapter. a colourful tale with no Buddhist moral (and indeed
every suggestion of being a non-Buddhist folktale), this jātaka is nonetheless
‘Buddhicised’ by the story of the present which is situated in a discussion about
vinaya rules also found in the Cullavagga. in it, a sermon is interrupted when
someone sneezes and everyone shouts ‘long life to you!’. The Buddha forbids this
as a superstition, but the people get upset when the monks do not respond when
they say ‘long life’, so he permits them to reply. The monks ask when this practice
began, and the Buddha relates the jātaka. in other examples the commentary
explicitly refers to another text for the story of the present, or states that the
story is taken from a particular sutta or vinaya passage.55
Whilst in the case of the earliest jātakas (such as those found in sutta and
vinaya), one might assume that the stories of the present at least might relate the
actual events surrounding the telling of the stories, this cannot be said of those
cases where non-Buddhist stories have been absorbed and thus have no story of
the present accompanying them. in such cases the compiler has done the best he
can with limited resources, and consequently the present story often seems to
have been invented in order to keep to the set structure, in many cases repeating
exactly the events of the past (as in the case of the monkey gardeners, discussed
in Chapter 1). Nonetheless, whatever means have been used to acquire a frame
story for each jātaka, the result is that all the stories are given a specific location
in the time and place of the Buddha. Furthermore, if a jātaka is only considered
such if narrated by the Buddha, then any story incorporated in this way must be
by definition buddhavacana.
in the JA, the location in which the Buddha tells a jātaka is more often than
not the Jeta grove – in fact Feer counted 410 stories where this is the case.56 most
55 For example JA 309 Chavaka-jātaka, which gives only a brief summary of the
situation in the present and refers the reader to the vinaya for the full story. in the vinaya
version, as we saw in the discussion above, the story is not explicitly identified as a
jātaka.
56 C.a.F. Rhys-Davids, Buddhist Birth Stories (london, 1880), p. 245. as Peris notes,
openings such as ‘this story was told by the master at Jetavana’ are just as formulaic as
‘when Brahmadatta was ruling in Benares’ (merlin Peris, Greek Story Motifs in the Jatakas

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 61
often the audience for the telling of the jātakas consists of either members of
the saṅgha or prominent laypeople. The setting is thus explicitly one of Buddhist
instruction, and this compensates somewhat for the lack of explicit Buddhist
content in many of the jātaka stories. The setting is also both historically and
geographically specific, and can be verified by visiting the places in which the
Buddha taught; the location of the storytelling is thus accessible, at least in
theory. The setting of all of the stories is in one region and one time period,
in contrast to the cosmic cycles traversed in the jātakas, and made explicit in
related texts that outline the patterns of the births of the previous buddhas. The
very specific nature of the setting of the stories thus allows the flexibility to
remain within the individual jātakas, whilst authenticating them and drawing
them into a verified and accessible Buddhist milieu.
The set structure established in the JA allowed the absorption of many
stories into this specifically Buddhist genre. almost any story could become a
jātaka with the simple association of one character (or even a totally silent and
uninvolved witness) with the Bodhisatta, and the placing of the story within the
teaching career of the Buddha. The result is that this rather motley collection of
predominantly secular stories is firmly established as Buddhist: they are stories
told by the Buddha, as part of his dhamma, about his time as Bodhisatta.
The Nidānakathā: Chronology and Biography
The addition of a long biographical preface known as the Nidānakathā to the JA
emphasises the idea that jātakas are part of the Buddha’s life story. The dating
of this portion of the JA is uncertain, though it cannot belong to the time of the
Jātakapāli, since it in part forms a commentary on the Buddhavaṃsa, which is one
of the latest additions to the canon. To make matters more complicated, it is
not merely a case of ascertaining a date for the composition of the Nidānakathā,
but also whether it has always been associated with the jātaka collection. For
(Colombo, 2004), pp. 185–6). Such lines are found with equal frequency in the stories of
present and past respectively, and remind us to treat the present episodes as part of the
jātaka narrative. We might also note that the Mūlasarvāstivāda vinaya text examined by
Schopen displays a natural bias towards setting all discourses in Sāvatthi, where the Jeta
Grove is located, just as it encourages the setting of all stories of the past in the reign of
Brahmadatta: ‘those who forget the name of the place, etc., must declare it was one or
another of the six great cities, or somewhere where the Tathāgata stayed many times.
if he forgets the name of the king, he must declare it was Prasenajit; if the name of the
householder, that it was anāthapiṇḍada … of the place of a story of the past, that it was
Vāraṇāsī, of the king, that it was Brahmadatta’ (Gregory Schopen, ‘if You Can’t Remember,
How to make it Up: Some monastic Rules for Redacting Canonical Texts’, in Petre Kieffer-
Pülz and Jens-Uwe Hartmann (eds), Bauddhavidyāsudhākara˙: Studies in Honour of Heinz
Bechert on the Occasion of His 65th Birthday (Swisttal-odendorf, 1997), pp. 571–82, p. 575).
Schopen suggests that the JA must have been guided by a similar injunction (p. 578), and
the statistics certainly support this view.

62 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
example, the text is found in a fourteenth-century Tibetan translation as a
separate work ascribed explicitly to the Buddha, yet still called ‘Jātaka-nidāna’
(Tib sKyes pa rabs kyi gleṅ gzi).57 it is not immediately clear why this biography
became associated with the jātaka collection, though Gaffney suggests: ‘That it
is set out in the form of an introduction to the Jātaka collection has perhaps
aided its acceptance by the Pāli tradition as an ancient and reliable source of
biographical material on the Buddha’s life.’58 although i find Gaffney’s argument
that the jātaka collection legitimated the Nidānakathā in some way quite plausible,
i believe that the legitimation may have worked in the opposite direction too:
attaching a biography of the Buddha to the collection legitimated the jātakas
by providing a frame for their telling in the career of the Buddha and their
occurrence in the long biography of the Bodhisatta.
The Nidānakathā is divided into three parts: the dūre-nidāna (distant epoch),
avidūre-nidāna (intermediate epoch) and santike-nidāna (the proximate epoch),
relating respectively: the life of the Bodhisatta in the time of previous buddhas;
the life of the Bodhisatta in the Tusita heaven and in his final life before
awakening; and the life of the Buddha up to the donation of the Jeta grove. Thus
this biographical narrative begins with the vow of the Bodhisatta (as Sumedha)
to become a buddha, which is made in the presence of the Buddha Dīpaṅkara.
The narration continues through the lives of the 24 previous buddhas in a text
based heavily on the Buddhavaṃsa. Then follows the life of the historical Buddha
from his time in the Tusita heaven and in his final birth, up to the time when
anāthapiṇḍika donates the Jeta grove to the saṅgha. Since many of his years of
teaching take place in the Jeta grove, and it is during these years that the jātaka
stories are said to be related, the Nidānakathā acts as an extension to the ‘stories
of the present’. However, the ordering of the jātakas is not chronological in terms
of present or past lives, since the nipāta system of increasing number of verses
is the organisational principle.59 There is, therefore, a disjunction between the
style of the Nidānakathā and the rest of the text.
The structure of the Nidānakathā has parallels in other biographical texts, such
as the Mahāvastu and Lalitavistara. Such texts are sometimes called ‘incomplete
biographies’60 as unlike some biographies they do not continue to the end of
57 Peter Skilling, ‘Theravādin literature in Tibetan Translation’, Journal of the Pali
Text Society, 19 (1993): 69–201, p. 106ff.
58 Sean Gaffney, ‘The Pāli Nidānakathā and its Tibetan Translation: its Textual
Precursors and associated literature’ in Tadeusz Skorupski (ed.), The Buddhist Forum, Vol.
IV (london, 1996), pp. 75–91, p. 89.
59 as proof that the ordering is in no way chronological we might mention that JA
95 is said to have been told by the Buddha as he lay on his death-bed, whereas JA 547 is
told during the Buddha’s visit to his home country; the past events of JA 539 took place
when the human life-span was 10,000 years, but most are said to have taken place during
Brahmadatta’s reign, when the life-span was much the same as now.
60 See for example J.S. Strong, The Buddha: A Short Biography (oxford, 2006), p. 5.

The Creation of a Jātaka Genre 63
the Buddha’s life, or to the dissolution of the relics and disappearance of the
dhamma, which later becomes identified as the very final end of the Buddha’s
biography. Frauwallner sees the origin of such biographies in the old Skandhaka
text of the original vinaya, which was related not in the form of a list of rules,
but as a historical account framed by the Buddha’s early life at the beginning
and his death and the subsequent councils at the end. The biographical preface,
he argues, was eventually detached and became a separate text, and in some
schools (including Theravāda) it was later appended to the jātaka collection.61
The texts vary slightly in where they end their narrative: the Nidānakathā
stops at the donation of the Jeta grove, the Mahāvastu ends at the formation of
the saṅgha, and the Lalitavistara ends at the first sermon. as an alternative to
Frauwallner’s argument i would like to suggest that these variations might be
a sign that the texts exemplify an early phase of biography writing, when the
aim was to give context and legitimation to the Buddha’s teachings (whether
vinaya or sutta). The Mahāvastu calls itself a vinaya text, and so may preserve the
form it had when appended to a set of vinaya rules, as Frauwallner argues. Thus
the biographical preface need only continue until the formation of the saṅgha,
when the monastic regulations began to be formed. The Lalitavistara is said to be
sūtra, and thus the biography stops at the beginning of the composition of the
Buddha’s discourses. in the JA many of the jātakas (including the first one) are
said to be told by the Buddha in the Jeta grove at the request of anāthapiṇḍika,
so the biography of the Nidānakathā stops there, leaving us in the presence of the
Buddha, awaiting the tales of his previous births.
as well as providing a context for the telling of the jātakas, the Nidānakathā
helps to reconcile the historical biographical aspect of the individual jātakas
relating to the one historical Buddha, with the repetitive nature of time and
experience, by putting the Buddha into a broader cycle. as Dimock comments
in relation to religious biography in india, ‘if the life of an individual has cosmic
significance, its particularities are unimportant, and if the particularities are
important, the life is not worth writing’.62 By prefacing the jātaka collection with
a biography of the 24 previous buddhas, the Nidānakathā changes the time-frame
of the narrative, setting the historical Buddha in a larger context. in addition
to providing a broader context than a single person (in his many lives), the
61 e. Frauwallner, The Earliest Vinaya and the Beginnings of Buddhist Literature (Rome,
1956), p. 50ff. Frauwallner’s argument has obvious implications for the dating of the
Nidānakathā, and places it somewhat earlier than we otherwise have evidence for, since he
argues that its precursor – the Skandhaka – dates to around the third century bce. However,
even if we could verify this theory, tracing the changes in the text, and in particular
ascertaining at what stage it was appended to the jātaka collection, is still an impossible
task.
62 edward C. Dimock, ‘Religious Biography in india: The “Nectar of the acts” of
