64 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Nidānakathā thus reinforces the idea that the JA should be seen as a biography
that is not unique. a consequence of this is that people are able to see themselves
as having the possibility of a similar biography, and of thereby being part of this
context. i will be returning to this idea later.
CoNClUSioN
i have now traced the history of jātaka texts from the early suttas through to the
JA. as i have noted, the earliest past-birth stories of the Buddha, preserved in
sutta texts, did not demonstrate the perfections acquired by the Bodhisatta, but
rather held him up in unfavourable comparison with the Buddha. even when the
stories were organised into a collection, biography was not a preoccupation, and
the ordering principle was purely mnemonic. only later, as the JA imposed a set
structure and the Nidānakathā was prefaced to it, did jātakas begin to demonstrate
the long life of the Buddha. even as this became the accepted rationale for the
text, the individual jātakas of the JA retained their various styles and uses, so
that biography remains only one of many preoccupations, albeit the one most
vigorously promoted in the canonical and early commentarial literature. To
what extent this idea of jātakas affected later texts and communities is the focus
of the next chapter.

Chapter 4
Generic Conventions embraced
and ignored
in the previous chapter we witnessed the birth of a genre. By the time the
Jātakatthavaṇṇanā ( JA) acquired its many layers a jātaka was clearly defined as a
story told by the Buddha that in some way illustrated the actions he undertook
whilst on the long path to buddhahood. Jātakas were thus associated with
biography and the gradual acquisition of the perfections by the Bodhisatta, and
were given a standardised structure that confirmed their status as buddhavacana.
That this definition emerged before the last texts were incorporated into the
scriptures is clear from its adoption in the late-canonical Cariyāpiṭaka, discussed
below. However, the later jātaka texts retain the biographical preoccupation
to varying degrees, and suggest that the real power of jātakas remains their
flexibility and variety. These qualities are still visible in present-day jātaka texts,
which at times seem totally unaware of the association between jātakas and the
Bodhisatta.
The purpose of this chapter is to examine to what extent the biographical
preoccupation of jātaka stories is preserved in texts created after the time of the
JA. To survey all the uses of jātakas in Theravāda texts through to the present
day would of course be an impossible task, so instead i will focus upon four
key areas: First i will examine the Cariyāpiṭaka, which adopts the biographical
definition of the JA along with several of its stories, and tightens the focus upon
the perfections, but has limited success as a narrative text. Second, i will discuss
two early post-canonical texts (Milindapañha and Visuddhimagga) in which jātaka
stories are explored according to the understanding that they are part of the
Buddha’s biography. Third, i will demonstrate how later jātaka texts (Mahājātaka
and Paññāsajātaka) develop and stretch the JA’s definition of jātakas in new ways.
Finally, i will bring the study up to date by exploring the present-day place of
jātaka texts in a single Theravāda country, namely Sri lanka. This survey of
attitudes towards the jātaka genre since the time of the JA suggests that there
is some tension between the presentation of jātakas in monastically preserved
texts such as scripture and treatises, and the way the popular stories were and
are interacted with in everyday life. This chapter will therefore highlight both
the pervasive influence, and the ultimate limitations, of the JA’s defintion of the
jātaka genre.

66 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
JĀTaKaS aND PeRFeCTioNS: CARIyāPIṭAKA
also known as the Buddhāpadāniyaṃ, the Cariyāpiṭaka forms the fifteenth section
of the Khuddaka Nikāya, and is considered to be one of the latest additions to the
Theravāda scriptures. it contains 35 jātakas relating explicitly to the acquisition
of the pāramitās or perfections, which are usually enumerated as a list of 10:
giving (dāna), morality (sīla), renunciation (nekkhamma), wisdom ( paññā), energy
(viriya), forbearance (khanti), truthfulness (sacca), determination (adhiṭṭhāna),
loving kindness (mettā) and equanimity (upekkhā). The first 10 stories of the
Cariyāpiṭaka relate to dāna, the second 10 to sīla, and the final 15 to nekkhamma
(5), adhiṭṭhāna (1), sacca (6), mettā (2) and upekkhā (1). There are no stories
referring explicitly to paññā, viriya or khanti, although some stories contain
implicit references. Some scholars have argued that there are missing stories,
and this is supported by confusion between references in the Nidānakathā to
Cariyāpiṭaka stories and those contained in the text as we now have it, though
i will later argue for an alternative explanation for these apparent anomalies.
Thirty-two of the jātakas in the Cariyāpiṭaka have parallels in the JA, one other is
in the Dīgha Nikāya, and one in the Majjhima Nikāya. The Cariyāpiṭaka extracted
the stories from these sources and retold them for its own purposes. The main
stylistic change is that they are told relatively concisely, entirely in verse, and in
the first person.
The purpose of the Cariyāpiṭaka is very clear: to glorify the Bodhisatta’s
perfection. Some of the stories chosen are unsurprising: Vessantara for generosity,
the story of the young quail’s act of truth for truthfulness, and so on. Some are
a little more surprising, particularly the Kapirājacariyaṃ (iii.7), the story of the
monkey and the crocodile, which is told in the section demonstrating sacca. in
the parallel stories of the JA (57. Vānarinda-jātaka; 208. Suṃsumāra-jātaka; 342.
Vānara-jātaka) the monkey (the Bodhisatta) tells a lie to the crocodile in order
to outwit him. in the Cariyāpiṭaka we are told: ‘No lie was spoken to him, i acted
according to my word.’1 This is clearly a deliberate attempt to rewrite the stories
already popular in the JA in order to give them a cleaner ethic. it is possible
that by this time the idea that the Bodhisatta cannot lie (declared in the JA and
examined in Chapter 2) had become current, yet to put this problematic tale into
a chapter about truthfulness seems courageous to say the least. The message is
clear: jātakas show the Bodhisatta being good, whatever the Jātakapāli and JA
might suggest to the contrary.2

Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka (london, 1974), p. 30, verse 310.

allows his wife to be taken away against her will, and claims he wouldn’t break his sīla

Further comparison between the Cariyāpiṭaka and Jātakapāli may be made
from the uddāna verses. For example, the verse that concludes the ‘atthakāma
vagga’ of the Jātakapāli (examined in Chapter 3 in comparison to verses in the
Dhammapada) runs:
Here the ten are mittaka, the mother’s excellent pigeon,
then Bamboo, Deaf-and-Dumb, Rohiṇī,
and also the monkey, the liquor, and the Cetans,
then the stars and the excellent sacrifice.3
The titles here mentioned are not those given as titles in the text itself. This
might suggest that in fact the titles now found in editions of the Jātakapāli are
imposed from the JA. However, the uddāna verses are in any case not so much
lists of titles, as lists of contents, and so no agreement should necessarily be
expected.4 There is also no mention of the Bodhisatta, and the characters in the
story are not exclusively those identified with him. in the JA, the same chapter
has the following uddāna:
The ten are losakatissa, the pigeon, Bamboo and also the mosquito
Rohiṇī, the disgrace of the garden, the liquor, innocent Vedabba
astrology, and the fool.5
Here it is the titles of the jātakas that are given, but still there is no mention
of the Bodhisatta (though some Jātakapāli and JA uddānas do mention that the
stories are jātakas6). in contrast, the first chapter of the Cariyāpiṭaka ends:
The brahmin akitti, Saṅkha, the Kuru king Dhanañcaya;
King mahāsudassana, the brahmin mahāgovinda;
Nimi, and Prince Canda, Sivi, Vessantara, the hare;
i was the one then who gave those most excellent gifts.
These were the requisites of dāna, these the perfections of dāna;
Having given my life to a supplicant, i fulfilled this perfection.
buffalo tormented by a monkey: he refuses to retaliate, saying that some other, less moral
creature is sure to kill the monkey eventually, thereby freeing him from both present and

Seeing one approaching for alms, i abandoned my own self;
There is no equal to me in dāna; these were my perfections of dāna.7
Here, though the structure (at least initially) is similar to both the Jātakapāli and
JA, the stories are referred to through the character of the Bodhisatta; indeed, it
is not so much the stories being referred to as the lives of the Buddha. if the first
two verses don’t convince one of the reason these stories have just been related,
the latter two spell it out in full. in addition, as i recently noted, the jātakas of
the JA are related in the third person whereas those in the Cariyāpiṭaka are in the
first person; this is true also for the concluding verses. as far as i am aware, this
is the only incidence of first-person uddāna verses in the Pāli canon.
The Cariyāpiṭaka is a very neat text, in terms of both structure and didactic
method, with a great unity and few problems with interpretation. it may even
have been composed explicitly for teaching purposes, and it demonstrates clearly
what aspects of the Bodhisatta’s behaviour should be admired and emulated, and
inspires faith in the Buddha’s teaching. in contrast to the JA, it is concise, clear
and relatively morally unambiguous, and is accepted as part of the Khuddaka
Nikāya. However, although the JA is unwieldy, inconsistent, and at times morally
problematic, as well as being accepted as only part-canonical, it seems to have
always been more popular than the Cariyāpiṭaka. i suggest there are several
reasons why the Cariyāpiṭaka was unable to supersede the JA in popularity. To
begin with, the JA was already well established as a text before the composition
of the Cariyāpiṭaka, and indeed the latter text mostly extracted stories from
the former, adding very little. although the JA is officially commentarial, such
distinctions seem to primarily concern scholars rather than Buddhists, and in
any case much of it appears to be older than the Cariyāpiṭaka, and was clearly
accorded a quasi-canonical status.
Perhaps the most serious reason for the Cariyāpiṭaka’s comparative failure,
however, is its neatness, since this allowed no flexibility in use. The JA’s motley
collection of stories provided (and continues to provide) an almost endless
source of material for preaching, illustration, education and entertainment. The
biographical focus is found only in the framing of the JA, thus anchoring and
unifying the text without interfering with the uses and types of its constituent
parts. Nor does the framing interfere with the plain fun of the stories, which
when retold in the Cariyāpiṭaka seem stilted and unappealing. The Cariyāpiṭaka’s
exclusive focus upon the praiseworthy parts of the narrative results in the loss
of narrative detail or whole portions of story. We are left only with verses of
praise, full of allusions to stories that we know are much more vibrantly told
in the JA. Thus although the early JA reshaped the jātaka genre with a strong
biographical focus, a text composed entirely according to this rationale has very
limited appeal.
7 Trans. of the uddāna verses for the Cariyāpiṭaka ‘Dānapāramitā’ chapter from the
VRi edition.

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 69
JĀTaKaS aND BoDHiSaTTa: MILINDAPAñHA and VISUDDHIMAGGA
The Cariyāpiṭaka demonstrates that the definition of jātakas established by the
JA was influential before the end of the period in which the scriptures were
compiled. The continued association of jātaka stories with the person of the
Bodhisatta after this time can be seen in two further texts that, though not
canonical, are very highly regarded within Theravāda Buddhism. each text is
compilatory in some way, and thus they together provide good evidence for
the ways in which jātakas may have been viewed and used during the centuries
leading up to the commentarial period.
The Milindapañha (‘The Questions of King milinda’) is a text composed in north
india but later translated into Pāli and given very high status within Theravāda
countries. it consists of a conversation between the Bactrian King milinda and
the monk Nāgasena, who settles each of the king’s questions in turn.8 on six
occasions individual jātaka stories form the basis of queries put by the king to
Nāgasena, and these can be summarised as follows (with the titles as found in
Trenckner’s edition):9
i, 4, Hetu bhūmicalassa, The Cause of the earthquake: There are said to be only
eight causes of earthquakes, so why did the earth also quake when Vessantara
gave his gift? answer: The Bodhisatta’s great generosity was exceptional and
therefore doesn’t count amongst the eight standard causes.
i, 5, Sivirāja, King Sivi: How did King Sivi get divine eyes after giving away his
own, if nothing arises without a cause? answer: The power of his statement of
truth was the cause.
iV, 6, Mettāya ānisaṃsā, The Good Result of loving Kindness: The Buddha said
that a person who cultivates loving kindness cannot be harmed, so how can
it be that Sāma was killed despite his cultivation of loving kindness? answer:
Sāma lapsed in his loving kindness at the moment he was shot.
V, 5, Lomakassapajāti, The Birth as lomakassapa: The Buddha is said not to have
harmed even in past births, so how is it possible that as lomasa Kassapa he
made a huge animal sacrifice? answer: The Bodhisatta was maddened with
lust, and madmen are not responsible for their actions.
8 although the king is identified with King menandros, who dates from the second
century bce, the text is not historical in nature but reads as a carefully constructed religious
dialogue. The dating of the Milindapañha is thus very problematic, and to complicate
matters the text appears to have several sections of different ages. For a full discussion
of the possibilities see oskar von Hinüber, A Handbook of Pāli Literature (Berlin, 1996),
pp. 82–6.
9 V. Trenckner (ed.), The Milindapañho (london, 1962).

70 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
V, 6, Jotipālajāti, The Birth as Jotipāla: How is it possible that when born as
the elephant Chaddanta the Bodhisatta respected the robe of a recluse yet as
Jotipāla he mocked and reviled Kassapa Buddha? answer: it was the fault of
his birth in a family of unbelievers, for he was tainted by this as cold water is
warmed by contact with fire.
Viii, 1, Sabbe Bodhisattā puttadāran denti, Do all Bodhisattas Give away
Their Wives and Children?: How can giving that causes harm to others, as
Vessantara’s gift harmed his children, still reap positive fruits? Shouldn’t he
have given himself instead? answer: Vessantara’s giving led to great renown. if
asked for his wife and children he had to give them – he could not give himself
instead. He knew the king would redeem the children and that they would
therefore not remain slaves.
These dilemmas show that the actions of the Bodhisatta in his jātaka stories were
taken seriously, and on occasion needed to be defended or explained in the same
way as the Buddha’s actions. The continuity between Bodhisatta and Buddha,
and the nature of jātakas as demonstrating the Bodhisatta’s path to buddhahood,
are assumed without question.
The other main occurrence of jātakas in the Milindapañha is in Meṇḍakapañha
iV, 7: Devadattassajātiyo – ‘The Births of Devadatta’. Here it is Devadatta, the
Buddha’s troublemaking cousin, who is the focus, in a long question about
whether or not good and bad actions result in the same fruit. The king points out
that in many previous births (he gives 16 examples) Devadatta, though behaving
badly, occupies a higher status than the Bodhisatta, and in some other cases (he
cites six) the two are on a par. Thus, he argues, good and evil actions must lead
to equal results. The majority of the examples cited by the king are found in the
JA. as Nāgasena points out, however, Devadatta did a variety of bad and good
things, and his karmic fruit was a lot more complicated and long lived than this
small number of births. once again the truth of the jātakas is not in question,
nor is the presence of the Bodhisatta. However, the fact that they appear not to
glorify the Bodhisatta over his obviously inferior cousin is deemed a problem
with wide reaching implications for ethical practice, since they form a challenge
to the doctrine of kamma.
an unquestioning acceptance that jātakas illustrate the Buddha’s long path to
buddhahood is also found in Buddhaghosa’s fifth-century treatise Visuddhimagga
(‘The Path of Purification’), which draws on a wide range of sources including
canonical texts, the old aṭṭhakathā material and the different bhāṇaka traditions
to present a demonstration of the Buddhist path. These varied sources include
jātaka stories, in the chapter on the Brahmavihāra (ix 25–35). Here Buddhaghosa
recommends certain jātakas that demonstrate the Bodhisatta’s forbearance
as meditation subjects for those wishing to eliminate resentment in order to
develop mettā:

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 71
Here is the way of reviewing it: ‘Now you who have gone forth, is it not
a fact that when your master was a Bodhisatta before discovering full
enlightenment, while he was still engaged in fulfilling the perfections during
the four incalculable ages and a hundred thousand aeons, he did not allow hate
to corrupt his mind even when his enemies tried to murder him on various
occasions? For example, in the Sīlavant Birth Story …’10
Buddhaghosa then proceeds to cite various examples, quoting from the Jātakapāli
and Cariyāpiṭaka where appropriate, and summarising the stories in prose.
Both of these texts show that the association of the jātakas with the positive
example of the Bodhisatta was prominent before the commentarial period. it was
an association that came with problems, but these were dealt with by the great
minds such as Nāgasena or his creator/author, leaving the Bodhisatta free to be
an example to all Buddhists. The definition created by the JA and embraced by
the Cariyāpiṭaka thus exerted a significant influence over Theravāda scholastics
during their discussion of jātaka stories.
STReTCHiNG THe BioGRaPHY: MAHāJāTAKA and PAññāSAJāTAKA
Certain jātakas of the JA have always been more popular than others, as is
evidenced in both textual and art-historical sources. in particular, the final 10
jātaka stories have long been considered as having greater importance than the
rest of the text, and may even have had their own specialist reciters.11 These 10
jātakas together are known in Southeast asia as Mahānipātajātaka or Dasajātaka
and contain the Mūgapakkha, Mahājanaka, Sāma, Nimi, Bhūridatta, Khaṇḍahāla,
Nārada, Vidhura, Ummagga and Vessantara jātakas.12 These stories also circulate
individually, often with their own commentary and/or translation into a
vernacular. in Sri lanka the 10 stories minus the last two circulated together in a
text known as Aṭajātaka, although Nevill, in his catalogue entry for the Aṭajātaka
10 Bhikkhu Ñāṇamoli (trans.), The Path of Purification (Visuddhimagga) by Bhadantācariya
Buddhaghosa (5th edn, Kandy, 1991), pp. 294–5 followed by examples and quotations
from the Jātakapāli and Cariyāpiṭaka on p. 295ff. Buddhaghosa’s use of jātakas before the
composition of the JA we have now, demonstrates that some form of commentary was in
circulation, but was not fixed or established enough to quote directly from (he quotes
only from verses).
11 e.W. adikaram, Early History of Buddhism in Ceylon (Dehiwala, Sri lanka, 1946),
pp. 30–31 refers to a story about a Mahā-jātaka-bhāṇaka in the Manorathapūranī, and
discusses the possibility that this refers to a separate bhāṇaka tradition for the Mahājātaka
(either just the Vessantara-jātaka, or the jātakas of the Mahānipāta).

72 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Sannaya, suggests that the absence of the last two is merely because they were
treated separately.13
The rise in importance of these jātakas appears to be related to the popular
idea that the last jātakas of the JA are chronologically last. in other words it is
commonly believed that the last 10 stories of the JA relate the Bodhisatta’s last
births before he attained buddhahood, and thus his final attainment of the 10
perfections, each of which is associated with a story. in the Mūgapakkha-jātaka
( JA 538), Prince Temiya (the Bodhisatta) pretends to be a dumb cripple in order
to avoid accruing the bad kamma that inevitably accompanies kingship. about
to be killed, he reveals himself and renounces, followed by his father, and this
story is therefore associated with the perfection of renunciation (nekkhamma). in
the Mahājanaka-jātaka ( JA 539), Janaka (the Bodhisatta) is shipwrecked and has
various adventures, demonstrating his acquisition of the perfection of energy
(viriya). The Sāma-jātaka ( JA 540) tells of how a blind ascetic couple are looked
after by their son Sāma (the Bodhisatta), who is acquiring the perfection of
loving kindness (mettā). in the Nimi-jātaka ( JA 541) King Nimi (the Bodhisatta)
finds his first grey hair and wishes to renounce. after a tour of the heavens
and hells he returns to his home and carries out his resolve (adhiṭṭhāna). in
the Khaṇḍahāla-jātaka ( JA 542) Prince Canda (the Bodhisatta) has perfected his
forbearance (khanti) to such an extent that he looks after his father despite the
latter’s attempts to kill him. in the Bhūridatta-jātaka ( JA 543) a nāga prince called
Bhūridatta (the Bodhisatta) is captured by a snake charmer, and demonstrates
the perfection of his virtue (sīla) by refusing to harm his captor. in the
Mahānāradakassapa-jātaka ( JA 544) Nārada (the Bodhisatta) descends from the
brahmā realm to persuade a king of his folly, demonstrating along the way his
perfection of equanimity (upekkhā). in the Vidhurapaṇḍita-jātaka ( JA 545) a sage
called Vidhura (the Bodhisatta) is devoted to telling the truth (sacca), and has
various adventures as a result. in the Mahā-Ummagga-jātaka ( JA 546) the wise
mahosadha (the Bodhisatta) solves various riddles and outwits his competitors,
demonstrating his perfection of wisdom ( paññā). in the Vessantara-jātaka ( JA 547)
Prince Vessantara (the Bodhisatta) gives great gifts, including the royal elephant,
his children and his wife, thereby perfecting his generosity (dāna).
The belief that the Bodhisatta’s birth as Vessantara in the very final jātaka
of the JA represents his antepenultimate birth,14 and thus the height of his
perfection, has been particularly important in Theravāda culture. it has led to
the Vessantara-jātaka becoming undeniably the most popular jātaka throughout
Southeast asia, to the extent that Gombrich has suggested that Buddhists in such
countries are as likely to be able to recount this story as the Buddha’s own life
13 K.D. Somadasa, Catalogue of the Hugh Nevill Collection of Sinhalese Manuscripts in the
British Library, vol. 1 (london, 1987), p. 112.
14 His penultimate birth is in the Tusita heaven.

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 73
story.15 it is the focus of annual chanting rituals in Thailand, Cambodia, laos and
the Shan States, and is the most popularly depicted jātaka in temple illustrations,
as well as being used in rituals related to marriage, death, renunciation and the
consecration of a new Buddha image.
The popularity and priority of the Vessantara-jātaka has puzzled scholars,
as the story is not about the perfection considered the highest, nor indeed the
highest form of the perfection. in addition, the story relates extreme giving, to
a socially unacceptable extent, since the gifts include Vessantara’s own children
and wife; as early as the Milindapañha Buddhists have had to justify this level of
generosity. in any case, in the account of the Buddha’s defeat of māra on the
night of his enlightenment, it is not Vessantara’s gift of his wife and children
that is cited as evidence for the Buddha’s perfection of generosity, but his seven-
hundredfold gift of alms made at the beginning of the story;16 it is also this
latter gift that causes an earthquake in the account of the jātaka. Thus, despite
its current popularity, many Buddhists and Buddhologists alike have expressed
discomfort about the story’s content and status.17
as Peter Skilling observed in his paper ‘The antepenultimate Birth of the
Śākyamuni’,18 there are at least two traditions about the last-but-one birth of the
Buddha. Whereas the Theravāda tradition insists that the Buddha’s last human
birth was as Vessantara, in the North indian schools this place is given to either
Jyotipāla or Uttara māṇava.19 Skilling raised the question of where the Theravāda
tradition originated; it seems likely, as was suggested at the conference, that it
was the result of the position of the story at the very end of the JA, a text not
shared with the other Buddhist schools. However, the position of the Vessantara-
jātaka in the JA – as in the Jātakapāli – is related merely to the number of verses
contained within it. as i showed in the previous chapter, ideas of chronology
and biography were introduced to the collection later, after the order of the

stories was fixed. if, therefore, the popularity of the Vessantara-jātaka is due to

The idea that the Vessantara-jātaka demonstrates the perfection of dāna and
comes at the end of the Bodhisatta’s long journey, and that the other jātakas of
the Mahānipāta illustrate the culmination of each of the other nine perfections,
has become particularly prevalent in Southeast asia. The titles of these stories
in Southeast asia differ from Fausbøll’s edition, which was created from Sri
lankan manuscripts. as well as slight changes in ordering,20 Southeast asian
manuscripts and inscriptions preserve four significant changes of title, which
reflect a growing focus upon the character of the Bodhisatta:21
Mūgapakkha-jātaka becomes Temiya-jātaka;
Khaṇḍahāla-jātaka becomes Candakumāra-jātaka;
Mahānāradakassapa-jātaka becomes Nārada-jātaka;
Mahā-ummagga-jātaka becomes Mahosadha-jātaka.
in the first case, the change is at first glance insignificant, since Temiya is
merely an alternative name for the character known as mūgapakkha, who is the
Bodhisatta. However, such an alternative title might indicate a certain reluctance
to refer to the Bodhisatta as ‘Dumb-cripple’, the meaning of mūgapakkha.

which are ordered mahosadha, Candakumāra, Bhūridatta, Nārada, Vidhura at Pagan, and
the same but with Candakumāra after Bhūridatta in manuscript collections. See G.H. luce,

a mnemonic that will be examined in Chapter 7, agrees with the Burmese manuscripts.

(omitting mahosadha and Vessantara, which were reputedly treated separately) held in

in the second case the change is more obvious: the story ceases to be named

Bodhisatta.

Clearly the association of these 10 stories with the 10 perfections post-dates both

between the jātakas and the perfections, painting for the first time a coherent
picture of the final stages of the Bodhisatta’s career.

76 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Whilst the Mahājātaka developed the final stages of the Bodhisatta’s path,
other stories composed in mainland Southeast asia further extended the
Bodhisatta’s biography, both outwards (by incorporating stories not found in
the JA) and backwards (by telling stories said to predate those found in the JA).
These collections of ‘non-classical’ (or ‘apocryphal’) jātakas are known by the
general title Paññāsajātaka, or ‘50 jātakas’.22 This should be considered a genre
rather than a text, for there are many different collections under this title, in
both Pāli and vernaculars, most of which do not contain exactly 50 stories.23 The
Pāli version from Burma, known as the Zimmé Paṇṇāsa, is the only one to be found
neatly arranged in five sections of 10 stories each; this is the version published
by the Pāli Text Society.24 as Jaini notes, the jātakas in this text copy the structure
of the JA, and are heavily preoccupied with Buddhist ideals, and thus they ‘were
acceptable as complements to the canonical Jātakas’.25 The vernacular and
Siamese Pāli traditions preserve many more motley collections, and in any case
Skilling comments that the stories contained in the Paññāsajātaka collections
were treated as individual stories, and used and retold as such, not as extracts
from a collection of 50.26
Despite their variety, the Paññāsajātaka stories demonstrate the same
preoccupation with outlining the Bodhisatta’s biography and illustrating his
acquisition of the perfections, as established by the JA and reinforced by the
Mahājātaka. manuscript evidence from temples in Northern Thailand leads
Skilling to suggest that ‘there may once have been a collection that classed the
stories according to the perfections that they illustrated’.27 There appears to
be a strong emphasis in many of the stories on the Bodhisatta acquiring the
perfection of dāna, especially through sacrificing his own flesh and blood. The
22 in addition to the Paññāsajātaka collections, the Lokaneyyappakaraṇa (of
approximately the same date and provenance) also draws on the tradition of the JA to
create a new composition, in this case a long jātaka about worldly wisdom. By synthesising
and expanding upon two jātakas – the Mahā-ummagga-jātaka ( JA 546) and the Kurudhamma-
jātaka ( JA 267) – the text manages to address simultaneously the types of worldly wisdom
relevant to both ordinary men and kings. its samodhāna identifies no fewer than 34
characters! See Jaini’s edition, to which he provides an excellent introduction: Padmanabh
S. Jaini (ed.), Lokaneyyappakaraṇaṃ (london, 1986).

Society, 28 (2006): 113–73, p. 130ff. Dating is problematic, and arguably unhelpful, since
there is no one collection and each collection relies upon older sources. The Zimmé Paṇṇāsa
perhaps dates to the fifteenth century and according to eighteenth- and nineteenth-

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 77
Bodhisatta’s gift of parts of his body is found in a few stories of the JA, and is
reasonably common in Sanskrit sources. as arthid Sheravanichku has observed,
this motif has a distinctive flavour in the Paññāsajātaka stories, in which
the Bodhisatta is generally explicit about the purpose of the gift (the future
attainment of buddhahood) and often enacts a form of devotional sacrifice. For
example, one story in a Thai collection tells of the Bodhisatta requesting that his
flesh be turned into gold leaf in order that he might decorate a buddha-image.
another shows him cutting off his head and burning his body as an offering to
the three refuges.28 Such stories once again blur the boundaries between jātaka
and avadāna, and reflect the increasing influence of devotional understandings
of Buddhist spiritual progress, but they nonetheless persist in associating jātakas
with the perfect biography of the Buddha.
in addition to a continued emphasis on the role of the perfections in
the Bodhisatta’s biography, this biography is extended further back in time
by the inclusion in at least one collection of the Padīpadāna-jātaka, which relates
the Bodhisatta-to-be’s last birth as a woman. in this story, which will be examined
in more detail in the next chapter, the woman makes an offering of a lamp to a
Buddha but does not receive the prophecy she wishes for as she is still a woman.
The merit of her action allows her to be reborn in a heavenly realm before being
reborn as a man (Sumedha) in the time of Dīpaṅkara Buddha, where the first
bodhisatta vow is made and the long journey to buddhahood begins. The story
explains the absence of the Bodhisatta during the time of the first three buddhas
of the present eon, as related in the Nidānakathā, as he was in one of the heavenly
realms. as Jaini notes, ‘it is very much to the credit of the author of this story
that he was thus able to improve on the Nidānakathā without in any way violating
the spirit of the canonical account’.29
in the Padīpadāna-jātaka and some other stories contained in the Paññāsajātaka
collections we find a certain amount of overlap with Sanskrit jātakas (sometimes
only now extant in Chinese sources). This may explain their ‘apocryphal’
status, since they are not in conflict with the JA, but are also not within it. The
Paññāsajātaka collections therefore demonstrate the flexibility of the jātaka
genre. Though the jātakas in this tradition share the structure of those in the JA,
and have doubtless been influenced by the latter’s preoccupation with biography
and the perfections, they also have the freedom to address times and events
outside of the realm of the JA. They can in fact be more broadly biographical
since they are not restricted by the imposed time-frame of the JA which, for
example, prevents female births, as it is said to relate only stories since the
bodhisatta vow. in addition the texts can incorporate jātakas not previously known
28 arthid Sheravanichkul, ‘Self-Sacrifice of the Bodhisatta in the Paññāsa Jātaka’,
Religion Compass, 2/5 (2008): 769–87, especially p. 778.
29 Jaini, ‘apocryphal Jātakas’, p. 28. For a more thorough discussion of this story see
Padmanabh S. Jaini, ‘Padīpadānajātaka: Gautama’s last Female incarnation’ in Collected
Papers in Buddhist Studies (Delhi, 2001), pp. 367–74.

78 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
to the Theravāda tradition, and address local concerns such as the mythology
surrounding local kings or landscapes. in this way the established jātaka genre
allows the incorporation of ever more stories, and an infinite expansion of the
Buddha’s biography.
GooD BUDDHiST SToRieS: JĀTaKa TexTS ToDaY
i have so far examined perceptions of the jātaka genre in some specific textual
sources from the time of the JA to the medieval period. These texts suggest that
the definition of jātakas established in the JA pervaded subsequent texts, both
later collections of jātakas and texts mentioning jātakas for diverse reasons.
Jātaka stories remain very popular in South and Southeast asia today, where the
jātaka book (whether in its Pāli form as the JA, or a vernacular translation such
as the Sinhala Jātaka-pota) are well known. Certain jātakas contained within the
collection have been subject to many retellings in a variety of different media.
However, it is here that our neat little picture begins to crumble, for modern
jātaka ‘texts’ (in which i am including modern media) seem largely unaware of
the idea that jātakas illustrate the long path to buddhahood.
i would like to end this chapter by bringing the history of jātaka texts up to
date, albeit only in a single Buddhist country, namely Sri lanka.30 even a cursory
glance at available jātakas in present-day Sri lanka reveals that in these popular
modern jātaka ‘texts’ we find an interesting contrast to the idea of the jātaka genre
given in the canonical and commentarial texts. i will examine a few examples
from modern Sri lanka to illustrate this: madhubhashini Ratnayake’s radio
plays broadcast in 1997; two series of children’s jātaka books; Ven. Dhammika
and Susan Harmer’s cartoon book Stories the Buddha Told; m.D.R. Perera’s english
poem The Sage of India; and Prasanna Jayakody’s film Sankara.31 in these sources
we discover the limitations of the JA’s definition of the jātaka genre.
30 i have only been able to study Sri lanka in any depth, though the scattered and
anecdotal evidence i have encountered in regard to Southeast asia suggests a similar
situation throughout Theravāda countries. in Thailand, for example, the children’s jātaka
books issued by the education ministry make no mention of the Bodhisatta but are merely
ethical animal stories. a more thorough study of the place of jātakas in contemporary
South and Southeast asian societies is clearly a desideratum.
31 madhubhashini Ratnayake, Voices From Afar: Radio Plays Based on Ten Jataka Stories
(Dehiwala, Sri lanka, 1999). Bosat Katā series (Colombo, 2000–2001); Jātaka Katā series
(Colombo, in association with Book matrix, india, 2005). S. Dhammika and Susan Harmer,
Stories the Buddha Told (Singapore, 2006). m.D.R. Perera, The Sage of India, or Maha-Ummagga
Jataka (Colombo 1992). (Perera also composed two other jātaka-based poems: Sasho the
Sage, and The Rime of Vessantara, but The Sage of India was the only one widely available
during my time in Sri lanka.) The film Sankara was released in 2006 and performed well
on the international film festival circuit: see www.sankaramovie.com.

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 79
We saw in Chapter 2 that some jātakas are a little problematic in their
presentation of the Bodhisatta, as he does not behave as we might expect a being
on the path to awakening should. one example given was the Aṇḍabhūta-jātaka
( JA 62), in which the Bodhisatta is a gambling king who pays someone to seduce
a young woman. Since only a selection of the jātakas of the JA are well known
in modern society, one might imagine that such a story would be intentionally
ignored or avoided. Rather, the Aṇḍabhūta-jātaka is one of just 10 jātaka stories
selected for rewriting and broadcasting as radio plays on Sri lankan national
radio in 1997. However, the problem of the Bodhisatta’s actions is sidestepped by
the absence of both the Buddha and the Bodhisatta; the Buddha is not explicitly
identified as the author, and the Bodhisatta is not identified with the king as in
the jātaka samodhāna.
although the author makes it clear that these radio plays were a Buddhist
endeavour, and consequently were broadcast on poya (P uposatha) days each
month, the Buddhist content is minimal. only two of the stories chosen (Illīsa-
jātaka and Apaṇṇaka-jātaka) include the paccuppannavatthu, and even in these
jātakas there is no identification of the births, nor do we ever hear the Buddha
speak. The Sutasoma-jātaka is the only story to contain the samodhāna, and the
reason for this is not clear, since there is no link to the ‘present’ time of the
Buddha in this case. in the case of the Aṇḍabhūta-jātaka there is a possible reason
for avoiding the identification of the births, yet in all the other stories there
is no reason not to identify the Bodhisatta, and many reasons why it might be
an enhancement. in the stories chosen the Bodhisatta is wise (Mātaṅga-jātaka;
Kosambī-jātaka; Apaṇṇaka-jātaka; Sambula-jātaka), generous (Illīsa-jātaka) and
forbearing (Khantivādi-jātaka), yet the audience may never appreciate these
qualities as belonging to him.
a similar situation is found in the Bosat Katā (‘stories of the Bodhisatta’)
series of Sinhala children’s jātaka stories, where only the story of the past is told,
and with little attention to the structure of the original text, even though the
stories are extracted from the Jātaka-pota, where all the different sections of the
‘classical’ jātaka are found. The stories are not even called jātakas in the title,
rather the books have such names as ‘The Story of the Heart’ (Hædaya Vastuva)
for the jātaka in which the Bodhisatta is a monkey who deceives a crocodile
into believing his heart is hanging in a tree. However, the identification of the
Bodhisatta’s character is included in small print in parentheses at the end of
each book, thus the Hædaya Vastuva ends:
(This story has been written from the Vānara-jātakaya of the Pansiya Panas
Jātaka Pota. in this the Bodhisatta is the monkey.)32
32 ( pansiya panas jātaka potē vānara jātakaya æsuren mē katāva liyæviṇa. mehi bōsat
vandurā ya.) The Pansiya Panas Jātaka Pota is ‘the book of 550 jātakas’ – the full title of the
fourteenth-century Sinhalese reworking of the JA, usually referred to simply as Jātaka
Pota – ‘Jātaka book’.

80 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
No characters other than the Bodhisatta are identified, even where the
corresponding stories in the JA give the characters for his companions.
Consequently, in this case the reader cannot know that the crocodile is Devadatta,
and that this is one of many occasions when he tried to kill the Bodhisatta.
in another set of books for younger children, this time with large pictures and
simple Sinhala, we find 10 stories in a series called Jātaka Katā (‘Jātaka stories’).
in these, however, there is no identification of the births, nor any indication of
the sources for the stories.33 in another sub-set of the series, aesop’s fables are
recounted. The emphasis, therefore, is on the entertainment of the story, and
the lessons it might contain about how to get on in the world. Thus although
it is true that children in Sri lanka are all familiar with jātaka stories, it seems
unlikely that they appreciate the significance of the stories in relation to their
portrayal of the Bodhisatta. Rather, they are just good stories, with passing
reference to their nature as jātakas, or stories of the Bodhisatta.
in some children’s jātakas even the fact that the stories specifically belong to
the jātaka genre is ignored. in the Great Buddhist Stories series of cartoon books,
we find a volume entitled Stories the Buddha Told, and in it are found several
jātakas. in all the cases only the story of the past is found, and in the case of
the Ummagga-jātaka only two small episodes from the career of the great sage
mahosadha are included, as separate, though consecutive, stories. in none of the
stories is there any identification of the births, or any indication at all that the
stories are anything other than stories the Buddha told. Nor does the book as a
whole give any indication that it contains jātaka stories. The jātakas are included
in the series (which also contains books entitled Buddha and his Disciples, and
Stories of Buddhist Lands) because of their merit as stories told by the Buddha, in
other words because of their didactic qualities. The sales pitch on the back of the
book highlights this preoccupation:
Throughout his life and when it was appropriate, the Buddha told amusing and
clever stories to illustrate his teachings. He did not sum up each story with a
‘moral’; the stories themselves did that. But the Buddha used these stories to
reveal to the world the values he held most dear – honesty, kindness, courage,
generosity and worldly wisdom. Stories the Buddha Told, the third volume in
Great Buddhist Stories, will charm and challenge the reader of today with the wit
and wisdom of the man who told them.34
33 The books are actually from an indian publisher, reprinted in Sinhala by m.D.
Gunasena. one book in the series – Nuvaṇakkāra Vandurā ‘The Clever monkey’ – contains an
amalgamation of JA 57, 208 and 342 in a word-for-word translation of the english version
by ellen Babbitt, which was first published in 1912. Just as the pre-JA history of the stories
is hard to pin down, jātakas have continued to be elusive in their textual history.
34 Dhammika and Harmer, Stories the Buddha Told, back cover.

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 81
The presence in jātaka stories of the ‘summing up’ in the form of the samodhāna
and often a little expansion of the moral by the Buddha in the story of the present,
is purposefully ignored in favour of presenting the stories as they stand alone. as
such, the stories demonstrate the wisdom and didactic skill of the Buddha, but
say nothing about the Bodhisatta.
The situation in adults’ books is no more straightforward, though adults do
have access to the full Sinhala translation of the JA, a Pāli edition of the JA in
Sinhalese characters, or the english translation by Cowell et al. in indian reprint.
a more likely way of accessing the genre is through modern literature, which is
often inspired by jātaka stories. a good example is Perera’s poem based upon the
english translation of the Mahā Ummagga-jātaka, which tells the many stories of
the skill and wisdom of the great sage mahosadha. Perera’s preface mentions
that the story is a jātaka, but gives little information about what this means,
and he provides no paccuppannavatthu or samodhāna in the poem itself. The
introduction, by l.G. Hewage, explains more fully that it is a jātaka about the
perfection of wisdom by the Bodhisatta. He continues:
The educational value of the Jataka Stories in building up in [sic] what is
called the Buddhist Cultural Heritage is well-known now and needs no special
emphasis here. Nevertheless, in contemporary society too Jataka Stories re-
interpreted in this manner may have a relevance in building up a peaceful
global society, where human wisdom will have a predominant role to play.35
once again, the story’s value is seen as being in its moral content and literary
worth, and the fact that one character is the Bodhisatta is not a matter of
importance. Rather than showing wisdom to be necessary for buddhahood, as
the JA would have us believe, the story shows wisdom to be necessary in the rule
of a peaceful kingdom. The poem ends:
To make Warring Nations lay down their arms,
To co-exist in Peace and sane remain,
To be free from fears of wars and alarms,
may wise Mahosadha be born again.36
Such an ending makes subtle reference to the idea that mahosadha will benefit
the world in future births, but implies this happens only in the same way as he
has done in this particular birth. This could be construed as an implicit reference
to the Bodhisatta, but it is well concealed, and there is no other reference to
either Bodhisatta or Buddha, or to the Buddhist religious system.
my final example from modern Sri lanka is a film that won the Silver Pyramid
award at the 2006 Cairo film festival. made in Sri lanka and written and directed
35 Perera, The Sage of India, p. vii.
36 Perera, The Sage of India, p. 91.

82 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
by Prasanna Jayakody, Sankara explores the emotions experienced by a young
Buddhist monk who is restoring paintings in a temple. The paintings in question
portray the Telapatta-jātaka ( JA 196), in which the Bodhisatta successfully
crosses a wilderness populated by demonesses as his five companions one by
one give in to each of the five sense pleasures and are devoured. The struggle of
the Bodhisatta in the jātaka story is echoed by the young monk’s own struggle
against his attraction to a local girl.
in this example, the fact that the story is a jātaka is important in the sense that
the Bodhisatta provides a good example of how to behave, and this ties in neatly
with sermons the Buddha gave about the importance of not giving in to lust. The
two characters – Bodhisatta and Buddha – are thus closely identified with one
another. in addition, the young monk is able to identify himself with characters
in the story, and finally with the Bodhisatta himself. Thus the story works as
both instruction and example, and this is neatly aided by the jātaka genre with
its past and present elements. However, this is only ever implicit in the film,
which does not even tell the story in full.37 Though this might be excused by a
general awareness of the story amongst Sri lankans, when asked why it had not
been deemed necessary to translate this story for the international audience,
the director answered that such explicit use of the story is unnecessary, since it
is the story of everybody’s life.38 The interpretation just given, therefore, is likely
to be made only by a scholar, not an ordinary audience member.
The presence of a jātaka illustration as the focus for this film is more significant,
as it provides a visual meditation (for the monk as well as the audience who
appreciate its allegorical significance) and also allows the film to include an
examination of the cultural context in which Buddhism is situated. Just as the
mural is repeatedly painted over by each successive generation (this is typical
of Sri lankan temple murals, and necessary twice in Sankara after a deliberate
sabotage of the newly restored paintings), so also each generation must undergo
the same mental struggle as the young monk to balance the spiritual and the
worldly. The international success of the film is not because of its reference to
the Bodhisatta, but is rather because the theme is one that can be appreciated
by everyone. The fact that the Bodhisatta demonstrated the correct choice
in the struggle is rather incidental to the film, and not communicated to the
international audience.
in modern Sri lanka, therefore, it seems that the JA’s idea of what a jātaka
is is widely ignored. although a jātaka is still accepted as a story of a previous
birth of the Buddha, his presence is at best incidental and at worst ignored
altogether. The awareness of which character in these popular stories is the
Bodhisatta is often absent, and this allows jātakas which seem not to fit into the
37 although the name and significance of the story is mentioned several times in
passing in the Sinhala soundtrack, the english subtitles omit this entirely.
38 Question and answer session after the screening of the film at the British Film
Festival, National Film Theatre, london, 26 october 2007.

Generic Conventions Embraced and Ignored 83
JA’s scheme of biographical and exemplary stories to remain in the common
cultural imagination. The individual lessons of the stories, which have survived
the process of appropriation and unification of the JA intact, are appreciated
once more outside the context of the genre. Their identification as jātakas gives
them both cultural and religious weight, without making the focus necessarily
on the character or qualities of the Bodhisatta. more important is the Buddha
and his ability to use stories that appeal to all kinds of people to illustrate his
teachings and his own wit and wisdom. The framing of the JA, though effective
in its own way, has had little impact on the stories themselves, which retain their
individual characters and uses, and their association with the Buddha’s vision
and wisdom, rather than that of the Bodhisatta.
CoNClUSioN
one of the issues that has recurred throughout the last three chapters is to what
extent jātakas are about the Bodhisatta. We saw in Chapter 2 that his character
is variable and sometimes very minor, and that this is explained by the many
different forms of story that became identified as jātakas. From a historical
analysis of the development of the jātaka genre it seems likely that initially the
association of the Buddha with jātakas was more important than the presence
of the Bodhisatta. it was only with the development of the JA that the focus
became the Bodhisatta and the path he follows, and although this preoccupation
remained in the texts, and the texts remained at least symbolically important, it
little affected the stories themselves in the imaginaire of Buddhist societies.
The rest of this book will be concerned with the emergence and purpose of
the preoccupation with the character and path of the Bodhisatta. Now that it
is clear that this has never been the essence of jātakas, but is rather a historical
development of limited (albeit significant) influence, this development requires
some explanation. in the next two chapters i will contextualise this development
in terms of the emergence of the bodhisatta ideal in the Theravāda tradition and
the rising importance of biography. Following that, in Chapter 7, i will look at
the ways in which this preoccupation with sacred biography affects the uses of
jātakas in a variety of Buddhist practices.

This page has been left blank intentionally

Chapter 5
Jātakas and Buddhology
my examination of the jātaka genre began by questioning the assertion – found
in many primary and secondary sources – that jātakas illustrate the Bodhisatta’s
gradual path to perfection. By tracing the history of the jātaka genre through
to the present day, i have shown that the association between jātaka stories and
the person of Gotama is twofold: just as important as the association with the
many lives lived as Bodhisatta, is the association with the teaching career of
the Buddha which provides a legitimate Buddhist setting and another level of
biographical emphasis. The insistence of the redactor of the Jātakatthavaṇṇanā
( JA) that jātakas illustrate the gradual perfection of the Bodhisatta over many
lives is therefore a development in the genre of significant, though ultimately
limited, influence. So what caused this development? Why was it necessary to
associate the jātaka genre with the long path to buddhahood? in this chapter i
will examine the circumstances – textual, historical and doctrinal – surrounding
the development of such a strong association between the jātaka genre and the
bodhisatta path.
one of the problems encountered in earlier portions of this book was the
dating of developments in the jātaka genre. Since it is impossible to reconstruct
the exact history of the JA, one is necessarily on insecure ground when trying
to place the rising biographical emphasis of jātakas into a historical context.
However, the context is in some cases easier to date than the jātaka texts, and so it
will help build up a more complete picture. For example, the major developments
in the genre occurred before and during the composition of some of the latest
texts of the Khuddaka Nikāya – namely Apadāna, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka.
Such texts are reflective of the rising interest in what we might call ‘buddhology’,
which carefully defined and systematically codified the nature of, and the path
to, buddhahood. They also reflect a rising interest in biography (of Buddha(s),
Bodhisatta(s) and arahats) as well as the use of self-consciously biographical and
historical genres. The development of these texts and the ideology they contain
can shed light upon the developing rationale of the jātaka genre.
The notion of multiple buddhas (past and future) and the resultant focus
upon and codification of a path to buddhahood, are both closely intertwined
with the changing ideology of the JA. The bodhisatta path is well developed in
Theravāda Buddhism, despite mahāyāna polemics insisting otherwise, and
the main aspects of the path – aspiration, exclusions and perfections – can
be reconstructed from jātaka and related texts. a comparison between this
path and the path to arahatship reveals that jātaka texts became associated
with all Buddhist paths on a practical level, even as they continued to glorify

86 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
the perfection of the Buddha and other awakened beings. in this way jātaka
materials subtly altered the soteriology of early Buddhism, and challenge the
binary opposition of mahāyāna and ‘Hīnayāna’ forms of Buddhism. Situating
jātakas within the context of developments in buddhology will thus allow me to
demonstrate the wider impact of the associations with Buddha and Bodhisatta
imposed on the genre.
maNY BUDDHaS
i noted in an earlier chapter that the jātakas provide a form of lineage for the
Buddha that refers back to his previous lives. another lineage of which he is a
part is that of the buddhas. in Theravāda Buddhism there are early lists of three
or six past buddhas as well as a future buddha, and later this list is expanded to
24, as in the Nidānakathā, the long biographical preface to the JA. By identifying
the Buddha as one of many, such accounts reinforce the idea that he realised
rather than formulated the dhamma, and that the dhamma is rediscovered time
after time. as i suggested during my examination of the overall structure of
the JA, such a repetitive context alters the time-frame in which the Buddha and
Buddhism are viewed. although the future buddha metteyya does not feature
in the JA, past buddhas imply future buddhas, and future buddhas imply present
bodhisattas. This prompts the formulation of a detailed account of the path to
buddhahood, as an exercise in glorifying past buddhas, a legitimation of Gotama
Buddha and his teachings, and an example to aspiring future buddhas. The JA,
as a text professing to be illustrative of the path of the Bodhisatta as well as the
authoritative teaching abilities of the Buddha, is an active player in this whole
discourse.
in the Mahāpadāna Sutta, the Buddha presents himself as seventh in a line of
buddhas, after Vipassī, Sikhī, Vessabhū, Kakusandha, Koṇāgamana and Kassapa.1
For each of the seven buddhas the sutta supplies details of the kappa (eon), jāti
(birth, social position), gotta (clan, family), length of life at the time, type of tree
under which they became enlightened, names of their two chief disciples, size of
their assemblies, name of their chief attendant, names of their parents and place
of birth. Following this is a more detailed narrative of Vipassī’s conception and
birth, along with the dhammatā (‘rules’) to which it adheres. Such rules include,
1 DN 14: T.W. Rhys-Davids and J. estlin Carpenter (eds), Dīgha-nikāya (3 vols,
london, 1889–1911), vol. 2, p. 1ff. in the Vepullapabbata Sutta (SN xV, 20) we find mention
only of the last three (l. Feer (ed.), Saṃyutta-nikāya (6 vols, london, 1884–1904), vol. 2,
p. 190ff.). Kakusandha appears again in the Māratajjanīya Sutta (MN 50) in a story of a past
birth of moggallāna (V. Trenckner and Robert Chalmers (eds), Majjhima-nikāya (3 vols,
london, 1888–1902), vol. 1, p. 332ff.). in the Ghaṭīkāra Sutta of the Majjhima Nikāya (MN
81), as related in Chapter 3, the Bodhisatta encounters Kassapa Buddha (Trenckner and
Chalmers, Majjhima-nikāya , vol. 2, p. 45ff.).

Jātakas and Buddhology 87
for example, that a bodhisatta’s mother is virtuous, that she bears him for a full
10 (lunar) months, and that he is unsullied by blood during birth. There is also
a narration of Vippasī’s youth as a prince with the 32 marks of a Great man, the
seeing of the four sights, his renunciation, realisation of the dhamma, reluctance
to teach, persuasion by Brahmā and the founding of the saṅgha. as several
scholars have noted, this narrative contains so many familiar details that it must
be either modelled on or provide the model for the biography of Gotama.
in the Buddhavaṃsa the list of past buddhas is expanded to 24, of whom the
last six are identical to those found in the Mahāpadāna Sutta. in this account,
the most detailed narrative is for the first buddha, Dīpaṅkara, and his encounter
with an ascetic called Sumedha, who is Gotama Buddha in a past birth. it is at the
feet of Dīpaṅkara Buddha (lying in the mud to make a clean path for him) that
Sumedha makes his first aspiration to buddhahood, and Dīpaṅkara comments:
See this ascetic, this mighty matted-haired ascetic,
immeasurable eons from now he will become a buddha in the world.2
This, according to traditional accounts, is the very beginning of the bodhisatta
career of the person who becomes Gotama Buddha. it involves two aspects:
a vow or aspiration (abhinīhāra) and a prediction (vyākaraṇa) by a buddha.3
The Bodhisatta encounters each of the 24 past buddhas that feature in the
Buddhavaṃsa, and each of them gives him a prediction to buddhahood.
The idea of previous buddhas was arguably borrowed from the Jains,4 who
claim 24 tīrthaṅkaras for each time cycle, though they include mahāvīra as one of
the 24, whereas Gotama Buddha is claimed as the twenty-fifth in Buddhist lists.
it is not only the number that is shared: the biographies of Gotama and mahāvīra
share certain features with one another as well as with each of their predecessors.
Gombrich has argued that the Buddha’s predecessors were recounted in imitation
of the Jains in order to provide authority for his message, which was thereby
shown to be independent from the individual teacher and rediscovered by a series
of great men. This was an alternative authentication to that used by the Buddha’s
2 Trans. from N.a. Jayawickrama (ed.), Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka (london, 1974),
p. 13, verse 60.
3 The term vyākaraṇa is better known in mahāyāna contexts, and this usage of the
term appears to be a relatively late one in Pāli literature. However, it is the term used in
the Nidānakathā and the commentaries to Buddhavaṃsa, Apadāna and Cariyāpiṭaka.
4 The evidence for this direction of movement is that the idea of past tīrthaṅkaras
is present from the beginning of Jainism, since mahāvīra makes reference to the idea
that he is following in the tradition of Pārśva, who is widely accepted as a historical
figure. Gombrich, amongst others, argues that the idea of there being 24 tīrthaṅkaras
also predates (and influences) the idea of 24 previous buddhas (Richard F. Gombrich, ‘The
Significance of Former Buddhas in the Theravādin Tradition’, in Balasooriya et al. (eds),
Buddhist Studies in Honour of Walpola Rahula (london, 1980), pp. 62–72).

88 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
contemporaries in early india, who would cite a lineage of their teachers. Since it
is a defining feature of a sammāsambuddha that he realises the dhamma unaided,
the Buddha could not authenticate his message by reference to teachers. The
past buddhas therefore performed this function of authenticating the Buddha’s
message, and later ‘acquired the function of ensuring the availability of that
message by launching Bodhisattvas on their careers’.5
it is notable, in support of Gombrich’s argument, that the Mahāpadāna
Sutta speaks only of the past buddhas, whereas the focus of the later account
in the Buddhavaṃsa includes both information about the previous buddhas and
an account of their interaction with the person who was to become Gotama
Buddha. The 24 past buddhas named in the Buddhavaṃsa are, after all, of special
importance because it is at their feet that the Bodhisatta receives his prediction
to buddhahood. in fact, the Buddhavaṃsa mentions three further buddhas before
Dīpaṅkara, but these never encountered the Bodhisatta, and so are less important.
They are therefore omitted, for example, from the popular illustrations in Sri
lankan temples, which show the Bodhisatta at the feet of each of the 24 buddhas,
receiving his prediction to eventual buddhahood.
although the prediction made by Dīpaṅkara Buddha is said to formally mark
the beginning of Gotama’s path to buddhahood, his biography can be extended
further back: the Saddharmālaṃkāraya, a Sinhalese work from around 1400,
contains stories about lives of the future Gotama before his birth as Sumedha,
and his encounters with many hundreds of thousands of buddhas.6 Thus the
web of buddhas grows ever bigger and more complex: as well as our Bodhisatta
encountering many past buddhas, the ways in which buddhas progress on the
path demand that past buddhas encountered each other as bodhisattas, to give and
receive predictions. in addition, bodhisattas who have not yet become buddhas
encountered some of the same buddhas encountered by our Bodhisatta.
an implication of the idea that Gotama Buddha was one of many, and indeed
encountered other buddhas during his long career as a bodhisatta, is that there
must also be future buddhas. Thus it is natural to find the doctrine of metteyya (Skt
maitreya), who is said to be currently in the Tusita heaven, waiting for the right
time to take birth as a human and become the next buddha. The only mention of
metteyya in the four main nikāyas of the Sutta Piṭaka is in the Cakkavatti (Sīhanāda)
Sutta of the Dīgha Nikāya. after outlining the decline of people’s morality,
happiness and life-span, and its subsequent recovery, the sutta states:
When the life-span of the people is eighty thousand years, monks, there will
arise in the world a blessed one called metteyya – an arahat, a perfectly and
fully awakened being, endowed with wisdom and conduct, auspicious, knower
of the worlds, incomparable trainer of men to be tamed, teacher of gods and
men, a buddha, a blessed one, just as i at present am arisen in the world an
5 Gombrich, ‘Significance of Former Buddhas’, p. 71.
6 Gombrich, ‘Significance of Former Buddhas’, p. 70.

Jātakas and Buddhology 89
arahat, a perfectly and fully awakened being, endowed with wisdom and
conduct, auspicious, knower of the worlds, incomparable trainer of men to be
tamed, teacher of gods and men, a buddha, a blessed one. Having experienced
and realised the knowledge himself he will declare it to this world with its
devas, māras and brahmas, to mankind with its recluses and brahmins, to gods
and men, just as i in the present time experienced and realised the knowledge
myself and declare it to this world with its devas, māras and brahmas, to mankind
with its recluses and brahmins, to gods and men. He will teach the Dhamma
that is beautiful in its beginning, beautiful in its middle, and beautiful in its
conclusion, in its meaning and its letter, and explain the holy life that is pure
and fulfilled in its entirety, just as i in the present time teach the Dhamma
that is beautiful in its beginning, beautiful in its middle, and beautiful in its
conclusion, in its meaning and its letter, and explain the holy life that is pure
and fulfilled in its entirety. He will have a community of thousands of monks,
just as i have a community of hundreds of monks.7
Thus metteyya is portrayed as identical to Gotama, with the exception of the size
of his retinue: this, along with the life-span of the time, is a common differential
for the different buddhas, past, present and future. There is quite clearly no
difference in his epithets or his task. This sense of continuity is reinforced in the
Buddhavaṃsa, where he is mentioned briefly at the very end, after a recap of all
the buddhas of previous eons mentioned in the text:
in this Bhadda-eon there have been three Teachers:
The guides Kākusandha, Koṇāgamana and Kassapa.
at present i am the fully awakened one; and there will also be metteyya.
These are the five buddhas, wise ones, compassionate towards the world.8
metteyya, we learn here, is no different to the other buddhas.
in later sources and those from outside the Theravāda tradition, however,
metteyya develops into a unique character with a variety of roles to play in
different Buddhist societies.9 The lack of definitive identification of metteyya-
to-be with any contemporary of Gotama Buddha (implying he is a relatively late
addition to buddhology), and the later development of an extensive mythology
and a multitude of practices involving metteyya, mean that the issue of his
7 Trans. from Rhys-Davids and Carpenter, Dīgha-nikāya, vol. 3, pp. 75–6.
8 Trans from Jayawickrama, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka, p. 101: chapter 27, verses
18–19.
9 in the Theravāda tradition we find metteyya in the Anāgatavaṃsa, Dasabodhisatt

90 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
origins and influences is hotly debated.10 However, whilst the mythology may be
varied and of external influence, the outline of metteyya and the possibility of
more future buddhas after him are natural Buddhist developments.11 as a future
buddha he is a symbol of hope, and is also accessible here and now, in contrast to
Gotama and the other buddhas we hear about in the Buddhavaṃsa.
metteyya is included in the pantheon of mahāyāna bodhisattvas. indeed, upon
first glance it might be tempting to see him as a proto-mahāyāna development.
However, that metteyya is a strictly pre-mahāyāna development is clear from
his role in mahāyāna texts, where he is often ridiculed in the same way as
chief disciples of Gotama Buddha such as Sāriputta. Whatever the origins of
his extended mythology, the idea of metteyya requires no further explanation
than that Gotama Buddha was not unique. Despite all the mythology, metteyya
remains a simple idea: a future parallel to Gotama and the other buddhas in
whose lineage he is situated, guaranteed to discover and preach the dhamma and
begin a new Buddhist dispensation, when the time is right.
as well as containing a story about metteyya, the Anāgatavaṃsa contains a
list of 10 future buddhas, whose stories are later told in the fourteenth-century
Dasabodhisattuppattikathā. These biographies include jātaka stories as well as
details of the final lives of the 10 future buddhas.12 Saddhatissa notes that several
eighteenth-century Sri lankan temple paintings include depictions of these 10
future buddhas,13 demonstrating their significance alongside the 24 past buddhas
and Gotama himself. Thus the lineage of buddhas is opened up and extended
yet further into the future. Gotama Buddha, we learn, was one of many buddhas
of past and future, and his story is thus both an individual biography and a
typological narration, intertwined with the stories of countless other figures.

mythology came from mithraic religion or Zoroastrianism. For a discussion see Joseph m.

and Hardacre, Maitreya, pp. 7–22. Nattier notes that the differences between maitreya and
the buddhas before him are limited to such things as arising at the time of a cakkavattin
king, and being welcomed by Gotama Buddha’s disciple Kassapa (who according to some

pp. 23–47 – see especially pp. 46–7, fn. 60).

the BodhiSatta PaTH
if there are future buddhas then there are present bodhisattas, and present
bodhisattas-to-be. This prompts the development of instruction as to how
to identify bodhisattas, and how to enter and follow the bodhisatta path. Such
developments are tied up in the biographies of Gotama Buddha as found in the
Nidānakathā and related texts, but have wider relevance to any aspiring bodhisatta.
The next task is therefore to examine the bodhisatta path – including the aspiration,
exclusions from the path, and the perfections necessary for its fulfilment – as
outlined in Theravāda jātaka and related texts, and assess the evidence that the
bodhisatta path was taken seriously in Theravāda communities.
according to mahāyāna polemics, ‘Hīnayāna’ Buddhism (the ‘lesser vehicle’
of which Theravāda is the only surviving form) is the vehicle of the ‘hearers’
(śrāvakas) since its followers rely on the Buddha’s teachings to help them attain
the limited achievement of arahatship. Such śrāvakas know nothing of the vow,
the path or the perfections that are required for attaining buddhahood, which is
described in mahāyāna traditions as the only complete form of awakening.14 as
a consequence of this polemic many scholars have characterised the difference
between Theravāda and mahāyāna Buddhism according to the different aims: in
Theravāda, it is said, one aims for arahatship, whereas in mahāyāna one aims for
bodhisattvahood, and ultimately full and complete buddhahood.
Samuels has been prominent in arguing against the identification of the
bodhisattva-yāna with mahāyāna Buddhism and the śrāvaka-yāna with Theravāda.
He urges scholars to pay more attention to the role of the bodhisatta path in
Theravāda, and examines evidence that this path was taken seriously in
Theravāda countries, by kings, scholar-monks and manuscript copyists.15 He does
not, however, give an extensive outline of the path as it is presented in Theravāda
texts. The main discussions of the path are found in the long introduction to
the JA known as the Nidānakathā, and in the commentaries to the Buddhavaṃsa,
Cariyāpiṭaka and Apadāna. Since this is a largely neglected area, it is necessary to
construct an outline of the path from primary sources alongside an examination
of its relation to the jātaka materials.
The Bodhisatta Aspiration
When Sumedha makes his aspiration to buddhahood at the feet of Dīpaṅkara
Buddha he utters an oft-quoted verse about the criteria he has fulfilled, which in
the Buddhavaṃsa reads:

in Jeffrey Samuels, ‘The Bodhisattva ideal in Theravāda Buddhist Theory and Practice:

(1997): 399–415, pp. 400–401.
15 Samuels, ‘The Bodhisattva ideal’.

92 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
The human state, male-ness, cause, seeing a teacher,16
Renunciation, attainment of the qualities, an act of service, strong will;
Through the combination of these eight things the aspiration
succeeds.17
although these eight qualities are required for the making of a bodhisatta
aspiration, there is no implication here that such qualities remain throughout
the bodhisatta career: in the Buddhavaṃsa the Bodhisatta is once a lion, a
yakkha and a deva, and twice a nāga, and he is only a renunciant in five of his 24
encounters with buddhas, and yet he still receives his predictions to buddhahood
and resolves to further practice the perfections required to fulfil this aim.
as i noted briefly in the previous chapter, the Southeast asian non-classical
Paññāsajātaka includes a story about the Bodhisatta-to-be’s last birth as a woman.18
The story has familiar dramatis personae: the buddha at the time is called Porāṇa
(old) Dīpaṅkara, to differentiate him from the bodhisatta at the time, who is called
Rāma and will become the (Pacchima – ‘later’) Dīpaṅkara Buddha who predicts
Sumedha to buddhahood at the beginning of the Buddhavaṃsa. The Bodhisatta-
to-be is a princess who makes an offering of lamp oil to Rāma who worships
Porāṇa Dīpaṅkara with lamps whilst making his bodhisatta vow. Her interactions
with the Buddha are thus indirect, even though he is her cousin. Hearing that
she wishes to become a buddha, Porāṇa Dīpaṅkara tells Rāma Bodhisatta that
he cannot predict her to buddhahood because she has not fulfilled the eight
factors. When Rāma asks what these are the Buddha replies with the same list
as Sumedha mentions in the Buddhavaṃsa.19 He then explains further: ‘Brother,
it is not possible for me to make a prediction for the princess because she is
a woman.’20 Unable to predict her to buddhahood, Porāṇa Dīpaṅkara instead
reveals that she will be reborn as a devaputta in the Tusita heaven as a result
of the merit of her gift. Then, when Rāma Bodhisatta has become (Pacchima)
Dīpaṅkara Buddha, (s)he will receive the desired prediction from him. This jātaka,

for making the aspiration to full and perfect buddhahood are extremely rare.

along with three others examined recently by Skilling,21 explicitly predates the

Exclusions from the Path
The implication in the Padīpadāna-jātaka is that the bodhisatta vow marks the
end of certain rebirth possibilities. Such a notion is taken up in a set of verses
found in the Nidānakathā, the commentaries on the Apadāna, Buddhavaṃsa and
Cariyāpiṭaka, and the ṭīkā to the Brahmajala Sutta:
Thus the men, perfect in all parts, fixed on awakening,
transmigrate for a long time through hundreds of millions of eons.
They are not born in the avīci hell, nor in the space between the worlds,
nor do they become asuras or beings tormented by hunger and thirst,22
nor do they become small animals,23 even when arising in the bad realm(s).
When born among men they are not blind at birth,
nor are they deficient in hearing or dumb.
The accomplished men, fixed on awakening,
do not take female form, nor do they become hermaphrodites or neuters.
Freed from the deadly misdeeds, everywhere associating with the pure,
with no use for wrong views, they understand the workings of kamma.
living in the heavens they do not arise as non-conscious (asañña) gods,24
and there is no cause [for rebirth] amongst the gods of the pure abodes.25
Good men, bent on renunciation, detached from the world in birth after birth, they
conduct themselves for the benefit of the world, fulfilling all the perfections.26
21 See Peter Skilling, ‘Quatre vies de Sakyamuni: a l’aube de sa carriere de Bodhisatta’,
in Pierre-Sylvain Filliozat and Jean leclant (eds), Bouddhismes d’Asie: monuments et
littératures (Paris: aiBl – Diffusion De Boccard, 2009), pp. 125–39. These stories are found in
the Thai tradition surrounding the beginning of the Bodhisatta career. in such sources the
Padīpadāna-jātaka occurs as the last of four stories where the Bodhisatta-to-be cultivates
intentions towards buddhahood. in none of these stories does he make an aspiration or
receive a prediction in the presence of a buddha.
22 This is a reference to the peta realm.
23 Since in the jātakas of the JA he appears as a rat, an iguana, a frog and a fish
(respectively JA 128 and 129; 141; 239; 75, 114 and 236) the interpretation of what exactly
counts as a ‘small animal’ is questionable.
24 literally ‘unconscious’, this class of devas have reached a realm of heaven
appropriate to their high meditative attainments. There is said to be a strong risk of

25 This is the heavenly realm where ‘non-returners’ are reborn and attain

94 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
From this set of verses we can see that some exclusions apply to the path after the
vow. There are clear reasons for denying certain rebirth possibilities: the worst
of the bad realms are not possible because of the great merit of a bodhisatta, and
the asañña realm is a place where a bodhisatta would be able neither to advance
on the path nor to assist others. When born as humans bodhisattas are able-
bodied men, explicitly not women, or those of both sexes, or of neither. one
assumes that this is because of the karmic laws preventing such deprived states
of rebirth, as well as the parallels between the requirements for buddhahood and
the path to it. indeed the Buddhavaṃsa commentary on the verse about the eight
conditions explains the need for the male state by a reference to the Buddha’s
declaration that a woman cannot be a buddha.27
in the commentaries to the Suttanipāta and Apadāna we find an alternative list
of ‘impossible states’ (abhabbaṭṭhāna) into which a bodhisatta cannot be born.28
This is a slightly expanded list, and is also more specific, for example it specifies
that when born as an animal a bodhisatta cannot be smaller than a quail or larger
than an elephant. The more sophisticated nature of this list implies that it may
be later than the one in the Nidānakathā, though the presence of both lists in the
Apadāna commentary might suggest that they merely represent two different
traditions. one of the most striking differences between these two traditions
is that women are not mentioned in the more detailed list. endo suggests that
this is because of the close relationship between the more general list and the
jātaka materials.29 The exclusion of women from the state of being a bodhisatta
is certainly supported by jātaka materials since the Bodhisatta is always male
in the jātaka stories of the JA, and indeed the Padīpadāna-jātaka explicitly seeks
to predate these stories with its tale of the last female birth.30 Since this is an
excellent example of the dynamic interplay between jātakas and ideas about the
bodhisatta path, it is worth examining the exclusion of women from this path in
some detail.31
