Jātakas and Buddhology 95
as well as being excluded from the vow and the path, women are of course
not able to become buddhas: in the Bahudhātuka Sutta of the Majjhima Nikāya,
the Buddha is recorded as saying that it is impossible for a woman to be a
fully awakened one – a sammāsambuddha.32 in mahāyāna Buddhism, where the
bodhisatt(v)a ideal is prominent, there has been much discussion about when
(and whether) a woman must become a man before attaining buddhahood,
with many texts sidestepping the issue by trying to demonstrate the ultimately
illusory nature of gender through tales of magical sex-change. However, the
exclusion of women from buddhahood and the formal path to it in Theravāda
Buddhism has not attracted much attention from scholars, who generally view
the restriction as unimportant given the priority of the arahat ideal (which
is open to women) and the ability in any case of women to be reborn as men.
as is becoming increasingly clear, however, the bodhisatta path has been taken
seriously in Theravāda Buddhism, and the exclusion of women from it may have
had a wider influence on attitudes towards women. Therefore it is necessary to
examine the origins of the exclusion of women from this path, and the role of
jātaka materials in the discourse.
The jātaka stories are rarely told in isolation, but form part of a large body of
story literature. The other most popular collection is the Dhammapada-aṭṭhakathā,
a long, story-filled commentary on the Dhammapada. many such stories portray
women in prominent (and positive) roles. Here, for example, we find the story
of Visākhā, a generous, compassionate and intelligent lay woman. We also find
the tragic story of Kisāgotamī, who was unable to accept that her baby son was
dead and carried him from house to house asking for medicine. The Buddha told
her that her son would be cured by a mustard seed from a house untouched by
death. Realising eventually that such a house did not exist, Kisāgotamī became
a nun and achieved arahatship.33 The lives of nuns and their experiences of
awakening are also found in the Therīgāthā, and stories of their previous births
are found in the Apadāna, alongside their male counterparts. in the Vimānavatthu
in Buddhist Jātaka Stories’, in Pamela Sue anderson (ed.), New Topics in Feminist Studies
of Religion (Dordrecht, 2010), pp. 103–15. it is worth noting at the outset that Foucher’s
assertion that the Bodhisatta is depicted in female form at Bhārhut is simply incorrect
(alfred Foucher, Les Vies Antérieures du Bouddha, D’Après les Textes et les Monuments de l’Inde
(Paris, 1955), p. 175, repeated in etienne lamotte, History of Indian Buddhism from the
origins to the Śaka Era, trans. from French by Sara Boin-Webb (louvain la Neuve, 1988),
p. 404). He argues that since the reliefs are sacred, and they wouldn’t be sacred unless
they depicted the Bodhisatta, the female protagonists portrayed on two examples must
be the Bodhisatta. in fact these female protagonists (as he himself notes) are identified
in the extant versions of the stories as characters other than the Bodhisatta. No close
relationship between the jātakas at Bhārhut and the person of the Bodhisatta has been
demonstrated.
32 MN 115: Trenckner and Chalmers, Majjhima-nikāya, vol. 3, p. 65.
33 e.W. Burlingame (trans.), Buddhist Legends (3 vols, Cambridge, ma, 1921).

96 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
and Petavatthu, we find stories of the actions of both men and women that led to
their rebirth in heavenly mansions, or in the realm of the hungry ghosts.
These other collections of stories demonstrate that women were not excluded
from good actions, any more than men were excluded from bad. There is rarely
any change of gender between births, suggesting that this was not relevant to
spiritual progress. many women made offerings to past buddhas and resolved to
become prominent nuns or laywomen (not monks or laymen) in the retinue of
Gotama Buddha. it is clearly demonstrated that both men and women are able
to attain arahatship, and so there is no need to aspire to a change in gender.
Similarly, the Bodhisatta keeps his male-ness throughout his jātaka stories,
whether he is identified as human, god, or even animal. Rita Gross describes
this as seeing ‘more affinity between male humans and male animals than
between female and male human beings’ and thus considers it to demonstrate
‘an extreme of androcentric consciousness in which, more than is usually the
case even for androcentrism, women are seen as outside the norm, as a foreign
object but not a human subject’.34 Seeing the JA in relation to other collections
of rebirth narratives suggests an alternative position, however: it is possible that
the jātakas of the JA originally reflected the soteriological equality that assumes
a fixed gender.
as i demonstrated in previous chapters, jātakas acquired their associations
with the bodhisatta path after the compilation of the early JA. Thus it was after this
text had been composed that the stories became viewed as demonstrating the
bodhisatta path from an initial vow that made rebirth as a female impossible to a
buddhahood that excludes women. in other words, the fact that the Bodhisatta
always happens to be male in his jātaka stories is simply because he is male in
his final birth. after the association of the stories with the bodhisatta path, his
consistently male character influenced the idea that a bodhisatta must be male.
a few stories in the JA and elsewhere demonstrate the idea that being
a woman is the result of bad actions in previous births. For example, in the
Mahānāradakassapa-jātaka ( JA 544) a woman who teaches the king about kamma
reveals her own previous births. as a consequence of one life where she was a
man who went after other men’s wives, she was fated to suffer in a hell realm,
followed by birth as a castrated goat, a monkey whose father bit off his testicles,
a castrated ox, and a hermaphrodite. This action also explains why she is stuck
in female form for the next six births, though we discover at the end of the
jātaka that she eventually becomes Ānanda, the Buddha’s (male) cousin and chief
attendant.35 The ability of humans to be reborn as either (or neither) sex is here
found hand-in-hand with the idea that rebirth as a woman is bad.
34 Rita m. Gross, Buddhism After Patriarchy: A Feminist History, Analysis, and Reconstruction
of Buddhism (albany, 1993), p. 43.
35 in the Therīgāthā the elder nun isidāsī relates a similar set of events and rebirths,
culminating in her final life, where she was rejected by several husbands before

Jātakas and Buddhology 97
in the early sources rebirth as a woman is seen as bad because of the suffering
inherent in a woman’s life, rather than because of the idea that women are less
capable of spiritual development. Such ideas are clearly linked, however, for
according to a simplistic view of kamma the reason for the suffering of a woman
is her spiritual and ethical immaturity in a previous birth. This idea is present
in Buddhist countries today: surveys conducted amongst Sri lankan women by
Huston, as well as Seneviratne and Currie, showed that many Buddhist women
believed their sex was decided by previous actions and that this explains the
extra suffering endured by women, for example during childbirth.36 Kabilsingh
records similar sentiments amongst Thai women, noting that this naturally
benefits the monks, who receive many gifts from women hoping to make merit
and attain a male rebirth.37 it is easy to see how the clearly evident fact of the
suffering endured by women in society can lead to the view that women are
spiritually less able than men. as well as being obstacles on the path of men, they
are then denied a path of their own until they become men.
like Buddhist texts in general, the JA contains a multitude of opinions on
women. Whilst some stories demonstrate ascetic misogyny (often at the expense
of Buddhist values), more positive characterisations of women tend to only show
them supporting the men. Such attitudes can be explained to a certain extent
by the context in which the text emerged: some stories are clearly aimed at
preserving celibacy amongst monks, and they were all no doubt compiled and
preserved in a monastic context. However, the association of the genre with the
bodhisatta-path has legitimated these arguably incidental attitudes, as well as
excluding women from certain attainments. What probably began as the result
of the perceived irrelevance of gender eventually supported the idea that a
bodhisatta must be male. This in turn contributed to the notion that a female
birth is not only unfortunate, but also restrictive, and that a woman’s role is
therefore to support the spiritual quest of her male family members and the
monks whilst aspiring to a male rebirth. That the popular literature, especially
the JA, participated in this debate about women’s nature and abilities is clear.
This is one example of the way in which the gradual association of jātaka stories
with the bodhisatta path has both affected and been affected by attitudes towards
the nature of this path.
renouncing and attaining arahatship (C.a.F. Rhys-Davids and K.R. Norman (trans.), Poems
of Early Buddhist Nuns (oxford, 1989), verses 400–447).
36 Perdita Huston, Third World Women Speak Out: Interviews in Six Countries on Change,
Development, and Basic Needs (New York, 1979), pp. 48f.; Thalatha Seneviratne and Jan
Currie, ‘Religion and Feminism: a Consideration of Cultural Constraints on Sri lankan

98 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
The Perfections
it is clear that jātaka materials, especially the Nidānakathā, have played a significant
role in establishing restrictions on who is able to make a successful aspiration
to buddhahood, and how they will progress. Jātakas have also contributed to
discussions about a central feature of the bodhisatta path – the pursuit of the
perfections, which are seen as an integral part of the practice of a bodhisatta.
in fact, the history of jātaka materials is closely related to the development of
doctrines surrounding the perfections. Therefore i will next examine what the
Nidānakathā has to say about the perfections, how the lists of perfections given
in this text relate to the Cariyāpiṭaka, and what Dhammapāla’s commentary on
the Cariyāpiṭaka adds in terms of ideas about the perfections’ chronology and
hierarchy in relation to jātaka stories.
immediately following the set of verses from the Nidānakathā that outline
the exclusions on the bodhisatta path, several examples of the cultivation of the
perfections are given. These examples are given in the form of references to
jātaka stories, mostly with supporting quotations from the Cariyāpiṭaka.38 For
example, in the discussion of dāna, the text mentions 10 jātaka stories in which
the Bodhisatta is said to have cultivated this perfection, and then provides a
quotation from the story of the hare. The set of examples ends with the following
statement: ‘This here is an abridgement; the meaning should be understood
from the detail of the Cariyāpiṭaka.’39 This implies a direct borrowing of all of the
stories from the Cariyāpiṭaka, yet the stories listed are not all found in that text.
This might imply that the version of the Cariyāpiṭaka familiar to the author of the
Nidānakathā was different from the text that has survived as such. Von Hinüber
suggests that the Nidānakathā may be referring to a Sri lankan Cariyāpiṭaka,
whereas the Cariyāpiṭaka which survives (and around which Dhammapāla wrote
his commentary) may be a South indian version.40
if there were – as von Hinüber suggests – two versions of the Cariyāpiṭaka,
perhaps the most interesting difference between them is that the stories quoted
in the Nidānakathā illustrate each of the 10 perfections, whereas in the Cariyāpiṭaka
that has survived only seven of the perfections are explicitly addressed. This has
led to suggestions that the Cariyāpiṭaka we have is incomplete. another possibility,
however, is that it represents an alternative list of perfections, one that predates
the standard list of 10. This would explain why a text that is internally coherent
so spectacularly fails to match up to the 10 perfections it is said to illustrate,
38 it is worth noting that the same list of perfections with quotations from the same
jātakas (but without the extra examples) is also found in the Buddhavaṃsa commentary,
where the references are also declared to be from the Cariyāpiṭaka (Madhuratthavilāsinī
commentary to the Buddhavaṃsa verses 76 and 77). like the Nidānakathā account, the text
refers readers to the Cariyāpiṭaka for the detail.
39 my trans. from Fausbøll, The Jātaka, vol. 1, p. 47.
40 oskar von Hinüber, A Handbook of Pāli Literature (Berlin, 1996), p. 64.

different manner. as l.S. Cousins has suggested,41 the list exemplified by the
Cariyāpiṭaka may have been combined with the North indian list in order to form
the list of 10. We can see how this may have worked from Table 5.1, which shows
how both the mahāyāna list of six perfections and the Cariyāpiṭaka list of seven
relate to one another and to the list of 10 that later became the standard for the
Theravāda tradition.

of 10 perfections. The latter incorporates all the perfections mentioned in the
Cariyāpiṭaka, as well as all those in the list of six.
The combining of the two shorter lists works particularly well if one accepts
two further proposals made by Cousins. First, Cousins argued that the Cariyāpiṭaka
may not actually contain stories that exemplify nekkhamma, and that those stories
41 i am grateful to l.S. Cousins for many fruitful discussions of this issue, and for
sharing with me the contents of his paper ‘Supreme Qualities: the Development of the

currently under such a heading actually fit better in the chapter on adhiṭṭhāna.
Second, he argued in favour of equating jhāna (Skt dhyāna) with nekkhamma. This
latter argument relies upon there being at least two conflicting etymologies
possible for nekkhamma: resignation/renunciation (related to Skt naiṣkramya) or
suppression of worldly desire (related to Skt naiṣkāmya). Cousins is convinced
that the latter meaning is in use in many Pāli texts, and that this brings the term
closer to jhāna as the practice of meditation which requires separation from
kāma, rather than the physical renunciation of the worldly life. This meaning
appears to be adopted by Dhammapāla in his commentary to the Cariyāpiṭaka, for
example he gives as the reasons why nekkhamma is stated after sīla in the list of
perfections: ‘because the accomplishment of sīla is achieved through nekkhamma;
in order to speak of good conduct of the mind after speaking of bodily and verbal
good conduct; because jhāna succeeds very easily for one who has purified his
sīla’.42 in many of the jātakas that address the issue of nekkhamma, it most often
involves both aspects – physically removing oneself to a secluded place and then
practising jhāna.
Cousins’ arguments suggest that the Cariyāpiṭaka very likely represents an
early list of perfections, before the list of 10 became prominent. if this is the case,
then the Cariyāpiṭaka represents an earlier tradition than the Nidānakathā, since
it demonstrates a list that was completely supplanted by the list of 10 found in
the latter text. The first reference to 10 perfections is in the Buddhavaṃsa, where
each of the 10 is presented in turn and the list of 30 is mentioned; this implies
that the Cariyāpiṭaka also predates the Buddhavaṃsa. The chronology of these
texts is supported by other evidence, which will be examined in Chapter 6.
in addition to highlighting some interesting questions regarding the
development of lists of perfections, the account in the Nidānakathā uses
the jātakas that illustrate perfections for an explicitly chronological focus. The
passage just examined is found towards the end of the Durenidāna (the first of the
three sections of the Nidānakathā) and followed only by the words:
Having fulfilled the perfections in this way, and having performed great
virtuous deeds that cause the great earth to shake, as is said in the Vessantara-
birth:
‘The earth, without intention and not knowing joy or sorrow,
even she shook seven times at the power of my generosity.’
when his life was concluded he passed away and was reborn in the Tusita
heaven. This – called the Dūrenidāna – should be understood as from the
[resolve at the] feet of Dīpaṅkara up to this rebirth in Tusita.43
42 Trans. from D.l. Barua (ed.), Achariya Dammapāla’s Paramatthadīpanī, being the
Commentary on the Cariyā-Piṭaka (london, 1939), p. 278.
43 my trans from Fausbøll, The Jataka, vol. 1, p. 47. The verse is quoted from

Jātakas and Buddhology 101
This chronology explicitly places the cultivation of the perfections (as illustrated
in jātaka stories) between the bodhisatta vow and the penultimate birth in
Tusita heaven. in addition, it explicitly states that the birth as Vessantara was
when the perfections were completed, and formed the antepenultimate birth.
The 10 perfections are also situated explicitly between the first aspiration and
buddhahood by the Buddhavaṃsa: after Sumedha has received his prediction
from Dīpaṅkara Buddha, he examines the qualities required for buddhahood
(buddhadhammā, bodhipācanā) and talks in turn about each of the 10 perfections.44
This sense of there being a chronology (and even hierarchy) to the
perfections is expanded upon in a lengthy appendix (called the Pakiṇṇakakathā)
to the commentary on the Cariyāpiṭaka by Dhammapāla, which takes the form of
a treatise on the perfections.45 after answering what a pāramī is, why it is called
such, and how many there are, Dhammapāla proceeds to outline two alternative
explanations for their sequence. in the first, he states that their sequence of
teaching reflects the order in which they are undertaken:
Herein giving, being easy to practice and of great help to virtue, is said at
the beginning. Giving accompanied by virtue is greatly fruitful and of great
merit, so virtue is said to immediately follow giving. Virtue accompanied by
renunciation …46
The second is an explanation for the list having a certain order, rather than the
practice following a particular sequence:
another method – Giving is said first because: it is common to all beings, and
there is giving even amongst the common people; it has the smallest fruit; and
it is the easiest. Virtue is said immediately after giving: because donor and
recipient are purified by virtue; in order to speak of eliminating the suffering
of others [through virtue] after speaking of the benefit to others [of giving];
in order to speak of a quality of abstinence after speaking of an active quality;
in order to speak of the cause of the attainment of [positive] rebirth after
speaking of the cause of the attainment of wealth. Renunciation is said directly
after virtue because …47
44 Chapter 2, verses 116–66: Jayawickrama, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka, pp. 16–20.
45 The treatise draws on the Buddhavaṃsa and JA, amongst other Theravāda texts,
and also upon the mahāyāna Bodhisattvabhūmi (the sixteenth section of the yogācārabhūmi
Śāstra, an encyclopedic work of the Yogācāra school from around the fifth century
attributed to asaṅga or maitreyanātha) and although the work remains consistently
Theravāda in tone and content, it takes the doctrine of the perfections to a new level
within the Theravāda scholastic tradition. This treatise is also found in an abridged form
in the ṭīkā to the Brahmajala Sutta.
46 Trans. from Barua, Paramatthadīpanī, p. 278.
47 Trans. from Barua, Paramatthadīpanī, p. 278.

102 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
each perfection is thus carefully linked to the next in the sequence. Whilst the
first sequence is explicitly chronological, the latter is not, and indeed there is a
sense that all the perfections feed into one another in complex ways. Thus their
chronology may not be quite as simple as the Buddhavaṃsa suggests.
Somewhat later in Dhammapāla’s treatise we find the list expanded into
30 perfections: 10 (ordinary) perfections ( pāramī), 10 intermediate perfections
(upapāramī), and 10 supreme perfections ( paramatthapāramī).48 after setting out
the various views about how these three sets of perfections fit into the bodhisatta’s
career, Dhammapāla gives us a definition for each:
The perfection of giving is said to be the relinquishing of wealth, wife, children,
and so on; the intermediate perfection of giving is the relinquishing of limbs;
and the supreme perfection of giving is the relinquishing of one’s own life.
Therefore the three perfections of virtue are not transgressing on account
of the threefold children, wife etc; the three perfections of renunciation are
the renunciation of those three fields having cut off attachment to them; the
three perfections of wisdom are acting with discrimination between what is
beneficial and harmful for beings, having abolished the craving for belongings,
limbs and life. The three perfections of energy are endeavouring to relinquish
and so on the aforementioned things; the three perfections of forbearance are
tolerance of those who obstruct belongings, limbs and life; the three perfections
of truth are the non-relinquishing of truth on account of belongings, limbs
and life; the three perfections of determination are steadfast determination
even at the loss of belongings etc, for the perfections of giving and so on
succeed through unshakeable determination; the three perfections of loving
kindness are not relinquishing loving kindness towards beings even when they
injure belongings, limbs and life; the three perfections of equanimity are the
acquisition of impartiality towards beings and formations whether of benefit
or injury in regard to the three aforementioned fields.49
Thus all the perfections are based around what are elsewhere called the five
great sacrifices ( pañca-mahāpariccāga) – of wealth, wife, children, limbs and life.
Such a division – here further defined as three types – fits well with a description
of dāna, but seems slightly awkward in regard to the other perfections. Here
giving is seen as both the root of, and in this sense the most important of, all the
perfections.
Demonstrating that dāna is the most important perfection in this way might
be seen as fitting the tradition that identifies Vessantara as the antepenultimate
birth of the Buddha, and his acts of generosity as the complete fulfilment of
48 The division into three lots of 10 must predate the Buddhavaṃsa as it is mentioned
– but not explained – when Sāriputta requests the teaching from the Buddha.
49 Trans. from Barua, Paramatthadīpanī, p. 321, with some readings preferred from
VRi edition.

Jātakas and Buddhology 103
the perfections. However, as Vessantara the Bodhisatta only gives according to
the ordinary pāramī, in contrast to several jātakas where he offers his life and
limbs. The Cariyāpiṭaka’s chapter on dāna begins with seven stories of offerings
of wealth, followed by the stories of King Sivi’s gift of eyes, Vessantara’s gift
of children then wife, and (finally) the hare’s self-sacrifice. in other words, the
order supported by the Cariyāpiṭaka (if indeed it is a deliberate order) is wealth,
limbs, children, wife, then life. This suggests that the order in which these five
gifts were placed was somewhat flexible. it is possible that Dhammapāla’s treatise
was influenced by jātaka stories preserved in other indian schools, where stories
of the Bodhisatta’s bodily self-sacrifice seem to be more popular as illustrations
of the Bodhisatta’s ultimate perfection of dāna.
as discussed in the previous chapter, the association between the final jātakas
of the JA and the perfections was initiated by the Nidānakathā but developed
further as the text spread through Southeast asia. it is therefore possible to see the
layers of development: first, through the influence of the early JA, jātakas become
seen as demonstrating the Bodhisatta on his path, and he is therefore seen as
illustrating certain key qualities required for buddhahood. Consequently stories
are explicitly retold in the Cariyāpiṭaka to illustrate a list of such key qualities.
after the lists of qualities are standardised (by the time of the Buddhavaṃsa), the
10 are associated with the final 10 jātakas of the JA (the Mahānipāta), which then
circulate separately as a way of both glorifying the Buddha and exemplifying the
qualities which Buddhists should follow. The development in understandings of
the perfections is intertwined with developments in the jātaka and related texts.
The jātakas are thus key to understanding developments in the notion of the
pāramīs, and vice versa.
Real Theravādin Bodhisattas
That the bodhisatta path – complete with aspiration, exclusions and perfections
– was well defined in Theravāda scriptures and commentaries is now clear. This
path is presented as an account specific to the future Gotama Buddha and other
named buddhas, but it opens up the possibility of there being bodhisattas in the
present who are destined to be buddhas of the future. Thus the textual discussions
of the bodhisatta path allow that any Buddhist can aspire to follow this path.
The evidence examined here demonstrates the presence in the scriptures of the
concept of the bodhisatta path, albeit not in such a well-developed form as in
mahāyāna texts; evidence from chronicles, inscriptions and scribal colophons
demonstrates that such a path has been genuinely aspired to by Theravāda
Buddhists.
Jeffrey Samuels has done much to draw attention to the presence of a
bodhisatta ideal in Theravāda.50 He notes a strong association between the notions
of kingship and bodhisattahood, and ascribes this in part to the Vessantara-jātaka
50 Samuels, ‘The Bodhisattva ideal’.

104 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
and its portrayal of perfect kingship and perfect bodhisattahood. The Bodhisatta
is commonly identified as a king in his jātaka stories, often a cakkavattin. Whilst
some stories (such as the Temiya-jātaka) advocate renunciation and speak of
the dangers of kingship, more show the Bodhisatta attaining kingship, which
forms a suitable happy ending. Commonly the Bodhisatta makes a good king,
providing for his subjects, giving alms, and protecting his kingdom from harm.
it is hardly surprising, therefore, that in Theravāda kingdoms several kings
were identified as bodhisattas, or identified themselves as such. Such references
range from the implicit and probably retroactive references in the Mahāvaṃsa
to Duṭṭhagāmaṇī’s compassionate resolve to become king of Sri lanka and unite
it in peace, to explicit self-identifications as bodhisattas in inscriptions from Sri
lanka, Burma and Thailand from the eighth century onwards.51
The bodhisatta ideal was also associated with highly achieving monks. For
example, the Cūḷavaṃsa records that the eminent monk Buddhaghosa, who
composed numerous commentaries on Pāli canonical works in the fifth century,
was compared by monks at the anurādhapura monastery to the future buddha
metteyya.52 Samuels also records the story of a twentieth-century Sri lankan
monk who claimed to have taken the bodhisatta vow.53
Further evidence to suggest that real Theravādins aspired to become buddhas
is found in the scribal colophons printed at the end of Fausbøll’s edition of the JA.
The first scribal colophon states, for example:
By this meritorious action, from here in my next birth,
having been reborn in the beautiful city of Tusita, abode of the gods;
Hearing the dhamma-teaching of world-protector metteyya,
and enjoying the great splendour with him for a long time;
When the Great Being – the Buddha – is born in the beautiful city of Ketumati,
conceived with the three [skilful] roots and born into a brahmin family;
Having given a robe and alms food – priceless, abundant and splendid –
and a dwelling and remedies to that great sage;
Having gone forth into the teaching, perfectly knowing and explaining the
unsurpassed three Piṭakas, being mindful and having supernormal powers;
in the future it will be predicted by him: ‘This person will become a buddha.’
Having given alms to buddhas each as they arise, they will also [predict] this.54
moving through saṃsāra like a wish-fulfilling tree,
with mind set on giving food and so on to beings as wished for;
Having fulfilled all the perfections – good conduct, renunciation, wisdom and so on,
having attained the peak of the perfection, having become unsurpassed buddha;
51 Samuels, ‘The Bodhisattva ideal’, pp. 404–6.
52 Samuels, ‘The Bodhisattva ideal’, p. 406.
53 Samuels, ‘The Bodhisattva ideal’, p. 406.
54 in other words, metteyya will be the first to predict him to buddhahood, but each
subsequent buddha will confirm this, as in the case of Gotama Buddha.

Jātakas and Buddhology 105
Having taught the sweet dhamma that brings happiness to the people,
having released the whole vast world, including the gods, from the bondage of
saṃsāra, may i attain the happy, splendid, serene place.55
it is clear that this copyist has a desire to emulate the biography he has just been
involved in preserving. even more explicit is the sixth scribe:
By this meritorious action, until i become a buddha,
by giving like Vessantara, owning like Jotiseṭṭhi,
knowing like mahosadha, may i be (thus) in birth after birth.
my wish and desire must surely take effect quickly;
the intentions and thoughts must come to fullness,
just like the moon on the fifteenth day.56
These aspirations may seem to be directly related to the JA, yet in fact such
aspirations are common on other Pāli texts copied in Sri lanka; even the
aspiration to be like Vessantara, Jotiseṭṭhi and mahosadha is found elsewhere.57
Such scribes, kings and eminent monks are hardly the norm, but rather are
exceptional human beings. it is important not to suggest that all or even most
Theravāda Buddhists resolved to become buddhas: the bodhisatta path is said
to be for exceptional people only. However, the principle that the Bodhisatta’s
example should be followed is presented in the jātaka stories, when they are
presented as illustrative of the bodhisatta path. Having established that the
bodhisatta path is part of the ideology of Theravāda jātaka and related texts, and
that Theravāda Buddhists seem to have taken the path seriously, it is now worth
examining to what extent this path differs from the main path in Theravāda
– the path to arahatship.

106 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
THe PaTH aND THe Goal
in mahāyāna polemics against ‘Hīnayāna’ Buddhism, the latter is accused of
knowing nothing about the stages on the path to buddhahood, the vow, or the
perfections. as is now clear, this accusation is unfounded. However, there is still
a significant development in mahāyāna Buddhism, for the goal of becoming a
bodhisatt(v)a becomes the focus of mahāyāna, whilst being reserved for only a
select few in the Theravāda tradition. This difference in application of course
later prompted developments in doctrine and practice in mahāyāna schools,
whilst the mainstream ideal of Theravāda Buddhism remained arahatship, the
very goal that is the object of mahāyāna accusations of selfishness and simple-
mindedness. However, during the period in which the bodhisatta ideal emerged
in Theravāda, the arahat ideal also underwent some changes that make the two
ideals converge a little. Though the bodhisatta ideal never gained a prominent
position in the Theravāda tradition, its influence on wider discussions about the
various paths and practices available to Theravāda Buddhists is significant.
The mainstream ideal of Theravāda Buddhism has always been arahatship,
yet this idea has undergone various changes. Bond argues that during the early
development of what became Theravāda Buddhism, the arahat ideal ‘developed
from an ideal readily attainable in this life … into an ideal considered remote
and impossible to achieve in one or even many lifetimes’.58 Bond’s evidence
lies in a close examination of the texts, the earliest of which narrate stories of
ordinary people becoming arahats easily and instantly, particularly after hearing
the Buddha speak the dhamma. later, arahatship became a more distant goal,
requiring specified actions on an increasingly well-defined path. Such a path
is laid out in a number of the early suttas, which focus upon renunciation of
the household life and the cultivation of certain qualities. although the gulf
between the putthujjana (‘ordinary person’) and the arahat is highlighted here,
at this stage it was still seen as at least theoretically possible to reach arahatship
in the same life, even if in practice this was not portrayed as likely. The third
stage identified by Bond makes it clear that arahats are few and the path is long,
requiring many births. as the goal became more distant, the path needed to
be more thoroughly set out, thus at this point the doctrines of the four paths/
fruitions and four noble persons (stream-enterer, once-returner, non-returner
and arahat) became codified. Commentarial literature expanded these and other
divisions of the path, and also provided stories of how the arahats of the Buddha’s
time had begun their practices in previous lives: ‘For the commentators, the path
had become central and arahantship a remote but controlling ideal.’59
58 George D. Bond, ‘The Development and elaboration of the arahant ideal in the
Theravada Buddhist Tradition’, Journal of the American Academy of Religion, 52/2 (1984):
227–42, p. 228.
59 Bond, ‘Development and elaboration’, p. 234.

Jātakas and Buddhology 107
The lengthening and codification of the path to arahatship took place over
the same period as the codification and opening up of the bodhisatta path:
changes in both can be seen by comparing the early canonical, late canonical
and commentarial expositions. as two alternative paths to nibbāna, it is notable
that neither is an instant fix; rather, both involve a long and very similar set
of practices and improvements. it is possible that the focus upon the paths
(rather than the distant goals) influenced the increased association between the
jātaka genre and the Buddhist path(s), and contributed to the view that such
stories provided examples of how to be a good Buddhist. as parallel paths, the
distinction between them is blurred, and thus the jātakas can be relevant to
both aspiring bodhisattas and those wishing to attain arahatship, since both can
practise the perfections (albeit to different levels60) and follow both the teaching
and example of the buddhas and bodhisattas.
although the distinction between a buddha and arahat was early considered
to be that a buddha required no teachers, it is not the case that a bodhisatta
therefore has no teachers, and this further blurs the boundaries between the
two paths. in a distinction first found applied to bodhisattas in the Cariyāpiṭaka
commentary, three types of bodhisatta are identified: (1) ‘one who understands
a brief instruction’ (ugghaṭitaññū) who is strong in wisdom and takes 100,000
eons and four incalculables to attain buddhahood; (2) ‘one who understands an
expanded instruction’ (vipañcitaññū) who is strong in faith and takes 100,000
eons and eight incalculables to attain buddhahood; (3) ‘one who is to be guided’
(neyya) who is strong in energy and takes 100,000 eons and 16 incalculables to
attain buddhahood.61 all three categories of bodhisatta presuppose that they are
at some point receiving teachings, and indeed the categories were originally
applied more generally to all persons. Whether bodhisatta or aspiring-arahat, the
path takes many lifetimes, depending upon both capabilities and effort.
as endo observes, there is a tension in early Theravāda Buddhism between
wanting to portray the Buddha as the same as his disciples (and buddhahood
and arahatship as essentially the same awakening), and the desire to glorify
60 although the perfections are strongly associated with the practice of bodhisattas,
the division into basic, intermediate and ultimate perfections, as well as the thorough
explication of their practice by Dhammapāla, make implicit the idea that other Buddhists
can practice them too. in some canonical sources the term ‘pāramippatta’ is used in relation
to the senior sāvakas, implying that the path to sāvaka-bodhi also involves perfections
(see for example MN 77 Mahāsakuludāyi Sutta. i am grateful to l.S. Cousins for providing
references for the occurences of this term).

Bibliographical excursion’, in Buddhist and Indian Studies in Honour of Professor Sodo Mori
(Hamamatsu, Japan, 2003), pp. 91–102. The detail comes from later (fourteenth- and
fifteenth-century) texts, though the distinction as applied to persons generally is found
in canonical and early commentarial sources, and is first applied to bodhisattas in the
Cariyāpiṭaka commentary.

108 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
the Buddha.62 extending the Bodhisatta’s path, setting out the requirements
of aspiration, prediction and perfections, and interweaving his path with that
of other buddhas, might be seen as a result of the latter tendency. endo argues
that this led to more emphasis being placed on the path to arahatship, as the
‘alternative and easier way to emancipation’.63 This might seem to argue for the
divergence of the two paths, rather than their convergence, for the lengthening
the bodhisatta path makes the Buddha and buddhahood seem greater and
therefore more distant. However, every path has two ends. at one end of the
bodhisatta path we have the glory of buddhahood, but there is still the nearer
end of the path, which is more accessible the further away it is from its glorious
destination. Rather than diverging, it would seem that both paths – to buddhahood
and arahatship – underwent similar extensions and clarifications over the same
period of time. The lengthening and codification of the paths has two effects: it
makes the glory of the Buddha and arahats greater whilst simultaneously making
the path more accessible. With the two paths looking ever more similar and ever
more long, everybody can have a place somewhere upon them.
CoNClUSioN
These developments – in past and future buddhas, the bodhisatta path and
associated perfections, and the lengthening of the path to arahatship – happened
alongside the developments in the jātaka genre examined in the previous
chapters. it seems likely, therefore, that the need for jātakas to be illustrative
of the Buddhist path(s) increased as the arahat ideal changed and the bodhisatta
ideal rose in prominence and accessibility. Jātakas became a way of showing just
how difficult and lengthy the path to awakening is, but also a source of practical
advice to those on all stages of all paths. Jātakas thus became a form of exemplary
and didactic biography; the example being set by the Bodhisatta but in the form
of teachings by the Buddha to those aspiring to any form of Buddhist progress.
The development of this twofold biographical association is closely linked to
other biographical texts such as the Buddhavaṃsa. The position of jātakas in
relation to other biographical texts, the rising popularity of biography within
the first few centuries of Buddhism, and the benefits of being a biographical
form of narrative, will be the focus of the next chapter.
62 endo, Buddha, pp. 223–4.
63 endo, Buddha, p. 241.

Chapter 6
Jātakas and Biography
more than any other form of religious narrative, perhaps, biography is a genre
that addresses both ‘doctrinal’ and ‘popular’ needs, as well as providing a
historical legitimation for a tradition or set of traditions. in the case of Theravāda
Buddhism, Woodward goes so far as to talk of a ‘biographical imperative’.1 as
Woodward points out, all of the Theravāda scriptures are framed by biographical
narrative: suttas hark back to Ānanda (and through him to the Buddha) through
the formula evaṃ me sutaṃ, ‘thus have i heard’, and vinaya passages contain
more explicit biography, both as background and as narrative explanations
for particular rules. Buddhaghosa and other commentators used extensive
biographical narrative in their works, and the former justified the buddhavacana
status of the Abhidhamma texts with reference to biographical materials.2 Thus
every aspect of Theravāda Buddhism – history, doctrine, philosophy, cosmology
– is interwoven with biography. Therefore biographical texts – which include
some jātaka texts – may be studied as rich sources for aiding our understanding
of the religious tradition.
as has already become clear, jātakas acquired an important role in the
biographical frame of Buddhism, and the biographical frame acquired an
important role in the jātaka genre, although the degree to which jātakas have
been viewed as ‘biographical’ has changed over time. i have spoken already
about the Bodhisatta’s perfections and imperfections, and the role of jātakas in
setting out the bodhisatta path. in this chapter i will examine how jātakas bind
together the notions of biography and autobiography, of Buddha, Bodhisatta
and Buddhists. i will begin by discussing the place of jātaka materials in relation
to the late canonical texts that preserve a biographical inclination, namely
the Buddhavaṃsa, Cariyāpiṭaka and Apadāna. Next will be an examination of the
historical setting for the apparent rise in biographical emphasis, in particular
the role of pilgrimage as popularised by asoka. The chapter will conclude with
a look at the special qualities biography has that make it both legitimating
and transformative, and how these characteristics of biography map onto the
jātaka genre.
1 mark R. Woodward, ‘The Biographical imperative in Theravāda Buddhism’ in
Juliane Schober (ed.), Sacred Biography in the Buddhist Traditions of South and Southeast Asia
(Honolulu, 1997), pp. 40–63.
2 Woodward, ‘The Biographical imperative’, pp. 43–9.

110 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
THe BioGRaPHiCal WeB
in Chapter 1 i began to examine the distinction between different genres of
Buddhist narrative, in particular jātaka and apadāna. although a definitive
distinction is elusive, the comparison is instructive, since only by saying what
jātakas are not, can we say what they are. Since apadānas were seen as a separate
genre at least by the close of the Khuddaka Nikāya, it is likely that jātakas were
by that time defined in relation to (and as distinct from) apadānas. it is therefore
impossible to examine the jātaka genre without also examining apadāna materials
and related texts. Since my focus is biography, it will also be instructive to look at
the wider field of biographical genres in early Buddhism.
When talking about genre, i do not wish to invoke or create a final or
exclusive definition. it should be clear by now that the jātaka genre developed
over time and in relation to other genres, and should not be viewed as static or
isolated. We might bear in mind the words of genre theorist alastair Fowler: ‘it
is neither possible nor even desirable to arrive at a very high degree of precision
in using generic terms. The overlapping and mutability of genres means that an
“imprecise” terminology is more efficient.’3 Fowler notes that genres are by nature
mutable, since every new work composed within a genre alters its character.
as a consequence ‘[g]enres are better understood … through a study of their
mutual relations … These relations are partly diachronic or dynamic (formation,
combination, mixture), partly static (similarities, contrasts)’.4 This view of genre
mutability is very relevant to the jātaka genre, which has been altered by every
text composed within it. However, whilst Fowler’s words are instructive, we
must be aware of the dangers inherent in applying Western generic terms to
a Buddhist context. life-writing might be seen as a particularly difficult area,
since Buddhist conceptions of ‘self’ are very different from the one essential soul
embodied from birth to death that tends to pervade Western understandings
and biographies. With these cautions in mind, however, it will still be instructive
to talk of genre in relation to jātaka, apadāna and biography, as there is a need to
examine how the texts situated themselves in the literary context. after all, the
lists of textual types found in early canonical texts demonstrate that the early
Buddhist community had ideas about genres that developed alongside the texts.
Three late-canonical texts – the Cariyāpiṭaka, Buddhavaṃsa and Apadāna
– form a useful group for reflecting on the nature of Buddhist biography during
the last phases of the Theravāda scriptures, after the Jātakapāli and its early
commentary had made its mark. i examined the Cariyāpiṭaka in some detail in
earlier chapters in relation to the JA, since both are collections of jātaka stories.
However, there is also good reason to group this text with the Buddhavaṃsa and
Apadāna. First, these three texts are considered to be some of the latest additions
3 alastair Fowler, Kinds of Literature: An Introduction to the Theory of Genres and Modes
(oxford, 1982), p. 130.
4 Fowler, Kinds of Literature, p. 255.

Jātakas and Biography 111
to the Theravāda scriptures. Furthermore, all three texts participate in the rising
interest in the biographies of enlightened beings and discourses on the nature
of enlightenment and the path to it. as a collection of jātakas exemplifying the
perfections acquired by the Bodhisatta, the stories of the Cariyāpiṭaka are in a
sense also apadānas, according to the definition suggested in Chapter 1; indeed
the colophon to the text calls it the Buddhāpadāniyaṃ. Such a term might also be
applied to the actions of the Bodhisatta in the Buddhavaṃsa, where he makes his
resolve to become a buddha at the feet of 24 past buddhas. all three texts narrate
some of the karmicly significant actions that lead a being towards the final goal
of nibbāna. The acts described are in some way typological and exemplary: they
are about buddhahood, not the Buddha, and arahatship, not arahats. Yet they are
also part of a personal biographical corpus.
The introductory passages of the Buddhavaṃsa also imply that it should be
treated in a pair with the Cariyāpiṭaka, for when Sāriputta requests that the
Buddha relate the text he asks:
‘Great hero, best of men, of what kind was your resolve?
at what time did you – wise one – wish for supreme awakening?
of what kind was your giving, moral conduct, renunciation, wisdom and energy?
of what kind was your forbearance, truth, resolve, loving kindness and equanimity?
leader of the world, wise one, of what kind were your ten perfections?
How were the higher perfections fulfilled? How the ultimate perfections?’
asked by him, he with voice as sweet as a karāvika bird gave an answer,
cleansing the heart and gladdening the world with its gods.5
The teaching that is the play of the victors who were buddhas in the past,
that is connected with the lineage of the buddhas,
through the understanding of his former abodes,
he explained for the benefit of the world with its gods.6
Thus Sāriputta’s request is for the life story of the Buddha, from his resolve
through the long path of acquiring perfections. only part of this is found in the
Buddhavaṃsa; the elucidation of the perfections might be seen as contained in
the Cariyāpiṭaka, and this association is confirmed in the commentary. indeed
the Buddhavaṃsa commentary here inserts quotations from the Cariyāpiṭaka in
illustration of the perfections.7
5 The text contains only sadevakaṃ but the commentary states that this means ‘the
world with the devas’.

10 perfections, but only seven of them, and probably predates the classification into three

112 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
in contrast to the other two texts here under examination, the Apadāna
focuses primarily persons other than the Buddha.8 The text demonstrates
the karmic results of actions from the perspective of those who have most
benefited, in other words awakened beings of the three types (sammā-sambuddha,
paccekabuddha, arahat). it is devided into four parts: (1) Buddhāpadāna relates the
Bodhisatta’s visualisation of a great offering to all the buddhas and disciples of
different eras; (2) Paccekabuddhāpadāna contains verses attributed to the Buddha
describing the merits of paccekabuddhas; (3) Therāpadāna relates around 550
stories by elder monks (who are implicitly arahats), about their previous deeds
and present results of these deeds; (4) Therī-apadāna relates 40 similar stories
by elder nuns. although most of the apadānas are indeed glorious deeds with
positive results, the presence of some tales of bad kamma influenced mellick’s
etymology of the term apadāna, as discussed in Chapter 1. mellick notes that the
sections about the Buddha and paccekabuddhas are likely to have been added in a
deliberate attempt to match the threefold ideal, since they have a very different
style and sit rather uneasily in the collection.9
as was noted in Chapter 2, the Therāpadāna contains a section, the
Pubbakammapiloti-apadāna, which relates the bad deeds committed by the
Bodhisatta that led to much suffering in hell, and eventually to the slight
discomforts experienced by Gotama in his final life. These stories are therefore
jātakas as well as apadānas. The real bulk of the text, however, consists of
biographies of the monks and nuns that made up the first Buddhist community.
The Apadāna thus demonstrates a rise in biography of the Buddha’s close
followers as a legitimate genre in itself, rather than as incorporated in the
Buddha’s own jātaka stories, which also contain many stories about members of
his early community. The arahats whose tales are told in the Apadāna provide a
model for Buddhists, in terms of both their final lives and their previous deeds.
Such a presentation of the previous deeds of the great followers of the Buddha
demonstrates the merit of devotion, and thus sets out a path for people lucky
enough to live in the time of a buddha or his teaching.
in many cases the stories contained in the Apadāna involve an encounter
with a past buddha, and in most the main character performs a pious act such as
the giving of a gift, makes an aspiration, and receives a prediction. after many
intervening births this prediction comes true when the character in question
becomes an arahat, and maybe also achieves a particular position within the
early Buddhist community. in the commentary to the Apadāna we find that the
paths to the different types of bodhi require aspirations like the one made by the
The link i am making here is conceptual and relates to genre; i am not trying to suggest
that the two texts were authored at the same time or by the same people.

Jātakas and Biography 113
Bodhisatta at the feet of Dīpaṅkara Buddha. endo notes that ‘paccekabuddhas
are required to satisfy only five conditions for abhinīhāra as against eight for
a Bodhisatta aspirant: viz., (1) a human; (2) a male; (3) being able to see an
enlightened one (Buddha, paccekabuddha, or arahant); (4) ready to sacrifice
one’s life for a Buddha; and (5) having a firm resolve. Disciples, on the other
hand, are required to satisfy only the last two conditions’.10 These requirements
deliberately strengthen the comparison between the three paths to bodhi, and
make explicit the relationship between the Apadāna and the stories of the
Bodhisatta’s long path told in the Buddhavaṃsa, Cariyāpiṭaka and JA.
Further intertwining of these texts is demonstrated by the fact that the
long path to the attainment of arahatship narrated in the Apadāna is only partly
directed by the actions of the disciples themselves. after making their aspirations
they must wait, through countless births (not all of them pleasant), for the time
when the Bodhisatta is ready to become Buddha, supporting him where they
can. This aspect of the stories is perhaps best exemplified in the yasodharā-
therī-apadāna, where Gotama’s former wife declares that she has supported his
quest for buddhahood right from the beginning, when she gave Sumedha (the
Bodhisatta) some flowers with which to make offerings to Dīpaṅkara Buddha.
She has renounced with him or been willingly given away in birth after birth to
help his pursuit of the perfections.11 The Bodhisatta’s path would not have been
possible, the Apadāna stories argue, without the support of a larger community
who eventually became the first Buddhist monks and nuns.
John Strong has noted that scholars tend to have what he calls an
‘individualistic bias’ in the study of sacred biography. Whilst this ‘may be
mitigated somewhat by the realisation that Buddhist (as indeed most indian)
sacred biographies do not properly begin and end with a single person’s lifetime
but incorporate a whole karmic history, including previous lives and future
existences as “other” beings’ he insists that ‘such longitudinal extensions of
individual biographies over many lifetimes run the risk of merely reinforcing
a sense of self, if they are not complemented by lateral expansions’.12 With the
addition of the Apadāna to the biographical corpus, the chief followers of Gotama
Buddha are shown to have biographies intimately connected to those of other
awakened beings. many – like the Bodhisatta – have interacted with past buddhas
and made aspirations that bind them to Gotama’s own destiny. We might say
that such apadānas demonstrate the formalisation of the lateral expansion of
the Buddha’s biography, which was begun in the jātakas of the JA. Such lateral

Pāli Commentaries (Dehiwela, 1997), p. 255.
11 For a full translation see mellick, A Critical Edition.

expansion complements the expansion back in time through the Buddhavaṃsa
and Cariyāpiṭaka, creating a complex web of biographical materials.
THe RiSe iN BioGRaPHY
The three texts i have just examined are evidently designed to fulfil the same
purpose: the provision of extensive biographical materials that form an example
for all Buddhists, glorify the particularly advanced beings who founded the
religion and formed its early community, and demonstrate in detail the actions
required for the achievement of the Buddhist goal(s). These texts are clearly
later than the Jātakapāli and whatever commentary was circulating with it in the
early period, and they show signs of having been influenced by the biographical
ideology of the JA (or its predecessor) examined in Chapter 3. They are also
influenced by the developments in buddhology and the codification of the
Buddhist path(s) examined in Chapter 5. it is now time to address the historicity
of this web of ideas and texts, and to ask how and why these developments
occurred when they did. answers to such questions are necessarily tentative,
since we cannot be certain of much in the early period, and must be cautious
when using traditional accounts of the early developments. However, this
study would be incomplete without an attempt to fix the developments i have
identified into a historical context.
We noted above that the Apadāna, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka are considered
to be amongst the latest additions to the Theravāda scriptures, and they were
indeed late enough to raise queries about their status as buddhavacana, leading
reputedly to their rejection by the Dīgha-bhāṇakas.13 Their dubious nature as
buddhavacana might well have been the reason why their compilers place the
accounts in a first-person narrative, in contrast to the more standard third-
person narratives of the JA. although such an account of these texts does help
to date them, we are still on unstable ground when trying to ascertain even the
date of the closing of the canon, if indeed there was such a concept of canon,
and if it was ever properly closed. if we accept traditional accounts that date the
writing (and therefore to some extent fixing) of the Theravāda scriptural corpus
to the first century bce, then we have a span of four centuries during which our
texts developed.
We have thus far treated the Apadāna, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka as
contemporaneous, but we can in fact place them in a relative chronology. Walters
argues that progress is from Cariyāpiṭaka to the Buddhavaṃsa to the Apadāna,
because progress is from Bodhisatta-biography, to the biography of many
buddhas, to the biography of other enlightened beings. He also notes that each
13 oskar von Hinüber, A Handbook of Pāli Literature (Berlin, 1996), pp. 42–3 provides a
good discussion of the contested contents of the Khuddaka Nikāya.

Jātakas and Biography 115
text in the sequence quotes verses from its predecessors.14 Von Hinüber notes
that the Apadāna ‘seems to be younger than the Buddhavaṃsa, but much older
than the commentaries’.15 The fact that the Buddhavaṃsa explicitly mentions the
10 perfections and implies that such a topic was covered by another discourse
by the Buddha, suggests that it is later than the Cariyāpiṭaka, which most likely
preserves an older list of perfections. We thus have several reasons for placing
these texts in such an order, and of course for placing the Jātakapāli and its early
commentary before the Cariyāpiṭaka. as a consequence, the textual history over
the four centuries following the death of the Buddha probably included the
development in turn of four separate texts, each of which developed the idea of
biography further.
This textual evidence is supported by archaeological and artistic evidence
for the developments in buddhology represented by the texts. in one of asoka’s
edicts (Niglīva, or Nigālisāgara, Pillar inscription) we hear of his repair and
enlargement of a stūpa for Koṇāgamana (here called Konakamana) Buddha,
demonstrating that past buddhas were known and deemed worthy of veneration
by the middle of the third century bce. The ancient monuments at Bhārhut and
Sāñcī have sets of seven trees, one for each of the group of seven buddhas, with
labels at the former making this link explicit.16 Thus, one assumes, the rise in
numbers of buddhas to 24 probably post-dates these earliest sites, but the concept
of past buddhas was current at the time of asoka. in addition to representations
of past buddhas, countless sites – including Bhārhut and Sāñcī – are illustrated
with jātaka stories.
it is clear that a crucial link in the history of jātakas and biography is the
reign of asoka, who united much of the indian subcontinent, sent out Buddhist
missions, and left behind a wealth of inscriptions. Several scholars have linked
developments in buddhology to the influence of asoka. Bareau, for example,
has argued that asoka’s reign ‘coincides with the zenith of the deified Buddha
Gautama’ and expansions of the buddha and bodhisatta ideals to include other
figures.17 Walters argues that the development of the extended biographies
in the Apadāna, Buddhavaṃsa and Cariyāpiṭaka necessarily took place after the
unification of asoka’s empire, which prompted a universalisation of Buddhist
14 Jonathan S. Walters, ‘Stūpa, Story, and empire: Constructions of the Buddha
Biography in early Post-aśokan india’ in Juliane Schober (ed.), Sacred Biography in the
Buddhist Traditions of South and Southeast Asia (Honolulu, 1997), pp. 160–192, p. 165.

116 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
doctrine and practice.18 asoka is credited with popularising pilgrimage to
key sites associated with the Buddha’s biography, and is legendarily said to
have established 84,000 stūpas containing relics of the Buddha. By so doing he
contributed greatly to the rising interest in Buddhist sacred biography.
Walters examines the link between pilgrimage sites and sacred biography
in the context of how the Cariyāpiṭaka, Buddhavaṃsa and Apadāna are linked to
the practices of building, decorating and using stūpa sites. i will have reason
to examine his arguments further in the next chapter in relation to the role
of jātakas at such sites, but his comments are more generally relevant here.
For Walters, stūpa cults are made meaningful not only by the inscriptions but
also by the three texts that map out an immensely complex biographical web
linked together by acts of devotion such as took place at the sites. Such acts of
devotion are recorded in the texts (particularly the Apadāna) and inscriptions,
and link the devotee/donor into the biographical web, the explication of which
culminated in the Apadāna and its portrayal of lateral karmic bonds. For Walters,
the Buddha’s biography ‘is indistinguishable from the biographies of all those
who were involved in the complex agencies that provided the context for his
(and their) actions throughout time’.19 Thus, he argues, ‘Apadāna is part of the
biography of a cosmic society still moving on together toward nirvāṇa: a universal
society whose members are the participants, past and present, in the stūpa
cult’.20 Walters tentatively dates the texts to shortly after the asokan period, and
argues that the rise in such literature is linked to the rise in devotional practices
associated with stūpa sites.
all the evidence points to the probable composition of the Cariyāpiṭaka,
Buddhavaṃa and Apadāna – in that order – during perhaps the second and first
centuries bce. The Jātakapāli and its early commentary must have been composed
somewhat earlier. The importance of biography in this period is linked to the
spread of Buddhism and the creation of a sacred landscape, as well as to the
developments in buddhology examined in the previous chapter. Biographical
narrative also brought with it certain possibilities unique to its generic
characteristics, which i will examine next.
WHY BioGRaPHY?
Having examined the historical and doctrinal circumstances that prompted and
accompanied the rise in biographical materials within early Buddhism, i wish now
to look at some of the reasons why biographical texts – and jātakas in particular
– may have been so popular and successful. To a certain extent my focus is now
moving away from historical matters towards the more timeless qualities that
18 Walters, ‘Stūpa, Story, and empire’.
19 Walters, ‘Stūpa, Story, and empire’, p. 176.
20 Walters, ‘Stūpa, Story, and empire’, p. 176.

Jātakas and Biography 117
jātakas embody, such as an ability to legitimate a wide variety of local and folk
stories, the transformative potential of being both teaching and example, and
the creative resolution of certain paradoxes about the nature of the Buddha. This
examination of the qualities of the jātaka genre will highlight further the reasons
for its popularity and development during the first four centuries of Buddhism.
Furthermore, it will form the basis for my examination of the role of the special
qualities of jātakas in Buddhist practice in the next chapter.
i noted above that the structure of the jātaka stories gives them a legitimate
place in the Buddhist scriptures, as both buddhavacana and Bodhisatta-biography.
Stories from diverse backgrounds and with diverse themes were raised up by the
generic setting as a jātaka, and even those stories with the most tenuous links
to Buddha and Bodhisatta were happily incorporated into a quasi-canonical
text. The identification of the Buddha as narrator and Bodhisatta as protagonist
is more powerful than mere naming of characters for legitimating purposes,
however. The interplay between Buddha and Bodhisatta allows the text to
address the wider Buddhist question of how one escapes from saṃsāra. it does
this by narrating both the painful and seemingly endless repetition of saṃsāra
and the end of time altogether for the narrator who has attained nibbāna.
as Steven Collins has suggested, a person’s life is like a story, and within
saṃsāra each life becomes a short story connected to other short stories.21 For
Buddhists the endless repetition is an unsatisfactory situation that can only
be ended by the attainment of nibbāna. The Buddha’s life, as related in the JA,
narrates both the short stories endlessly connected to one another (in each
jātaka), and the story that ends all these (in the frame narrative). The text
therefore incorporates both repetitive (or seemingly cyclical) time and non-
repetitive (or linear and progressive) time, in much the same way as Collins
demonstrates for the Buddhavaṃsa.22 The presence of the Buddha thus gives
more meaning to the actions of the Bodhisatta, for he represents the end-point
of the narrative – nibbāna. Collins argues that nibbāna is unnarratable because it
is timeless, and narrative can only deal with finite time. The way in which the
Pāli imaginaire deals with this is by using nibbāna as the full stop, the closure
marker in both systematic and narrative thought.23 This is clear in the case of
the JA, where nibbāna becomes an expression of the end of finite time for one
person, within which the individual jātakas relate small pockets of seemingly
inescapable repetition.
21 See, for example, Steven Collins, Nirvana: Concept, Imagery, Narrative (Cambridge,
2010), pp. 22, 27.
22 Steven Collins, Nirvana and Other Buddhist Felicities: Utopias of the Pali Imaginaire
(Cambridge, 1998), and Nirvana: Concept, Imagery, Narrative, chapter 5.
23 He notes (Collins, Nirvana and Other Buddhist Felicities, p. 282): ‘inexpressible,
timeless nirvana is a moment in the Buddhist textualization of time, the explicit or
implicit closure-marker in its discourse of felicity. it is the motionless and ungraspable
horizon, the limit-condition which makes of the Pali imaginaire a coherent whole.’

118 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
By being placed in the shadow of nibbāna, the individual jātaka stories are free
to address worldly issues, to comment on kamma and narrate the transmigration
of a person through many lives, with all their ups and downs and encounters
with the lives of others. The Bodhisatta may act in ways that challenge our
perception of him, and he may also take a back seat in stories that focus more
upon other characters. He may fall in love, or obtain a kingdom, without
challenging Buddhist doctrines. Furthermore, the stories can address seemingly
insignificant matters of daily life or local concerns, whilst still being anchored to
the biographies of both Buddha and Bodhisatta. The diversity and repetition of
the jātakas is legitimated by the assumed ending, which every audience member
already knows – that the hero attains awakening. The individual jātakas may
thus comment on kamma, rebirth, heaven and hell, yet as a whole the JA also
teaches about arahatship, buddhahood and nibbāna, and speaks to its audience
about the possibility of having a finite biography as impressive as the Buddha’s.
Thus the broad biographical framing of the text allows for continuing diversity
within a united – and very influential – master narrative.
outside the great textual collection the same legitimative association
with both Buddha and Bodhisatta applies, and is perhaps best seen in the
role of jātakas in local sites.24 The travelogues of the Chinese pilgrims Faxian
(fifth century ce), Songyun (sixth century ce) and xuanzang (seventh century
ce) describe sites in North india that are associated with the events of jātaka
stories.25 Several scholars have drawn attention to the fact that such associations
with jātakas were most popular in (greater) Gandhāra, a place where no sites
could reasonably be associated with the final birth of the Buddha, and which
therefore benefited greatly from associations with past births of the Buddha or
potent Buddha-relics.26 The Gandhāran preoccupation with making Buddhism
relevant locally is also evident in the recent manuscript finds, which contain
local traditions and stories.27 The localisation of jātaka stories in this region was
thus part of a wider strategy for associating this newly Buddhist land with the
activities of the Buddha, and – vice versa – incorporating local features into the
newly arrived religion.

of Buddhism see Naomi appleton, ‘a Place for the Bodhisatta: the local and the Universal
in Jātaka Stories’, Acta Orientalia Vilnensia, 8/1 (2007): 109–22.
25 For detail of the sites visited and the associated stories see appleton, ‘a Place for

26 For example see Shoshin Kuwayama, ‘The Buddha’s Bowl in Gandhāra and

Jātakas and Biography 119
Such localisation is not limited to Gandhāra: for example Namobuddha, near
Panauti in present-day Nepal, remains a popular pilgrimage site with Newar
Buddhists, who hold that it is where the Bodhisatta sacrificed himself to a starving
tigress, as narrated in the popular Mahāsattva-jātaka or Vyāghrī-jātaka.28 in a part
of eastern Thailand inhabited by laopuan people, there is a town associated with
the characters and events of the Mahā-Ummagga-jātaka, and in several cases other
jātakas have been ‘expanded with explanations attached to the story or with
episodes invented by the villages to explain puzzling incidents or geographic
features in the community’.29 Some jātakas have even become associated with
several locations simultaneously, for example the Aśvarāja story, which has a
strong association with Sri lanka (and is even recounted by xuanzang as a story
of the origin of the Sinhalese people) but is transported in the Newar version to
the Himalayas to transform the story into a warning for traders planning a trip
to Tibet.30 The Vessantara-jātaka, though strongly associated with a pilgrimage
centre in Gandhāra, is also believed by some to have taken place in Northeast
Thailand.31
in addition to the many free associations between jātakas and local areas, the
flexibility of the setting of a jātaka is evident in the pictorial representations of
jātakas found in temples and on manuscripts. These tend to reflect the land and
culture contemporary with the painting, with no concern for the historicity or
place of the stories. Thus we see the Bodhisatta in eighteenth-century Burmese
court dress, or travelling in a clipper ship, and, as Griswold points out, ‘the
happy anachronism serves as a reminder that the lessons the tales teach are
timeless’.32 This flexibility of jātaka stories relies upon the fact that most stories
do not contain a concrete association with any specific place.33 it also relies upon
