120 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
the fact that jātakas are a legitimate genre, closely linked to both the person of
the Buddha (who is anchored in time and place to fifth century bce North india)
and Bodhisatta (who is free to roam in any time and any place). only through
these two biographical associations can jātakas maintain both the flexibility and
authority needed for the successful incorporation of local tales into Buddhism,
and Buddhism into local tales.
Biography is not only a legitimating form of narrative, however; it can
also have a transformative nature, eliciting some change in the audience or
reader. in the context of religious biography or hagiography, appreciating the
exemplary behaviour of a senior religious figure – even if the example cannot
really be followed – can inspire a believer to better themselves. The inimitable
nature of the Bodhisatta’s perfection may inspire a lower level of imitation:
after appreciating the Bodhisatta’s gift of wealth, wife, children, limbs, and even
life, the gift of food to the monastery seems easy and minor. once again we can
see that the gradual path is open to anyone at any stage, and the greatness of
the other end (buddhahood or another form of awakening) is matched by the
modesty of the near end. in this way the jātaka stories are a way of resolving
the paradox of the Buddha’s inimitable example, since they contain advice for
early stages of the path in the form of teachings from the Buddha about his
past lives, whilst contributing to the glorification of the Buddha, Bodhisatta
and bodhi.
The ability of the Buddha’s biography to be an example is reliant upon its ability
to be both an account of a single person’s life and a general account of a path.
in other words, the Buddha/Bodhisatta must be both an individual and a ‘type’
and jātakas are also able to negotiate this balance. Sarah Shaw has examined the
complex layers of narrative voice in the JA, noting the typical structure of a third-
person account by an external narrator of the present situation, a third-person
account by the Buddha of one of his previous births, and then the only first-person
statement: ‘i was such-and-such a character at that time.’ She sums up:
The threads of the Buddha, described in the third person, the ‘he’ of the
Bodhisatta and the ‘i’ at the end of each tale are woven in and out of each
other like a plait, evoking a succession of lives. These three elements suggest
neither the ‘eternalist’ view, an abiding self, nor the ‘annihilationist’ view
that the self ceases at death … a moving point, like a kind of ‘middle way’,
arises from the process itself, in the constant movement between the first
ascetics retreat to the Himalayas, and young brahmins are sent to Takkasilā for their
education) locates most jātakas of the JA in North india, yet this rather spurious precision
betrays a lack of interest in the ‘true’ location of the events of the story. Where places
are specified, they are not given in detail, and many are not given a location at all. in the
early textual sources, the association between jātakas and specific places appears rather
irrelevant, making later associations all the more straightforward.

Jātakas and Biography 121
person acknowledgement of the Buddha and his third-person character, the
bodhisatta.34
in contrast to the complex layers of the jātakas in the JA, the jātakas related in the
Cariyāpiṭaka are straightforwardly in the first person. This is also the case for the
sections of the Apadāna that deal with the Buddha, as well as the Buddhavaṃsa
and its counterpart in the Nidānakathā. Shaw suggests that the move to a first-
person voice is because such events are exemplary and typological. in contrast,
the distance provided by the primarily third-person narration in the JA allows
the Bodhisatta to have a wider variety of roles and act out much more human
situations. Thus in jātakas the Bodhisatta can be both type and person.
another example of the layering of Buddhist biographical narrative has been
examined by Jonathan Silk. He notes the major paradox contained in traditional
life-stories of the Buddha, that ‘at birth he is virtually omniscient, but by
adolescence when he encounters the famous “four sights” – an old man, an ill
man, a corpse, and a mendicant – he does not know how to understand them’.35
Silk does not try to ‘solve’ this paradox through historical or philological enquiry,
but rather investigates the story’s ‘potential logic and the ways in which it may
be seen to resolve its own tensions creatively’.36 He concludes that there are two
levels on which the Buddha’s life story is operating:
First, there is a story of the result of an almost infinitely long career. in this
present and final lifetime the miraculous results of a long course of spiritual
development spanning multiple lifetimes finally come to full fruition. Second,
and almost entirely disjunct from the first, is a story of discovery, of encounter
with unpleasant facts of the reality of life in the world, the discovery of a means
to cope with those facts, and the efforts at asceticism and mental cultivation
that ultimately lead to liberation. Both of these levels to the story – and we
must keep in mind that there is, nevertheless, one and only one story – provide
elements of a model for the seeker, elements of a path that can be followed by
every Buddhist, models to emulate.37
The two models Silk outlines are, he argues, both essential for a person on
the Buddhist path, for this person may on one level feel inspired to emulate
the Bodhisatta’s renunciation, whilst on another level he or she is forced to
acknowledge that emulation is not entirely possible given the many lives in
34 Sarah Shaw, ‘and that was i: How the Buddha Himself Creates a Path between
Biography and autobiography’ in linda Covill et al. (eds), Lives Lived, Lives Imagined:
Biography in the Buddhist Traditions (Boston, 2010), pp. 15–47, pp. 36–7.
35 Jonathan a. Silk, ‘The Fruits of Paradox: on the Religious architecture of the
Buddha’s life Story’, Journal of the American Academy of Religion, 71/4 (2003): 863–81, p. 863.
36 Silk, ‘The Fruits of Paradox’, p. 866.
37 Silk, ‘The Fruits of Paradox’, p. 870.

122 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
which the Bodhisatta has been preparing for this moment.38 Thus, for Silk, the
‘blatant narrative inconsistencies in the story’ are evidence of the ‘profound
psychological insight of those ancient masters who composed or compiled it’.39
Silk’s investigation is not only instructive for its approach to narrative
layers, or its view that Buddha-biography is by nature exemplary. The two
models he identifies resonate with my earlier discussion of developments in the
nature of the paths to arahatship and buddhahood. Whereas jātaka stories may
have originally been associated only with the path to buddhahood as a way of
glorifying the Buddha, as the arahat path began to be extended and codified and
be seen as also stretching over many lives, such stories were able to provide a
model for all Buddhists, whatever ideal they aspired to. according to the early
ideal of arahatship, a simple realisation of the true nature of things can lead to
liberation, in the same way as it did for Gotama. on the other hand, Gotama, we
are told, had spent many lives preparing for this moment; so, it seems, must we.
Silk explicitly avoids a historical analysis of his materials, yet we can perhaps
see in his two models a reflection of this development in the proposed path
for the Buddha’s followers. Similarly, we might see the emergence of the two
biographical associations of the jātaka genre as a reflection of developments in
buddhology and the outlines of the Buddhist paths discussed in the previous
chapter. it is clear that biographical narrative has a creative role in making the
path accessible yet the person inimitable.
Jātaka texts, as a form of Bodhisatta- and Buddha-biography, are thus closely
linked to other biographical texts that rose in prominence during and shortly
after the time of asoka. These biographical texts together construct a complex
Buddhist past, in which the Bodhisatta’s actions are intertwined with those
of other buddhas and the past lives of arahats. The association between jātakas
and both Buddha and Bodhisatta, as established by the JA, allows the stories to
contain – and creatively resolve – several paradoxes about the Buddha: (1) he is
an inimitable exemplar; (2) he is a worshipped teacher; (3) his teachings and lives
were both local to particular times/places and are universal; (4) he is both a person
and a ‘type’. Jātaka stories provide examples for followers of the Buddha, whilst
simultaneously demonstrating the superiority of the Bodhisatta’s perfections;
they demonstrate both his superior teaching ability and the perfection acquired
over countless lives that makes him worthy of reverence; they link the universal
pattern of buddhahood and the universal application of the dhamma with local
places, peoples and concerns, as well as with a single individual’s long biography.
The ability of jātaka stories – through their biographical associations with
Buddha and Bodhisatta – to successfully negotiate these paradoxes, makes them
ideal sources for sermons, art and various Buddhist practices. it is these uses of
jātakas to which i will turn in the next chapter of our story.
38 Silk, ‘The Fruits of Paradox’, p. 871.
39 Silk, ‘The Fruits of Paradox’, p. 872.

Chapter 7
Jātaka ideology in Practice
it has become clear that there are two ways in which jātakas interact with the
Buddha’s biography, namely with him as Bodhisatta and with him as Buddha.
in one way the stories are necessarily buddhavacana, since they are episodes
remembered by the Buddha, and therefore they are both dhamma and Buddha-
biography; in another way they are stories about his previous births and
therefore Bodhisatta-biography. i have observed these two aspects in operation
in jātaka and related texts and examined the historical and doctrinal contexts
that provoked an increased interest in the biography of both Buddha and
Bodhisatta. in this chapter i will be examining how these two biographical
aspects of jātaka stories affect the ways in which the stories are interacted with
by believers and practitioners, and to what extent the association with Buddha-
dhamma and Bodhisatta-biography is necessary for practices involving jātakas to
be meaningful.
although i will be examining practices from all periods and regions, this
chapter is not intended to be an all-inclusive survey of Buddhist practices that
involve jātakas. Rather it presents a selection of case studies that demonstrate
the power and flexibility provided by the biographical associations that have
been woven into jātaka materials. my sources include Pāli scriptures and the
writings of Chinese pilgrims, ethnographic accounts of contemporary or near-
contemporary Southeast asia, and first-hand observation of contemporary Sri
lanka. Such examples of the uses of jātakas are not entirely confined to the
Theravāda tradition, although this does remain the focus. Where examples from
other schools of Buddhism are used, they are to provide useful context, and
support the more philosophical arguments presented in this chapter. The varied
geographical and historical contexts help to build up a general framework for
viewing the jātaka genre and its interaction with Buddhist practice. This chapter
therefore moves away from the strong historical and textual focus that has so far
dominated my discussion.
i will begin with perhaps the most widespread ‘use’ of jātaka stories: their
illustration in temples and at stūpa sites. Such use is by no means limited to
narrating the stories; the illustrations are also on occasions more iconographic
than narrative, and sometimes appear to have symbolic cosmological significance.
my examination of jātaka images will include some comments on the notion of
worshipping the dhamma(-kāya) of the Buddha, which i will then return to in an
investigation of the power and significance contained within the physical frame
of a jātaka book. This will be followed by a look at several practices that involve
using words from the jātaka book for apotropaic purposes. This discussion of the

124 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
jātaka as story, image, dhamma book and source of powerful words, will set the
scene for the examination of how jātaka text (as book and source of powerful
words) and image come together in a powerful ritual context. all these examples
persuasively suggest that jātakas have many and varied roles in Buddhist societies
that have little to do with the stories themselves, and much more to do with the
symbolism inherent in the very idea of what a jātaka is.
Because my interest here is primarily the biographical significance of the
jātaka genre as a whole, i will not be examining their merit as individual stories.
i do not mean to suggest that their use as didactic narratives in Theravāda
countries is unimportant; indeed the subject deserves a whole book of its
own. Such uses are many and varied; for example, as well as being popularly
depicted in children’s books and used in sermons, the stories have been used
in psychotherapy by a Sri lankan psychiatrist, Dr D.V.J. Harischandra, who has
also published a book surveying the psychiatric conditions and treatments
mentioned in the Jātaka-Pota and providing a host of examples of how he uses
jātaka stories in therapy (a practice he claims goes back to the Buddha himself).1
Several structural factors unique to jātaka stories might contribute to such uses,
and indeed in the previous chapter i noted the effects of the two biographical
associations (with Buddha and Bodhisatta) on the stories’ legitimative and
transformative effects. other aspects of the genre not related to biography also
play a part in making the stories meaningful, for example the use of humour,
allegorical symbolism, or typological characters and places. Such qualities of
the jātakas, though interesting, are outside the scope of this chapter. We must
instead turn our attention to the symbolic qualities of the genre, in other words
those qualities not related to narrative content but rather to the biographical
associations of the genre as a whole.
THe PoWeR oF THe imaGe
as well as the textual (and oral) traditions of telling jātakas, the stories have
been used as a source for the art of temple and stūpa sites since at least the time
of Bhārhut (second century bce). The practice of depicting jātaka stories has
continued to the present day, where they remain a popular theme for temple
illustrations in South and Southeast asia. Jātaka images have generally been
viewed as simple illustrations with the same didactic function as the stories
themselves. However, a few scholars, notably Robert Brown and Jonathan Walters,
have seen more significance than this in the portrayal of biographical narratives
in general, and jātakas in particular. in addition, analyses of the purpose and
function of images of the Buddha can help to illuminate the possible place of
jātaka images, especially when combined with an investigation of the place of
1 D.V.J. Harischandra, Psychiatric Aspects of Jataka Stories (Galle, Sri lanka, 1998).

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 125
buddha-biography and buddhahood at such sites, and the notion of the dhamma-
kāya in Theravāda Buddhism.
Illustrating the Narrative and Narrating the Illustration
most art historical analyses of jātaka portrayals tend to focus upon the
identification of the story and the source text, how the story is ‘narrated’
visually, and how the jātakas are arranged. Such studies treat the images as
narratives, and as Vidya Dehejia, for example, states: ‘Stories revolve around
actions, human or otherwise, that occur in space and unfold in time; for the
artist, the protagonists of a story, together with the elements of space and time,
are the three major components of narrative.’2 Dehejia proceeds to examine the
modes of visual narration of jātaka stories with these three elements in mind,
and categorises them thus: (1) monoscenic narrative, which identifies the story
with a single scene and leaves the viewer to narrate the story to themselves; (2)
synoptic narrative, where multiple episodes are shown within a single frame
with no temporal sequence communicated; (3) conflated narrative, where there
are again several scenes within one frame, but the figure of the protagonist is
shown only once, acting several parts in sequence; (4) continuous narrative,
which shows the protagonist in each scene, with temporal succession and spatial
movement clearly indicated; (5) linear narrative, which is essentially continuous
narrative but with frames separating the scenes from one another; (6) narrative
networks, where multiple scenes are shown in an apparently random order,
which may be dictated by concepts of geographical groupings or thematic
clusters. She suggests that different modes can suit different settings, for example
a continuous narration of the Buddha’s life might suit the wall encircling a stūpa,
where it could be viewed in sequence by a pilgrim circumambulating the shrine.
Some types of narration presuppose prior knowledge of the story, and serve
merely as hints or memory-joggers.3
Robert Brown’s ‘Narrative as icon’ contains a critique of Dehejia’s analysis,
but he does not criticise her categorisation. instead he criticises the assumption
(which also underlies most other analyses) that the images are designed to be
narrations or narrative prompts. Brown uses some of Dehejia’s own observations
to refute this assumption. First, he points out, many visual representations
are not organised as narratives (one might suggest that this applies to all of
Dehejia’s types except four and five) but instead are practically unreadable
or unrecognisable. Scenes are often grouped geographically or according to
causal links, and crucial scenes are often omitted, making the identification
of any narrative content very difficult. in addition, the reliefs are frequently
not accessible to the visitor, being situated high overhead or at foot level,
2 Vidya Dehejia, ‘on modes of Visual Narration in early Buddhist art’, The Art
Bulletin, 72/3 (1990): 374–92, p. 374.
3 Dehejia, ‘on modes of Visual Narration’.

126 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
narrated in the opposite direction to circumambulation, or even covered up.
The practicalities of ‘reading’ such illustrations as narratives, or using them
didactically, are problematic. For example, the ananda Temple at Pagan has 912
small plaques depicting all of the jātakas from the Pāli collection. Scholars have
suggested a didactic or proselytising purpose for these illustrations, despite the
fact that they are situated on the roof, unidentifiable from the ground.4
Brown insists that the meaning of jātaka reliefs is not to be found in their
textual context, but in the context of the monument. one example he cites is
Wat Si Chum, a fourteenth-century temple in Sukhothai, Thailand.5 This temple
contains illustrations of the first 100 jātakas of the JA carved in stone panels in
the ceiling of a dark, narrow passageway. Several scholars have suggested that
the jātaka illustrations must have been moved there from a more accessible
location, but it has recently been demonstrated that the carvings were custom-
made for this ceiling.6 The intention must therefore have been something other
than didactic narration of the stories. as Brown and others have suggested,
the presence of the illustrations in the passageway implies that they provide a
sense of progression, through following the footsteps of the Bodhisatta. Such an
association is supported by the illustration of a Buddha-footprint in the ceiling
above a turn in the stairs, before the jātakas continue from JA 34.7
Perhaps the most effective example used by Brown, which has also been
comprehensively studied by, amongst others, Paul mus, is Borobudur, a complex
eighth-/ninth-century mahāyāna monument in Java which contains reliefs of
jātakas, avadānas and biographical material from Śākyamuni’s final life, as well
as buddha images and stūpas. These images are arranged on concentric stepped
terraces surrounding a central stūpa. at the lowest level are reliefs illustrating
karmic consequences (from a text called the Karmavibhaṅga), but these are
hidden from view by an additional terrace. This illustrates neatly the idea that
these images do not need to be seen to justify their existence. instead, their

refers specifically to the work of Gordon luce. There is of course an argument that the
plaques are intended to be viewed by devas and other non-human beings, who are also
in need of Buddhist teaching. This argument cannot, however, be applied to all Brown’s

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 127
presence is necessary in relation to what follows higher up the stūpa, but this
has no relation to any perceived audience. The next level up shows jātakas and
avadānas,8 followed by biographical scenes from the Lalitavistara which narrate
as far as the Buddha’s first sermon. Further up, the story of Sudhana from the
Gaṇḍavyūha Sūtra is illustrated, showing Sudhana’s quest to find spiritual answers
and his eventual bodhisattva-vow made in the presence of the Bodhisattva
Samantabhadra. This is interspersed with and followed by buddha images, some
in perforated stūpas.
Brown analyses the arrangement of images at Borobudur both from the
outside in, and from the inside out. The pilgrim begins at the base, in the world
of desires, which are, however, covered and left behind. Next he must follow
in the steps of Śākyamuni Buddha through his past lives and final life. Next he
participates in Sudhana’s quest, maybe even in his vow to bodhisattvahood,
as he climbs ever higher towards the perfection (reflected in the ever more
perfect geometry of the terraces) of the Buddha/buddhahood. From the centre,
buddhahood is manifest first as ‘Ādibuddha’ hidden in the central stūpa, then
partially obscured in perforated stūpas, then fully visible as the (mahāyāna)
buddhas on the balustrades, and accessible in the reliefs portraying Śākyamuni’s
many lives. it is interesting that the images at Borobudur were clearly inspired
by some easily identifiable texts, yet, Brown insists, ‘even when the visual images
are organised to reflect the narrative nature of a verbal text (events related
through time), the visual images are there to express the Buddhistic nature of
the monument, to locate the Buddha and his teaching, not to tell a story’.9 at
Borobudur and in other examples presented, Brown sees images of jātakas as
parallel to images of the Buddha or buddhas, as a way of making his presence felt
and in order to inspire a similar spiritual quest. They form an intermediate stage
of purity between the glorious Buddha/buddhahood and the Buddhist pilgrim,
and do not have to be read to fulfil this.
8 Brown notes ‘Avadānas are stories of previous births that include individuals other
than the Buddha, but they function like jātakas and need not be differentiated for our
purposes’ (‘Narrative as icon’, p. 105, n. 52). However, avadānas are not explicitly part of
the Buddha’s biography in the same way that jātakas are, and thus cannot function in the
same way in Brown’s scheme. i have two suggestions for side-stepping this issue: (1) the
avadānas form a bridge between the biographical stories and those of the Karmavibhaṅga,
i.e. a bridge between the person of the Buddha and the practice of his path by others; (2)
as Strong argues in the same volume, there can be a ‘lateral expansion’ of biography in
the Buddhist tradition to include ‘not only the “histories” of identifiable karmic continua
over a series of lives, but also of ongoing karmic nexuses’. John S. Strong, ‘a Family Quest:
The Buddha, Yaśodharā, and Rāhula in the Mūlasarvāstivāda Vinaya’ in Juliane Schober
(ed.), Sacred Biography in the Buddhist Traditions of South and Southeast Asia (Honolulu, 1997),
pp. 113–28, p. 114. Thus since the Buddha is karmicly linked with his followers, their
biographies are in some way a part of his. This resonates with Walters’ argument about
participation in the Buddha’s biography presented later.
9 Brown, ‘Narrative as icon’, p. 80.

128 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Jonathan Walters, in ‘Stūpa, Story and empire’, has a similar suggestion to
Brown’s regarding the significance of early indian stūpa illustration. He takes
three types of evidence together in his examination of stūpa cults: images on stūpa
carvings, three interrelated texts he believes accompany the early post-asokan
stūpas (Cariyāpiṭaka, Buddhavaṃsa and Apadāna), and the donative inscriptions.
Walters begins with a purely textual analysis, looking at the development from
Cariyāpiṭaka (the first Buddha-biography to include his previous lives, or rather
the first explicitly biographical collection of jātaka stories) to Buddhavaṃsa
(which expands the scenario to include the lives of past and future buddhas) to
Apadāna (which contains the lives of eminent monks and nuns, in a catalogue of
acts and their results). He claims that this illustrates a growing universalisation
of Buddhist ideals. Next he examines the inscriptions on the stūpas, which
make it clear that the donors were from a variety of backgrounds, reflecting
the universalism of the texts. in addition, the stūpa itself is illustrated with the
cosmic Buddha biography: not just jātakas and scenes from Gautama’s life, but
also the scenes of stūpa worship. These last scenes are part of the biography
because we are told in the texts that such actions in this life will result in being
present at the time of a future buddha; similarly, previous actions such as this
have resulted in the opportunity to worship the stūpa of this buddha. Returning
to the inscriptions, Walters notes that the early inscriptions state only the
name of the donor(s), omitting the later trend for extolling the merit of giving
and specifying who will receive the merit accrued. The process of funding and
creating the stūpas was doubtless very complex, so there must have been an
understanding of the merits, and Walters argues that this was provided by the
texts, particularly the Apadāna and its catalogue of merit-making. later, when
the popularity of these texts waned, more explicit donative inscriptions were
required. in conclusion, Walters suggests that stūpas should in fact be viewed
as a cosmic buddha-biography, containing the rūpakāya (in the relic) and the
dharmakāya (in the illustrations). This can parallel Brown’s analysis of the Buddha
‘manifest’ in the images and ‘unmanifest’ in the relic/stūpa.10 Both scholars see
the inclusion of such images as a result of the struggle to maintain the presence
of the Buddha in a post-parinibbāna world.
Both Brown and Walters see jātaka images as fulfilling a symbolic role, rather
than, or as well as, a narrative one, and this is related to the place of jātakas in
the extended biography of the Buddha. Their fulfilment of this role would not
be possible were they not stories about the Bodhisatta who later becomes the
Buddha. Thus for Brown and Walters the whole of stūpa (and, for Brown, temple)
10 Here Brown draws a direct comparison with representations of Hindu deities,
which have long been accepted as being a ‘manifest’ form of a normally ‘unmanifest’ deity.
He notes, in passing, that ‘in part the Buddha manifests himself in terms of a history, a
biography (and this includes jātaka tales) much more strongly than do the Hindu deities,
who manifest themselves in mythic terms, often of a cosmic nature’ (‘Narrative as icon’,
pp. 74–5).

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 129
symbolism is focused upon the person of the Buddha. This is also true for mus,
who argues that the long career of Śākyamuni is the focus even at monuments
that appear to have cosmological symbolism, for ‘the cosmic planes will always
be characterised by the personal adventures of the Buddha’.11 For mus, the
jātakas are a stage closer to the devotee, and thus contrast with the figure of
the Buddha, especially in the earlier so-called ‘aniconic’ phase of Buddhist art.
in this phase, he argues, a devotee circumambulating the stūpa would see the
Buddha as a Bodhisatta time and time again, but the primary figure vanishes
as he reaches his final life.12 as mus points out, the absence of any figure of the
Buddha only highlights the extent to which his person is the focus of the image.
Regardless of the slightly differing detail of their analyses, for all three of these
scholars the person of the Buddha, also as Bodhisatta, is of central importance in
understanding the symbolism of sacred sites.
A Little Assistance from Buddha Images
Since the person of the Buddha is clearly important in interpreting temple and
stūpa sites, i might here stray productively into scholarship on Buddha images,
in particular the work of Jacob Kinnard, whose book Imaging Wisdom will lead
us neatly back to jātakas. When it comes to interpreting the significance of
sacred images and the worship of such images, there are two basic views: either
the image is or becomes the sacred, or it is a symbol or representation. Kinnard
argues that, rather than seeing these two views in opposition, it is possible to see
images as bringing together the two positions in some way:
Practicing Buddhists are able to maintain at once that the Buddha is present in
such things as relics, sculptures and paintings, and at the same time able to hold
the conflicting (and seemingly contradictory) view that it is only the emblem,
or reminder, of the no-longer present Buddha that they worship. in other
words they are able simultaneously to maintain an ontological and a semantic/
symbolic position on the presence of the Buddha. and, what is more … they use
sculptural images as a means of reflecting on the tensions between these two
simultaneously held positions.13
11 Paul mus, Barabuḍur: Sketch of a History of Buddhism Based on Archaeological Criticism
of the Texts, trans. alexander W. macdonald (New Delhi, 1998), p. 94.
12 mus, Barabuḍur, p. 70ff.
13 Jacob N. Kinnard, Imaging Wisdom: Seeing and Knowing in the Art of Indian Buddhism
(Richmond, 1999), p. 11. We might compare Gombrich’s idea that to Theravāda Buddhists
the Buddha is ‘cognitively’ absent, but ‘affectively’ present (Richard F. Gombrich, Precept
and Practice: Traditional Buddhism in the Rural Highlands of Ceylon (oxford, 1971) with
particular regard to relics see p. 105). However, Kinnard’s contribution is to replace this
dichotomy with a dialectic. Strong sees a similar dialectic in Buddhist relics, which ‘toy

130 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Kinnard uses Thomas Kasulis’ notion of metapraxis – reflection on how what is
doctrinally true relates to actual practice14 – as a way of analysing the many
ways in which images function. He argues that images are actually a form of
metapractical reflection, bridging the gap between the absence and presence of
the Buddha.
as an example of metapractical discourse in relation to Buddha images,
Kinnard cites the legendary saint Upagupta’s request that newly converted māra
take the form of the Buddha to ease what Strong calls Upagupta’s ‘rūpalogical
deficiency’ (he has seen the Buddha’s dharma-kāya but wishes also to see his rūpa-
kāya).15 although Upagupta knows that the image of the Buddha he sees before
him is not actually the Buddha, he still bows down before māra. as Kinnard
analyses this:
We can now see Upagupta’s response to māra as reflective of the dynamic
interplay between metaphysics, praxis, and metapraxis: ‘of course, i know
that the Best of Speakers has gone altogether to extinction [metaphysics] … i
bow down before that Sage [praxis]. But i do not revere you! [metapraxis]’.16
Thus, far from being stuck in a simple dichotomy between belief and practice,
images are part of an active discourse between them. images have power precisely
because of this tension, because of the possibility of mistaking the image for the
real Buddha. For Kinnard, the reality of the Buddha’s absence and the desire
to be in his presence are mutually contributive: ‘it is only with the positing of
absence that presence becomes important, and thus the significance of the
Buddha’s presence is always caught up in the ultimate reality of his absence.’17
or, as Kevin Trainor puts it, ‘it is precisely because the Buddha is not present that
he is worthy of offerings’.18
with the opposition between “gone” and “not gone”’ (John S. Strong, Relics of the Buddha
(Princeton and oxford, 2004), p. 239).
14 Kasulis defines his terms thus: ‘By “metaphysics,” we will mean simply the
development of a philosophical theory about the nature of reality. Basing our sense of
the term on its philological components instead of historical etymology, we can say
metaphysics theorizes about what lies “behind” or “beyond” (meta) “natural things and
powers” ( physis). By analogy, “metapraxis” is the development of a philosophical theory
about the nature of a particular praxis, in this case, religious praxis. it theorizes about
what lies behind or beyond the practices of a religious tradition. Thomas P. Kasulis,
‘Philosophy as metapraxis’ in Frank Reynolds and David Tracy (eds), Discourse and Practice
(albany, 1992), pp. 169–95, p. 174.
15 John S. Strong, The Legend and Cult of Upagupta (Princeton, 1992) discussed in
Kinnard, Imaging Wisdom, p. 31.

18 Kevin Trainor, Relics, Ritual, and Representation in Buddhism: Rematerialising the Sri

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 131
already we can hear an echo of mus’ theory about how both aniconic
representations of the Buddha, and representations of his previous lives,
function. Both, mus argues, help to bridge the gap between the glorious, absent
Buddha, and the possibility of following in his footsteps. The relevance of such
arguments to my own study might be stated as follows: just as Buddha images
mediate between the Buddha’s absence and presence, jātakas mediate between a
historical person that it is possible for Buddhists to emulate, and the marvellous
and incomparable glory of the Buddha/buddhahood. Thus they each form a part
of the link between Buddhist practitioner and buddhahood. Jātakas also mediate
between universal paradigmatic truths (of buddhahood or nibbāna, demonstrated
through the association with the 24 previous buddhas, or, in mahāyāna, the
bodhisattvas) and the particular instance of the Buddha who founded the present
buddha-sāsana after many years of preparation.
Kinnard’s theory about the metapractical importance of sacred images
actually forms a preface to the main thrust of his argument, that in Pāla-period
Buddhism, the priority of prajñā influenced sculptural art, and can help to
explain it. He traces the rise in the idea that Śākyamuni is an embodiment of
prajñā, and thus that prajñāpāramitā is the source of the Tathāgatas, the equation
of prajñāpāramitā with the dharma-kāya, and the consequent rise in worship of
both book and deity. in conclusion he says:
the visual and textual data i have assembled and analyzed here not only
demonstrates that prajñā was a central part of Buddhist discourse and practice
during the Pāla period, but also that the visual representation and presentation
of wisdom – as opposed to only the textual/intellectual discourse that is so
frequently the object of our inquiries – was of great importance to Pāla-period
Buddhists. That is, Pāla-period Buddhists did not only want to think about
the Buddha; they also wanted to see him, and to be in his presence (and, as
i have argued, to think about the nature of this presence). indeed, it is the
amalgamation of these two faculties, seeing and knowing, that is at the root of
Pāla-period visual practice.19
as an analysis of medieval North indian mahāyāna Buddhist practice, Kinnard’s
argument might seem far removed from the context of Theravāda jātakas.
However, he highlights an important distinction: between worshipping (or
perhaps ‘appreciating’) the dhamma as something to be learned (internalised)
and worshipping the dhamma as an image of wisdom, with the idea that ‘seeing’
such an image is in some way equivalent to ‘seeing’ (internalising) the dhamma.
19 Kinnard, Imaging Wisdom, p. 177. Kinnard provides a good discussion of the
importance assigned to seeing the Buddha as an aid to seeing the dhamma (pp. 57–64). See
also Trainor (Relics, Ritual and Representation, especially p. 173ff.) for an analysis of much
of the same material in relation to the functional equivalence of seeing a relic and seeing
the Buddha in Sri lankan Theravāda.

132 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Such a distinction is applicable to my examination of jātaka images too, for they
are both transmitters of, and symbols of, the dhamma. i will be returning to this
idea shortly.
For Kinnard, the ability of the prajñāpāramitā literature and deity to embody
the dhamma is based upon the conviction that the Buddha (and indeed – in the
mahāyāna context – all buddhas) is the embodiment of prajñāpāramitā. This prompts
me to make another connection with jātakas, for, as i showed in earlier chapters,
jātaka stories become strongly associated with the 10 pāramitās, to the extent that
these become the main rationale for the jātaka genre before even the time of the
closing of the Theravāda canon. in Southeast asia the perfections, as exemplified
in the final 10 jātakas of the JA, also form the basis for practices involving jātakas,
as discussed below. if the JA, selections of it, or the jātaka genre more broadly,
can be conceived of as representing or somehow embodying the perfections
that not only make the Buddha a buddha but also allow Buddhists to follow in his
path, then jātaka literature is not so different from the prajñāpāramitā literature
of Pāla-period Buddhism that Kinnard examines. This level of interpretation
might provide the key to understanding why it is jātakas that are the subject of
illustrations on temple and stūpa sites throughout the Buddhist world.
Dhamma and Dhamma-kāya: Narrative and Symbol
Jātakas, i have argued, can have symbolic meaning as representations of the
Buddhist path because they both portray the Bodhisatta’s biography and transmit
the Buddha’s dhamma. We have so far given very little attention to the idea that
jātaka images have a didactic function, but in fact in many sites this is clearly a
preoccupation. Brown is perhaps a little too keen to highlight the non-narrative
functions of jātaka illustrations almost to the exclusion of any narrative value,
though he is quite right to draw our attention to the other reasons for their
presence. Walters’ investigation relies rather more on the narrative aspect of
the stories interacting with the donors, images and stūpa. For Walters it is the
fact that the donor and the visitor play a part in the narration of the Buddha’s
biography that is crucial to an understanding of the sites. as well as providing a
narrative frame for a devotee’s interaction with a sacred site, however, i would
argue that the simple didactic narrative value of jātaka illustrations is an essential
counterpart to the more symbolic interpretations. in order to see this, we must
turn our attention to the idea of the dhamma-kāya.
The Buddha’s admonition of the monk Vakkali, when the latter expresses his
sadness at not having been able to see the Buddha, is well known:
enough Vakkali! What is the point of you seeing this vile body? Vakkali, he
who sees the dhamma sees me; he who sees me sees the dhamma.20
20 Trans. from the version in the Vakkali Sutta of the Samyutta Nikāya (l. Feer (ed.),
Saṃyutta-nikāya (london, 1884–1898), vol. 3, p. 120).

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 133
Some scholars have taken this passage as evidence that the Buddha did not
wish to be worshipped, but merely wanted his followers to follow his teachings.
Whatever the reality of this interpretation, in fact this equation of Buddha and
dhamma seems to be the root of the idea that the Buddha can be worshipped
through his dhamma, and indeed vice versa. We might here see the origins of
the idea of the dhamma-kāya, a term rarely found in Pāli but well-known in the
context of later mahāyāna theories of the Buddha’s three bodies.21
Whatever its later meanings, the dhamma-kāya is initially a reference purely
to the body of teachings of the Buddha. Paul Harrison urges us to remember this
even when examining early and middle mahāyāna texts and practices, where
the term becomes caught up in the trikāya theory. He tracks down all the early
occurrences of the term (it is rarely found in the Pāli canon,22 though there are
some references in the Chinese āgamas and later texts) and insists that in all
these it refers to the teachings/truth of the Buddha, not to a spiritual body. He
concludes:
as far as the Buddhists who wrote the texts were concerned, what was
important was the identification of the Buddha with the dharma or dharmas, of
the Teacher with the truth which he taught or the principles which he realised,
considered either in the abstract, or concretely embodied in scripture. a
simple equation perhaps, but with far-reaching consequences, especially for
Buddhist cult-practice, in which the cult of relics eventually coalesced with
the cult of the book.23
Harrison’s reference to the cult of relics and the cult of the book is of particular
interest here, since he sees the origin of their amalgamation in the idea that the
dhamma-kāya (in the form of a book) is equivalent in status and function to the
rūpa-kāya (in the form of a relic).
That a book containing the teachings of the Buddha can have symbolic and
ritual functions in a similar manner to a physical relic of the Buddha is attested
in contemporary Theravāda practice, as discussed below. it is also found in
medieval indian practice, such as that described by Kinnard, and also by Daniel
Boucher, who charts the influence of the pratītyasamutpādagāthā during the same
period. This is the verse used to convert Sāriputta and moggallāna, who were
21 For an analysis of the importance of the Buddha’s many bodies in the Theravāda
tradition see Frank e. Reynolds, ‘The Several Bodies of Buddha: Reflections on a Neglected
aspect of Theravada Tradition’, History of Religions, 16/4 (1977): 374–89.
22 Harrison suggests only once in the whole canon, but Peter Harvey has drawn my
attention to three further references in the Apadāna (1.13, 1.532 and 1.168) identified by
C. Jantrasrisalai in Early Buddhist Dhammakāya: Its Philosophical and Soteriological Significance
(University of Sydney Ph.D. Thesis, 2007).
23 Paul Harrison, ‘is the Dharma-kāya the Real “Phantom Body” of the Buddha?’,
Journal of the International Association of Buddhist Studies, 15/1 (1992): 44–94, p. 76.

134 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
to become the Buddha’s chief disciples. it is recorded in the Mahāvagga that the
monk assaji repeated the verse to Sāriputta, who then repeated it to moggallāna.
The pratītyasamutpādagāthā is said to embody the dhamma in a single verse, and
it therefore formed the focus for medieval practices of inscribing tablets to be
enshrined in stūpas. There is even textual evidence of the practice, including
the declaration that he who sees pratītyasamutpāda (conditioned arising) sees the
dharma, and he who sees the dharma sees the Tathāgata.24 like Kinnard, Boucher
sees this practice as a way of negotiating the absence and presence of the Buddha,
without committing to one or other camp. He argues that ‘by transforming
the notion of Buddha-as-dharma into relic, medieval Buddhists were able to
harmonise the impulse to fix the Buddha in real time and space with the long-
established scholastic definitions that, apparently, could not be ignored’.25 Robert
Campany writes that during the same period in China, Buddhist hagiographies
and miracle tales provide ample evidence for the idea that ‘sūtra texts embody
the beings whom they describe and whose words they contain; to harm the
texts is to harm the beings and to incur the karmic retribution appropriate to
such a heinous act … The Buddha, his teaching, Buddha images, and sūtra texts:
all seem to have been regarded – or at least were represented – as functionally
interchangeable’.26 He notes that the equation of text with Buddha or bodhisattva
means that devotional uses of texts are far more prominent than the injunction
to read or understand them. Yet, as with the pratītyasamutpādagāthā, it is the
contents of the texts that allow, and even encourage, identification as an object
of veneration.
What i have been trying to illustrate through these examples is that the notion
of dhamma-kāya as an object of veneration is inextricably linked to the content
of the dhamma. in other words, although the practices involve identifying the
dhamma with the Buddha, and thus are not concerned with reading/hearing
the contents of the dhamma, this is possible (and efficacious) only because the
Buddha is venerated because of the teachings he left behind. in theory any portion of
teachings could be considered dhamma-kāya, yet those most popular as objects of
devotion have a special reason to be thus singled out. For Campany’s examples,
the texts are associated with (and therefore embody) a particular bodhisattva;
in the case of the pratītyasamutpādagāthā, it is the fact that the realisation of
conditioned arising is seen as the very essence of awakening that promotes its
veneration; for the prajñāpāramitā literature, prajñā is the essence of bodhi.
24 Daniel Boucher, ‘The Pratītyasamutpādagāthā and its Role in the medieval Cult of
the Relics’, Journal of the International Association of Buddhist Studies, 14/1 (1991): 1–27, p. 11.
He mentions an explicit reference in the āryapratītyasamutpādanāmamahāyānasūtra as
well as more implicit references elsewhere.
25 Boucher, ‘The Pratītyasamutpādagāthā’, p. 16.
26 Robert F. Campany, ‘Notes on the Devotional Uses and Symbolic Functions of Sūtra
Texts as Depicted in early Chinese Buddhist miracle Tales and Hagiographies’, Journal of
the International Association of Buddhist Studies, 14/1 (1991): 28–72, p. 43.

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 135
i’d like to suggest that to a lesser extent the jātaka genre also encourages
its own veneration, thanks to its dual associations with Buddha and dhamma.
The Buddha’s biography is of central importance because of his role as exemplar
and teacher, and this importance is reflected in strong biographical emphasis in
temple and stūpa art. Jātakas clearly have a symbolic role in these sites, but their
ability to do so is reliant upon the fact that they are also part of the dhamma that
simultaneously demonstrates the great teaching ability of the Buddha and the
greatness of the Bodhisatta. as part of both the exemplary biography and the
teachings, i would therefore argue that, like the other examples i have presented,
jātakas have a special status within the dhamma-kāya.
Returning to Images and Some Conclusions
To return to temple images, an example might help to demonstrate what i
mean by the assertion that jātaka images can be both part of a cosmic Buddha
biography, and a simple exposition of the dhamma. in the tradition of the Kandyan
School of temple art,27 found mostly in the Central Province of Sri lanka from
the eighteenth to early nineteenth centuries, jātakas form a popular subject for
illustrations. Such temples are often lavishly illustrated with jātaka stories at a
perfectly convenient height and in a coherent progressive narrative, traditionally
in horizontal bands across the wall, and often with captions in Sinhala. even
today such illustrations form the subject of sermons; they are also a handy way
of imparting a little understanding of Buddhism to any visitors who should
chance their way. other popular subjects for depiction in such temples are: the
life of the Buddha (in narrative bands like the jātakas); the defeat of māra (in a
large single scene); the request of the gods to the Bodhisatta to take birth and
become Buddha (in a large single scene); the Bodhisatta receiving his prediction
to buddhahood at the feet of each of the 24 previous buddhas (24 panels in grid
form); the events of the seven weeks after enlightenment (in consecutive panels);
and the 16 sacred places in Sri lanka (also in panels). all of these subjects, but
especially the latter two, are portrayed in a highly stylised and symbol-heavy
form, in contrast to the jātakas, where, although the distinctive style of the
Kandyan School is still apparent, Dehejia’s three elements – protagonist, time
and place – are all clearly defined.
it would be difficult to deny the narrative purpose of the jātaka images in
such temples, yet when taken in the context of the other paintings and images,
it is clear that there are other factors influencing their position. Such a temple
arrangement demonstrates that the choice of location for each subject was
determined by both practical and ideological considerations. Certainly jātakas
27 i am deeply indebted to Ven. Wimalagnana, a student and visiting lecturer in
the Department of Pāli and Buddhist Studies at the University of Peradeniya, for many
conversations on the Kandyan School – the subject of his own research – and for helping
me to gain access to many of the paintings during my visits to Sri lanka.

136 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
are always portrayed in narrative bands on easily-viewable portions of the
walls, but they are also ideologically linked to the other subjects through being
biographical in nature. in Kandyan temple art, subjects for illustration are all
in some way biographical – in the broader definition, in other words to do with
the life of the Bodhisatta and Buddha and his relics – and each has its traditional
place. For example, a painting of the Buddha’s defeat of māra’s army or the gods’
entreaty to the Bodhisatta in Tusita heaven will be on the ceiling, with the defeat
of māra having pride of place directly above the main Buddha image. Jātakas,
by contrast, are most often on the outer walls, or the walls that divide the main
shrine from the porch area, so that in many temples there is a definite feeling of
progression inwards towards buddhahood. Jātaka images in this context therefore
function both as didactic narrative demonstrations of dhamma, and (collectively)
a representation of the Buddha. Thus, i argue, it is possible for jātaka images that
are used as teaching aids to also take their place in the wider symbolic structure
of temple art. Just as the symbol-heavy style of Kandyan period painting does not
damage the narrative value of the jātaka illustrations, so the symbolic role these
illustrations play in the wider temple complex need not exclude the possibility
of the illustrations also being read as stories.
it is of course important to remember that jātaka images – as in the case of
jātaka stories and texts – need not have performed the same function in all cases
at all times; indeed, a careful study of the various ways in which jātaka images are
presented and ordered can reveal a variety of emphases at a variety of sites. in
some cases, changes in emphasis over time can be seen, providing evidence for
changes in the make-up or preferences of the local community of the temple or
site. For example, Hsio-Yen Shih notes a development in the Dunhuang murals
and concludes:
The three early caves 275, 254 and 257 have been interpreted as gradually
evolving from the use of narrative paintings as reminders to the monastic
community of the fundamental ideal of bodhisattvahood so exemplified in
Siddhārtha, and the steps necessary to spiritual advancement. By the sixth
century appeal to laity became an additional factor in the increasing popularity
of avadāna for narrative depiction and its advocacy of bhakti worship.28
Shih argues that this demonstrates a change in the nature of the Dunhuang
community and the role of its cave temples. as another example we might
compare the preoccupations at Bhārhut and Pagan, through their inscriptions.
at Bhārhut, titles are given for the jātakas illustrated, frequently using the term
jātakaṃ, but without any obvious emphasis upon the character or nature of
the Bodhisatta. in contrast, at several of the temples in Pagan (the eleventh-
28 Hsio-Yen Shih, ‘Readings and Re-Readings of Narrative in Dunhuang murals’,
Artibus Asiae, 53 (1993): 59–88, p. 88.

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 137
to thirteenth-century capital of Burma) which illustrate all 547/550 jātakas,29
the captions give not only the title but also the character which is identified as
the Bodhisatta.30 There is even evidence for the creation of images only of the
Bodhisatta as found in each story, in some fifteenth-century bronze statues from
ayutthaya.31 These Southeast asian sites demonstrate a developed focus upon
the role of the stories as part of the Bodhisatta’s biography.
i have here examined two complementary ideas about the significance of
jātaka images. First, the jātakas are part of the sacred and cosmic biography of
the Buddha, and they symbolically provide cosmological completeness and an
intermediate stage of purity which parallels the long path to arahatship. Second,
they are the dhamma-kāya of the Buddha, shown on stūpas as a partner to the relics,
and in temples as a partner to the image of the Buddha. Neither of these layers of
meaning requires that they have some didactic function, though in many cases
they do, and this only serves to increase their ideological significance, since it
is the content of the jātakas that gives them their power as symbols of Buddha,
dhamma and buddhahood. The very representation of the dhamma (which is the
Buddha) has a place in the network of sacred symbols present at a temple or
stūpa site. The image does not necessarily need to be ‘read’ or even viewed in
order for it to perform its role: its very creation (for the donor) and existence
(for the pilgrims and monks) are enough.
THe PoWeR oF THe TexT
The non-narrative function of jātaka images can help to illuminate the many
functions which the jātaka narratives and texts themselves appear to have. on
an individual level the jātakas are of course undeniably narratives, but that
does not exclude the possibility that they do something other than tell a story.
For example, as a genre, they might fulfil the same need for some manifest
form of post-parinibbāna Buddha as the images. They also represent, rather
than merely describe, the path to bodhi. one might even go so far as to suggest
that they perform this function even without an audience, as in the case of the
29 Some of the temples follow the numbering familiar in Fausbøll and other editions,
but the West Petleik inserts three more after 496 (the last of the Pakiṇṇaka Nipāta): Velāma
497, Mahāgovinda 498, Sumedhapaṇḍita 499, making a full 550 in total (G.H. luce, ‘The 550
Jātakas in old Burma’, Artibus Asiae, 19 (1956): 291–307, p. 294f.).
30 See luce, ‘The 550 Jātakas’, pp. 292–4. out of the seven sets of illustrations he

images: their creation and presence are more important that the fact that they
are read; and the fact that they are jātakas is more important than that they
are read as such.
in Reverend Clough’s Sinhala–english dictionary the entry for ‘Jātaka-
pota’ records: ‘work of great celebrity among the buddhists … this book is so
sacred amongst the buddhists that they will offer to it and worship it’.32 Such
book-worship is not unheard of in Theravāda countries, though it is more
commonly associated with the development of mahāyāna Buddhism. although
the Theravāda scriptural corpus may not contain texts which so explicitly
enumerate the benefits of copying, reciting and honouring themselves as in, for
example, prajñāpāramitā literature, historical records, contemporary practices
and manuscript colophons all indicate that such ideas were not alien. an
anecdote may help to illustrate this.
on a visit to the Temple of the Tooth in Kandy in 2007 my tour guide
enthusiastically showed me a copy of the Jātaka-pota that was displayed in
a glass cabinet in the temple library. it was proudly labelled: ‘This Pansiya
Panasjathakaya, 550 life Stories of the Buddha’s Former Births, Composed &
Written on 1600 ola leaves 960 years ago.’ Whilst pilgrims paid their respects and
tourists took photographs, i was naturally suspicious. The title was in Sinhala,
and so presumably referred to the Sinhala reworking of the JA, which is dated
to the fourteenth century, substantially less than 960 years ago. The label was
ambiguous as to whether the manuscript itself was claimed to be so old; the tour
guides, however, boldly declared it to be the oldest manuscript on the island, a
claim that even a cursory look indicated was false. a few weeks later i was lucky
enough to be allowed to look closely at the manuscript, which was removed from
the cabinet and opened especially, much to the joy of passing devotees who were
able to touch it. Upon inspection, however, the manuscript turned out to be a
copy, in Pāli, of the Khuddaka Nikāya, dated (in the cover) to 1886. Chuckling,
the senior monk who had very kindly arranged for me to view the manuscript
suggested that the monks had probably just assumed it was the Jātaka-pota
because of its size; in any case, he said, the public like it to be the Jātaka-pota. on
a subsequent visit three months later, the label remained unchanged.
like the worship of images and relics discussed above, devotion towards
manuscripts might be interpreted in many different ways. Some might suggest
that there is no ‘magical’ benefit from reverentially touching a manuscript
of the jātakas, as many lay Buddhists joyfully did on the occasion of my visit
to the Temple of the Tooth, but that the act merely reinforces in the mind of
the devotee a determination to follow the teachings of the Buddha embodied
in the text. others talk of acquiring merit from such acts, as they are equated
with worship of the Buddha. Both of these options are found in the examples
of scribal colophons to the JA that i examined in Chapter 5: in some cases the
32 Rev. B. Clough, Dictionary of English and Singhalese, and Singhalese and English
Languages, vol. 2 (Colombo, 1830).

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 139
scribe wished to follow the path exemplified by the Bodhisatta, or taught in the
stories; in many cases, however, the merit accrued from the act of copying the
text was mentioned. of course, in the case of colophons, the scribes have read
the text, inasmuch as they have copied the words, yet it is not the reading that
brings the benefits. These two aspects are not incompatible: just as the image of
a book (whether as an associated deity or as illustrations, as discussed above)
can be held up as both a symbol of the Buddha (and bodhi) and as an example
of the dhamma, so too can the physical manuscript or book.33 in Kandy, the
other manuscripts contained in this temple library were rammed in cupboards
all around the room, and none attracted the attention of the (assumed) Jātaka-
Pota, implying that it contains something other texts do not. i suggested some
possible reasons for this during our investigation of the dhamma-kāya in relation
to image and relic worship, and i would argue here that once again it is the
two biographical associations of the jātaka book that promote and explain such
devotion. an additional factor might of course be a widespread familiarity with
the contents of the text, which doubtless contributes to its popularity, though it
is difficult to see how it might contribute to its sanctity.
THe PoWeR oF THe WoRDS
even in the case of oral/aural interactions with jātakas, it is not necessarily
important to understand the words of the story, or to learn from them. For
example, Hayashi writes that the monks’ recitation of jātakas at the Bun Phrawet
festival in Northeast Thailand is ‘more like music than a sermon’ and that the
audience, though familiar with the story, respond more to the narrative style
of the monk, with its special intonation and rhythm.34 This ritual recitation of
the Vessantara-jātaka, which takes place across Thailand, Burma and the Shan
States, has also been studied by Tambiah in the village of Phraan muan. Tambiah
notes that ‘villagers count Bun Phraawes as merit from listening to a sermon’35
and that the recitation of the jātaka confers good fortune on the villagers, and
ensures plentiful rain. He notes that ‘a problem is posed as to the mechanics of
the Buddhist ritual – how the use of sacred words which deal with the virtues of
renunciation transfers to the participants the seemingly opposite benefits of life

subject of systematic cults of veneration, as has been suggested in the case of certain
mahāyāna texts. What i am suggesting is that in some way the jātaka book has greater
sanctity for Sinhalese Buddhists than other Buddhist texts.

p. 134.
35 S.J. Tambiah, ‘Bun Phraawes’ in Frank e. Reynolds and Jason a. Carbine (eds), The
Life of Buddhism (Berkeley and los angeles, 2000), pp. 62–9, p. 62.

affirmation’.36 Though he offers no solution, it is clear from both accounts of the
ritual that the recitation of the Vessantara-jātaka contains some form of power
that can be directed at worldly ends. Such ends can also be more in line with
Buddhist soteriology; for example the Thai text Phra Malai states that listening
to a recitation of the Vessantara-jātaka can lead to rebirth at the time of metteyya
Buddha.37 as in the case of the scribal colophons, both merit and soteriological
progress are implicit benefits of hearing (not understanding) or copying (not
reading) a jātaka.
in Thailand the first syllable of the titles of the final 10 jātakas of the JA form
a mnemonic: te ja su ne ma bhu cha na vi ve.38 Textual mnemonics (called hua chai
– ‘heart’ in Thai) exist for the Tipiṭaka as a whole, as well as various sections of
it, and teachings contained within it. Thus, for example, the four ariyasaccāni are
condensed to ‘du sa ni ma’ (for dukkha – unsatisfactoriness, samudāya – arising,
nirodha – cessation, and magga – path). Skilling notes that such mnemonics have
many different functions, including protective ones.39 Since the final 10 jātakas
are said to exemplify in the highest form the Bodhisatta’s acquisition of the 10
perfections, they might be seen as having significance because of an association
with bodhi. it is also possible that this particular mnemonic is able to provide
protection because of the association with the (very powerful) perfections. after
all, in many jātakas we hear of how the power of the Bodhisatta’s good practice
made Sakka’s throne heat up, or caused an earthquake. it might also serve to aid
the internalisation of the perfections it symbolises.
The Thai mnemonic, if it is a safeguard, would not be the only jātaka-sourced
protective, for jātakas are also included in paritta ceremonies. The Pāli term paritta
( pirit in Sinhala) means ‘protection’ and is used to refer to a body of texts that are
chanted for their protective powers at all important life events such as birthdays,
new jobs, weddings, and at times of sickness. Paritta texts and rituals date to
before the compilation of the Milindapañhā, which contains a discussion of their
efficacy. in addition, there are references to paritta in the Pāli commentaries,
for example the prose of the Telapatta-jātaka ( JA 96) contains a description of a
paritta ceremony, leading Gombrich to conclude that ‘no important feature of a
modern pirit ceremony post-dates commentarial times’.40 as with many Buddhist
practices, however, both texts and Buddhists claim that the origins go back to
36 Tambiah, ‘Bun Phraawes’, p. 68.
37 Collins, Nirvana, pp. 375–8.

the Buddha himself. Two particular paritta texts that have their source in the
Sutta-piṭaka are often cited in defence of this position: in the Khanda Sutta of the
Aṅguttara Nikāya41 the Buddha recommends a verse expressing loving kindness
towards snakes as a remedy against snakebites, and in the Aṅgulimāla Sutta of
the Majjhima Nikāya42 aṅgulimāla makes a saccakiriya (‘act of truth’) in order to
ease the suffering of a woman who has been in labour for seven days. The verse
uttered by aṅgulimāla is still chanted to women in labour today.
The texts chanted for protective purposes are collected together in
the Catubhāṇavarapāli (‘text of the four recitals’, also known in Sinhala as
Piruvānapotvahanse, ‘the venerable recitation book’), which contains 29 suttas,
of which three are of central importance. These are, as lily de Silva describes
them: ‘the Ratana Sutta dealing with the invocation of blessings by the power of
the truth (sacca) of the infallible virtues of the Buddha, Dhamma, and the Saṅgha,
the Metta Sutta, advocating the cultivation of loving kindness (metta) towards all
sentient beings, and the Mahāmaṅgala Sutta describing social ethics’.43 She links
these main paritta suttas with the overall rationale for the efficacy of recitations
of such texts by identifying truth and loving kindness as the core themes of
paritta suttas. as she notes, truth-telling can have the power to override natural
laws, and loving kindness has great protective power.44 The importance of the
person of the Buddha – as one who demonstrates and teaches sacca and mettā
– in the efficaciousness of the paritta ceremony is demonstrated by the ritual
apparatus. During a paritta ceremony, the presence of the Buddha is represented
by his relics, of the dhamma by (a physical copy of) the paritta text and the saṅgha
by the monks present.45 all three aspects are physically linked to the thread
which is later tied around the wrists of devotees to provide protection.
The power of the truth in protecting Buddhists, that is so central to the
efficacy of paritta, is also demonstrated by a popular yantra in Thailand, now
frequently sold to tourists. on it is depicted the Bodhisatta’s birth as a quail,
during the Vaṭṭaka-jātaka ( JA 35). in this story the young quail uses a saccakiriya
to hold back a forest fire, and it is said in the JA that no fire can be kindled there
for the remainder of the eon. in present-day Thailand, quail yantras are placed

inside the house – often inscribed on beams – to protect against fire. The power
of the truth uttered by the Bodhisatta is thus used to protect against a very
