as well as gaining efficacy through the power of truth and loving kindness,
the paritta suttas gain authority through being considered to be the words of the
Buddha or one of his enlightened disciples. as a buddha or arahat, such people are
well able to set an example in terms of both sacca and mettā, which are two of the
10 perfections. most of the paritta suttas are discourses given by the Buddha and
have parallels of varying levels of similarity throughout the Sutta Piṭaka. Three of
the paritta suttas – Khandaparitta (no. 10, verses found in JA 203), Mittānisaṃsa (no.
12, see JA 538) and Moraparitta (no. 13, see JA 159) – find parallels in jātaka stories.
in the Khandavatta-jātaka, the death of a monk from snakebite prompts the
telling of a story from the past, when the Bodhisatta advised his fellow ascetics
to speak verses of kindness towards snakes in order to avoid harm. These verses
are those of the Khandaparitta. in the Temiya-jātaka, the Bodhisatta is a prince
who pretends to be dumb in order to escape kingship and all the worldliness it
entails. about to be killed by his charioteer (on the order of the king) he declares
10 verses in praise of good friendship, which are those found in the Mittānisaṃsa.
in the Mora-jātaka, the Bodhisatta is a peacock who gains protection from certain
verses he has composed in praise of the sun and of the wise. The authority for
the latter two parittas thus comes from the fact that they were composed by
the Bodhisatta and used by him to good effect. The introductory verses to the
paritta suttas themselves mention the occasions in the Buddha’s previous lives
that prompted their use. in the case of the Khandaparitta, however, the paritta
sutta makes no mention of the use of the verses in the time of the Bodhisatta, but
places the authority and composition directly with the Buddha.
of course, the paritta texts are chanted in Pāli, and so the contents will not be
understood by the majority of those present, though Tambiah notes that:
for the Buddhist layman the fact that he does not understand does not mean
for him that the chants are mumbo-jumbo. He believes quite rightly that for
those who know Pali the words contain great wisdom and sense; his ignorance
is a reflection of his unworthiness and involvement in an inferior mode of life
compared with that of the monk.47
However, the contents might be seen as of secondary importance to the
monks too: Samuels emphasises the importance of the monks’ deportment,
appearance and sweetness of voice during the paritta ceremony, over and above
the contents of the texts, which are in many cases not even understood by the
46 my thanks to Peter Skilling for information about this practice, provided during a
conversation in December 2007.
47 S.J. Tambiah, Culture, Thought and Social Action: An Anthropological Perspective
(Cambridge, ma, 1985), pp. 26–7.

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 143
monks themselves.48 like the other examples examined thus far, the contents
are – once again – not the immediate source of meaning or power. However, as in
the other examples, the inherent association of the contents with both Buddha
and dhamma are essential.
THe PoWeR oF THe GeNRe
The practices we have examined thus far have helped to illuminate the different
levels on which jātaka materials – images, texts and words – function. Drawing
these different levels together is the ritual practice of consecrating a new
Buddha-image, as examined in its Northern Thai form by Donald Swearer.49 i
discussed in detail the symbolisms and functions of images of the Buddha
above. What i neglected to examine there, is that an image of the Buddha
only acquires significance after a ritual consecration. This ritual, as enacted in
Northern Thailand, is essentially biographical in focus, and attempts to recreate
symbolically the night during which the Bodhisatta became Buddha, thus
imbuing the image with ‘buddha-ness’.
During the image consecration ritual, the recitation of biographical texts,
including material about his previous lives, ‘reminds’ the statue who he is. Thus
the image becomes the Buddha through a narrative based ceremony: ‘as a gifted
actor becomes the person he or she plays by identifying with the character
portrayed in the drama, so the Buddha image becomes the Buddha’s double after
being instructed in the tathāgata’s life history.’50 as the image relives the Buddha’s
life so does the audience. Thus, Swearer hints, it is the buddha-biography’s ability
to function as an instructive narrative, as well as the particulars it contains about
the person of the Buddha, that ensure the efficacy of the ritual. Jātakas, as part of
that biography, help the image to become the Buddha, and help the devotees to
aspire to his biography. in addition, the Buddha’s life story becomes more than the
life of an individual: the recitation conjures up buddhahood itself, ‘instantiating
in the buddhābhiseka a paradigmatic truth’.51 Thus the image doesn’t just become
a person; rather it becomes a ‘type’, or a paradigm of buddhahood.
Swearer outlines two stages in the ritual of consecration: ‘instructing the
image’ and ‘empowering the image’.52 The first is achieved through the recitation
of biographical texts, which instruct the image in its life history, including
48 Samuels, ‘Texts memorized, Texts Performed’.
49 Donald K. Swearer, Becoming the Buddha: The Ritual of Image Consecration in Thailand
(Princeton, 2004). See also Donald K. Swearer, ‘Consecrating the Buddha’ in Donald S.
lopez Jr. (ed.), Buddhism in Practice (Princeton, 1995), pp. 50–58.
50 Swearer, Becoming the Buddha, p. 122.
51 Swearer, Becoming the Buddha, p. 122.
52 These are the chapter headings for chapters five and six of Swearer, Becoming the
Buddha.

144 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
past lives and vows to buddhahood. The second stage is achieved through the
recitation of texts that transfer the qualities of the Buddha – or of buddhahood
– to the image, thus broadening the focus beyond the person of the Buddha
to the universal paradigm of buddhahood. During the first stage of the ritual,
jātakas are important as part of the biography, which is instructive both to image
and devotees. although jātakas are not explicitly a focus for the second stage of
the ritual, they are ideologically present. For example, the main text used – the
Buddha Abhiseka – contains the following passages:
The lord Buddha, filled with boundless compassion, practiced the thirty
perfections for many eons (four asaṅkheyya and 100,000 kappa), finally attaining
enlightenment. To that Buddha i pay homage. may all the Buddha’s qualities
(guṇa) be invested in this image. may the Buddha’s boundless omniscience be
invested in this image until the Buddhist religion ceases to exist … mahākassapa
Thera approached the Buddha and inquired about the tradition of the Buddhas
(Buddhacarita). The Buddha then preached the Cariyapiṭaka to him. may the
truth of this teaching become a part of this Buddha image for the remainder of
the life of the religion … The Buddha, referring to the miracle of the Pokkhara
rainfall during the time of Prince Vessantara, preached the Mahāvessantara
Jātaka. may the supernatural power of the jātaka also be instilled in this Buddha
image for 5,000 rains retreats.53
Thus the idea that the Buddha’s enlightenment was only possible because of the
long path he traversed through many eons is used to justify the supernormal
greatness finally achieved by the Buddha, and transferred into the image. in
addition, the jātaka stories he taught (in the Cariyāpiṭaka and the Vessantara-
jātaka, texts which are explicitly related to the perfections) are also seen as
having special power which can be transferred to the image.
another link between the image of the Buddha and his long path of gradual
perfection is made in the Lakkhaṇa Sutta of the Dīgha Nikāya where his positive
actions in past births are said to be the reasons behind each physical feature
of the Buddha’s perfect body.54 Thus, as Skilling puts it, ‘the jātakas have, in a
sense, culminated in the image’.55 The link made between the powerful image
and a system of ethical or religiously meaningful acts means that devotion
to the image can be ethically justified and linked to his teachings as well as
example.56 although this link is not explicitly made during the ritual, it is clear
53 Swearer, Becoming the Buddha, pp. 162–3.
54 T.W. Rhys-Davids and J. estlin Carpenter (eds), Dīgha-nikāya (3 vols, london, 1889–
1911), vol. 3, p. 142ff.
55 Skilling, Past Lives of the Buddha, p. 68.
56 Devotion might even be viewed as necessary, in gratitude for the wonderful acts
of the past, as is argued by Berkwitz in relation to the Sinhala Thūpavaṃsa. in this text’s
description of the night of awakening, each of the perfections is brought to life as a warrior

Jātaka Ideology in Practice 145
that the many levels on which jātakas operate – as extension of the Buddha’s
biography, as requirement for his enlightenment and thus somehow closely
linked to enlightenment itself, and as powerful teachings – are all connected to
the consecration of images, as well as to the power they are subsequently said
to contain.
The different biographical layers of jātakas are paralleled by Swearer’s
assessment of the three buddhological themes he sees underlying the ritual:57
first, the Buddha as omnipresent through time and yet particularised in history,
which requires two lineages: that of previous buddhas and of Gotama’s previous
lives. Second, the equivalence between Buddha and dhamma: the conclusion
(and climax) of the ceremony is a re-enactment of the Buddha’s first sermon,
during which the image becomes the dhamma-kāya. a text identifying each part
of the Buddha’s physical body with an aspect of the dhamma makes this link
explicit. The third theme is that the image is the mediator of power. all of these
themes resonate with our discussion of jātaka stories, as personal narration and
typological exemplar, conducting power through an association with dhamma
and buddhahood. Thus jātakas, through being able to operate on each of these
levels, fit into each of the different aspects of the ritual.
CoNClUSioN
all the practices examined in this chapter demonstrate that it is the two
biographical aspects of jātaka stories (the associations with Buddha and
Bodhisatta) that allow them to give meaning to a variety of different activities.
in artistic portrayals jātakas can simultaneously narrate the Bodhisatta’s
biography and the Buddha’s dhamma; they can also represent these two aspects,
and thus take an important place in the complex web of symbolism at stūpa sites
and in temples. Their association with the powerful teachings of the Buddha
allows them to form part of paritta collections, where it is both the fact that such
parittas were used by the Bodhisatta, and taught by the Buddha, that allows them
to be considered efficacious. Jātaka manuscripts are also seen as having special
status, which i argued is once again because of this association with Buddha and
dhamma, making their identification as dhamma-kāya more meaningful. even in
those practices which are explicitly concerned with the final life of the Buddha,
his previous existences are seen as an essential counterpart because of being
both biography and teaching. So, for the ceremony that inaugurates a new
protecting the Buddha from māra’s army. The perfections each speak of their gratitude
for the great acts of the past, for example ‘Renunciation’ says ‘Will i not perform an act
of service for the Dear lord who protected me while giving up royal prosperity in crores
of births like this?’ (Stephen C. Berkwitz, Buddhist History in the Vernacular: The Power of the
Past in Late Medieval Sri Lanka (leiden, 2004), pp. 282–3).
57 Swearer, Becoming the Buddha, chapter 7, especially pp. 175–6.

146 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
Buddha image, his biography is related to him to bring him to life. Yet whilst this
happens, the devotees are simultaneously able to hear a dhamma teaching and
can resolve to participate in the Buddha’s biography.
The jātaka genre thus contributes to the dynamic interplay between Buddha,
dhamma and Buddhist, in a way that has little to do with the stories themselves
and much more to do with the established idea of what a jātaka is. Particularly
important are the associations between the genre and the perfections that
empower the Buddha, underscore his teaching of dhamma, and are aspired to
by all Buddhists to different degrees. The perfections provide a bridge between
the person of the Buddha (who is a buddha because he has acquired all the
perfections) and Buddhists who strive to follow both his example and teachings.
Therefore in the next and final chapter i will look more closely at the role of the
perfections in weaving together the many different levels on which the jātakas
operate.

Chapter 8
The Perfection of Storytelling
i began this study with a discussion of the varying ways in which jātakas can be
and have been defined. This helped to highlight the fact that defining jātakas as
part of the biography of the Bodhisatta, though the focus of the JA and of this
study, was only one of several possible definitions. as a consequence i concluded
that the origins of this definition within Theravāda Buddhism required serious
investigation. Such investigation has revealed that jātakas were not originally
conceived of as demonstrating the gradual perfection of the Bodhisatta, but
were in fact more strongly associated with the person of the Buddha, whose
accomplishments in the ‘present’ allowed him to remember and tell the stories,
each one appropriate for its audience. The absorption of many stories into
the early form of the JA naturally resulted in a diverse collection that then
benefited greatly from an overall framework and rationale that provided links
to both Buddha and Bodhisatta, and through them to the Buddhist path(s). Such
developments in the jātaka genre were influenced by developments in the history
of the Buddhist community and its doctrines about the paths to bodhi. The two
resulting biographical associations – with Buddha and Bodhisatta – allow the
stories to creatively resolve several tensions that surround the person of the
Buddha, such as his unique yet typological behaviour, his inimitable example,
and his teaching that is worthy of worship. They also allow the stories to work
on several different levels as artistic representations and in Buddhist practice.
Throughout this book i have argued that the structure of the jātaka genre
established and promoted by the JA means that the stories are as much examples
of the Buddha’s great teaching ability, as of the Bodhisatta’s long path to
perfection. The genre thus works on two different levels that are tied together
by the person of the Buddha. These two ideological layers are particularly visible
in the ways in which Buddhists interact with jātakas: as part of the sacred and
cosmic biography of the Buddha, jātakas demonstrate the magnificence of the
Buddha and of his path, but also provide Buddhists with the possibility of sharing
in such a biography; in this way they can function as examples or as objects
of worship that equate with the person of the Buddha. as dhamma teachings
jātakas demonstrate the Buddha’s great skill as teacher, as well as his wit and
wisdom, and allow all forms of narrative to be appropriated and legitimated; as
such they are didactic, but simultaneously worthy of worship as the Buddha’s
dhamma-kāya.
The link in this web that has recurred time and time again is the doctrine
of the 10 perfections. The perfections link the Bodhisatta to the Buddha, since
the opportunities for practising perfections in the jātakas allow him to progress

148 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
from one to the other. They also link the Bodhisatta and ordinary Buddhists,
since by practising the perfections one can emulate the path of the Bodhisatta,
even if one is right at the other end of the path. Finally, the perfections link
the Buddhist renunciant community (with the Buddha at their head) with lay
Buddhists, since through the practise of dāna (often portrayed in jātakas as the
highest perfection) the monks are supported materially, and the laity spiritually.
Thus by linking jātakas to the perfections, Theravāda scholastics allowed them
to be meaningful to both lay and renunciant Buddhists, on all stages of all
paths. Furthermore, the Lakkhaṇa Sutta of the Dīgha Nikāya links the superior
parts of the physical body of a buddha to their cause in the perfection of certain
qualities in previous births. Therefore the Buddha not only demonstrates his
gradual acquisition of the perfections and teaches their benefits, he is the literal
embodiment of them.
There are in fact three main players in this web: the Buddha, the Bodhisatta
and Buddhists. The stories are not just about the person and path of the
Buddha and Bodhisatta, for they allow Buddhists to explore the nature of their
relation to past and future buddhas as well as their own past and future selves.
The interactions between Buddha, Bodhisatta and Buddhist, facilitated by the
doctrine of the perfections, can be seen on both the level of individual stories
and texts, and in the ways in which the genre as a whole is used. To draw this
study to a conclusion, therefore, i would like to look at some examples of how
this interaction works.
a PeRFeCT SToRY: THe moNKeY GaRDeNeRS ReViSiTeD
let me return once more to the story with which i began: after visiting a garden
ruined by a foolish gardener, the Buddha tells of how a wise man (the Bodhisatta)
once commented upon the folly of monkeys who pulled up plants in order to see
how much water they might require. in this story there is an interaction between
Buddha, Bodhisatta and the Buddhist audience, mediated by the perfections.
The Buddha, because of his cultivation of the perfections, is able to see into the
distant past, and to relate events unknown to his audience. He also knows the
appropriate story to tell in a given situation, and can use it to entertain and
thereby – to paraphrase W.S. Gilbert – to trick into learning with a laugh. The
Bodhisatta, in the process of cultivating the perfections, demonstrates his wisdom
in observing the monkeys and commenting on their situation. By appreciating
that the Bodhisatta has now become the Buddha we can also see his wisdom in
following the Buddhist path, which prevents him from being drawn into karmic
repetition, as demonstrated by the gardener both in his birth as a monkey and in
his later human birth. Hearing this story a Buddhist may be inspired to develop
faith in the Buddha and his words, and to strive for greater wisdom. moreover
a Buddhist might aspire to eventual escape from the repetitive karmic cycles
that make repeating the same mistakes in birth after birth – as the gardener

The Perfection of Storytelling 149
did – almost inevitable. This escape requires the cultivation of the perfections
demonstrated by Buddha and Bodhisatta.
The Buddha, Bodhisatta and Buddhists are thus in a constant interaction,
and this is because of the qualities taught and demonstrated by the Buddha
and Bodhisatta, and appreciated and aspired to by the Buddhist audience. The
quality under discussion in this particular story is wisdom, in both a worldly
and Buddhist sense. To appreciate the importance of the framing of this story as
a jātaka, we might consider how the story would operate were the Buddha and
Bodhisatta absent. We would still learn that these monkeys are foolish, and that
the wise man is wise, and that the storyteller is witty. But without the presence
of a man on a path and another man demonstrating the end of this path, the
story has little impact. The framing as Bodhisatta-biography and Buddha-
dhamma make the story more able to communicate Buddhist ideals such as the
perfections, even where the central message of the story itself seems to be of
little importance.
as i have noted, it is the inclusion of the story in the JA with its united frame
that add these layers of value. The real success of the JA is its ability, through
its framing structure, to comment on kamma (with some continuity between
lives) and arahatship and buddhahood (with the realisation of anattā), heaven
and nibbāna, the repetitive nature of existence and the possibility of a finite
biography. individual stories can have little inherent Buddhist value, and yet the
perfection of the narrator and the understanding that the stories demonstrate
the pursuit of perfection, result in a perfect Buddhist story.
The interaction between Buddha, Bodhisatta and Buddhists is not only a
feature of the JA, nor is it limited to Theravāda jātakas composed in the shadow
of this great collection. in mahāyāna Buddhism these three groups remain
central, though here the characters are multiplied in number, time and place.
By highlighting the common importance of interactions between these three
groups in both mahāyāna ideology and Theravāda jātaka texts i do not intend to
suggest that jātakas are ‘proto-mahāyāna’, nor that they in any way compromise
Theravāda ideology. Rather, the importance of the Bodhisatta and his path in
early Buddhism and the continued development of this theme in Theravāda texts
demonstrates that the gulf between mahāyāna and other Buddhist traditions is
not, perhaps, as great as it is often perceived to be. after all, these traditions co-
existed in South asia for many years; even Sri lanka was influenced by mahāyāna
ideals, most strongly in the abhayagiri Vihāra, but also in the mahāvihāra
commentarial tradition. as a step towards understanding the relationship
between jātakas and the bodhisatta path, i hope that my study also contributes
towards a wider understanding of the relationship between mahāyāna and non-
mahāyāna (i hesitate to use the term Śrāvakayāna for obvious reasons) ideas
about buddhahood and bodhisatt(v)ahood.
When talking about non-mahāyāna Buddhism i am of course referring to
more than just the school which eventually identified itself as Theravāda. most
likely because the process of compiling jātakas ran over into the period after

150 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
the geographical dispersal of Buddhism, each school of early indian Buddhism
has a different number of jātakas as well as a different way of organising them.1
No school has a collection comparable to the JA, and so the generic associations
of jātakas propagated in that text must be seen as specific to the Theravāda
school. The different early indian texts adhere to these generic characteristics
to different levels. For example, the many jātakas in the Mūlasarvāstivāda vinaya
and the Mahāvastu share the biographical framing in the teaching career of the
Buddha, which is also central to the JA. Āryaśūra’s Jātakamālā, on the other hand,
is a literary Sanskrit composition, with no pretence of being buddhavacana. it
does, however, claim to illustrate the exemplary behaviour of the Bodhisatta.
The Liu Du Ji Jing – a Chinese jātaka collection translated from Sanskrit in the
third century ce – goes a step further by structuring its stories around the six
perfections.2 The analysis i have made of the JA and the Theravāda tradition
of jātakas more generally may not be applicable to all Buddhist jātakas, but the
widespread portrayals of the perfections of the Buddha and Bodhisatta suggest
there may be considerable overlap.
a PeRFeCT GeNRe: VeSaK CeleBRaTioNS iN SRi laNKa
one final example will help to demonstrate that the association with the
perfections, as well as with the Buddha and Bodhisatta, allow the jātaka genre
to play a vibrant role in Buddhist societies. This will involve taking a step away
from the level of individual stories and texts, in an examination of the role of the
genre as a whole in an important Buddhist festival: the celebration of Vesak in
Sri lanka.3 This festival marks the day upon which three major events in the life
of the Buddha – his birth, awakening and death (or complete awakening) – are
1 We have, after all, little understanding of how the fifth nikāya or āgama of the
early Buddhist schools was put together. as collections of stories it is possible that jātaka
texts may have been only preserved orally, or in local languages. This might explain a text
such as Āryaśūra’s Jātakamālā, which clearly had a prior collection as its source, perhaps a
collection similar to the JA. The survival of the Jātakamālā in the absence of its source text
could be explained by its superior (and not exclusively Buddhist) literary form. However,
if jātaka collections like the JA existed and shared its biographical associations, it seems
unlikely that a literary authored text would survive and not a buddhavacana collection.
The varying types of texts containing jātakas, as well as the survival of some smaller
collections exclusively of jātakas, suggest to me that it is more likely that each school
developed different ways of organising their own jātakas. However, much more work is
required before the history of such collections can be understood.

(4 vols, Paris: adrien-maisonneuve, 1962, first published 1910–1935 1962), vol. 1, stories

3 During this section, which is based on fieldwork carried out in Sri lanka during
Vesak week 2007, i will make use of Sinhala terms. i have transliterated these according to

The Perfection of Storytelling 151
believed to have occurred. it is thus necessarily focused upon the biography
of the Buddha. The activities which make up the festival are imbued with
significance because of their association with the Buddha’s life story. although
the festival is focused upon events in the final life of the Buddha, Jātaka stories
form a prominent part of the celebrations, and are tied to the final-life biography
through their associations with the perfections required for buddhahood as well
as their place in the dhamma-teachings given by the Buddha.
of central importance to the Vesak celebrations is the moment of awakening,
which forms the backdrop to many of the more prominent activities during
Sri lankan Vesak. many Buddhists make or buy lanterns and hang coloured
lights around their homes and gardens, as a symbol of the rays of light that
emanated from the Buddha’s body as he achieved buddhahood. The shadvarna
(six-coloured) flag which is now flown in every Buddhist residence during Vesak
week also represents these rays of light, said to be blue, yellow, red, white and
orange, followed by a combination of the five (Sin pabasara; P pabhāsa – literally
‘brightness’ or ‘illumination’). other aspects of the festivities, such as the singing
of Bhakti gī (devotional songs, originally inspired by the use of carols at Christmas)
and the sending of cards are relatively recent ‘cultural borrowings’, and though
the content is specifically Buddhist, it is not clear how relevant to Vesak in
particular these activities are. The cards contain Sinhalese verses praising the
Buddha and invoking the blessings of the triple gem on the recipient, and the
songs contain similar praises, and sometimes include biographical descriptions
that highlight the Buddha’s greatness. in addition, one popular justification for
the songs, apparently used by mrs Pushpa Hewavitarana when she argued for a
revival of the practice in 1956, is that the devas burst into song when they saw
that Gotama had become Buddha. Thus an attempt is made to link this practice
too with the moment of awakening.
The moment of awakening is the result of a long preparation and a sustained
cultivation of the perfections. according to many Buddhists, a celebration of the
Buddha’s life is really a celebration of the Buddhist path that he demonstrated,
and therefore it comes as no suprise that the perfections are also closely tied
up in Sri lankan Vesak celebrations. as is the case throughout the year, the
two most prominent perfections are sīla and dāna. The former is observed with
greater than normal strictness during Vesak, when Buddhists traditionally
strive extra hard to keep the pansil (P pañcasīlāni – five precepts), and some take
on a further three or five precepts temporarily. The observation of the first and
fifth precepts – against killing and consuming intoxicants – is even enforced to a
certain extent by the state: the sale of alcohol is strictly prohibited during Vesak
week, and the slaughter of animals is also avoided for the day itself, while some
animals due for slaughter are set free. There is also a great flurry of dāna: the
usual temple gifts are swelled in numbers and supplemented by the traditional
the same criteria as Pāli transliterations to allow consistency and easy comparison with
Pāli cognates.

152 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
giving of robes to monks and books to piriveṇas (monastic schools). Devotees
dressed in white carrying bags of money process with their extended family
towards the temple. Temple visitors are bombarded with pleas to donate to one
or another good cause, and roadside stalls appear with free food and drink for
all passers-by. a gift of the body is said to be still more meritorious, and the gift
of blood is a widespread practice: newspaper advertisements invite all (18 to
55 years of age, over 50 kilograms in weight) to practice the perfection of dāna.
Containing even clearer resonances with jātaka literature is the registration at a
Colombo temple’s 2007 Vesak celebrations of corneas to be donated to the blind.4
This practice is a deliberate emulation of the very popular Sibi-jātaka, where the
Bodhisatta gives away his eyes in a demonstration of his great dāna.5
Jātakas, especially thanks to their strong association with the perfections, are
thus subtly invoked in some Vesak activities. However, there is also explicit use
of jātaka stories, the most obvious being on toraṇas (also called pandal). These are
large wooden structures covered with illustrated boards or canvases and wired
up to hundreds of coloured bulbs that are set to flash in a variety of complex
patterns. The former illustrate jātaka stories, historical events or scenes from
the life of the Buddha ( jātakas are easily the most popular), with an image of the
Buddha himself forming a centrepiece. The latter imitate the light emitting from
the Buddha as he attained enlightenment. During Vesak week evenings, crowds
gather around the base of the toraṇa. in case the audience are only there for the
magnificent lights, they may be reminded of the true significance by a ceremony
involving such activities as raising the Buddhist flag, burning incense, chanting
the three refuges, and listening to a sermon based on the story in question. only
then is the image of the Buddha uncovered and the lights lit, with spectacular
effect. in some places the painted scenes are brought to life by singers or actors,
broadcast over the huge sound system that is incoporated into the toraṇa.
most of the practices of Sri lankan Vesak are relatively recent: the lanterns
and flags are said to date from the late nineteenth century; the singing also dates
from the colonial period and the cards from even more recent times. However,
the use of jātaka illustrations during Sri lankan festivals may be attested to in
much older sources, although admittedly in this case the festival is not Vesak
but the parade of the Sacred Tooth Relic. The fourth-century Chinese pilgrim

Faxian witnessed one such parade and records that 10 days beforehand the king
appointed someone to remind the people of the Bodhisatta’s great acts with a
declaration beginning:
The Bodhisattva practiced for the incalculably long time of three asaṅkhyeya
(immeasurable) kalpas, never sparing his own life. He gave up his kingdom, his
wife, and his child. He even tore out his eyes to give them to others. He cut his
own flesh to ransom a dove, gave his head as alms, offered his body to feed a
famished tigress, and did not begrudge his marrow and brain.6
Following this proclamation:
the king set up on both sides of the road [images of] the various transmigratory
forms of the Bodhisattva in his past five hundred incarnations, such as Sudāna,
Śyāmaka, the elephant king, a deer, and a horse. all these forms, true to life,
were painted in colors and richly adorned.7
The implication is that for each story the Bodhisatta’s character was represented
by painted statues. Contemporary Vesak banners may not attempt to illustrate
all the jātakas, nor do they focus so explicitly on the person of the Bodhisatta,
but there is still a striking continuity between the two festivals. in both cases the
intention is undoubtedly to demonstrate the greatness of the person who forms
the subject of the festival, for, like Vesak, the Tooth Relic Perahera is focused
upon the person of the Buddha.8
one advantage of the modern tendency to illustrate just a few jātaka
stories, rather than all as happened in Faxian’s day, is that they can be carefully

chosen. For example the primary toraṇa in central Kandy in 2007 illustrated
the Mātupōsaka-jātakaya (Mātiposaka-jātaka, JA 455) in which the Bodhisatta
is an elephant devoted to looking after his mother. one day he helps a lost
traveller find the way home, but the latter hears a proclamation that the king
requires a new royal elephant, and subsequently betrays the Bodhisatta into the
service of the king. The Bodhisatta tells the king of his duty to his mother and
is allowed to return home. after his mother dies and he performs the funeral
rites, he lives with some hermits who subsequently build an image of the
Bodhisatta to worship. The other, smaller, Kandy toraṇa (a few hundred yards
away) illustrated the Mahāsupina-jātakaya (Mahāsupina-jātaka, JA 77), which tells
how the Bodhisatta correctly interpreted the king’s 16 dreams, each of which is
portrayed in a separate scene. The toraṇa outside Kelaniya temple illustrated the
Apannaka-jātakaya (Apaṇṇaka-jātaka, JA 1) where the Bodhisatta is a wise caravan
leader. other toraṇa in Colombo portrayed Sama-jātakaya (Sāma-jātaka, JA 540; the
Bodhisatta cares for his blind parents), Kelisīla-jātakaya (Keḷisīla-jātaka, JA 202;
the Bodhisatta as Sakka teaches a king to respect old age), and Samibulā-jātakaya
(Sambula-jātaka, JA 519; the Bodhisatta admonishes his son for mistreating his
wife despite her loyalty in the face of danger). in addition to the toraṇa, Vesak is a
time for plays and dances in the villages of Sri lanka. The plays more often than
not take jātaka stories as their base,9 and once again the most popular jātakas for
enacting are strongly biographical in focus: according to Disanayaka, the four
most popular are Kusa ( JA 531), Vidhurapaṇḍita ( JA 545), Ummagga ( JA 546) and
Vessantara ( JA 547).10 The latter three are the last stories of the jātaka collection,
and are thus associated strongly with the perfections. in contrast to modern
jātaka books, therefore, jātaka stories at Vesak seem to be chosen with the specific
aim of holding up the Bodhisatta as a good example of both specifically Buddhist
qualities and responsible social practices.
The jātakas used in Vesak are thus deliberately used as snippets from an
exemplary biography, and one way they fit into the rest of the festival is by
extending the Buddha’s biography back to the time when he was a Bodhisatta.
The biography is pushed back still further by a form of Vesak dance called Sūvisi
vivarana naeṭum, ‘Dance of the 24 predictions’, which is performed in villages in
the Kandy district. accompanied by song, the dance narrates the 24 predictions
to buddhahood given to Gotama by the 24 previous buddhas, as related in the
Nidānakathā.11 Just as in the texts and the temple illustrations, the biography of
Gotama Buddha includes this narration, as well as narrations of jātaka stories
9 according to J.B. Disanayaka (The Vesak Fullmoon Festival (Nugegoda, Sri lanka,
1993), p. 26), apart from the general popularity of the stories, one possible reason for this

in contrast, is not a problem. This allows biographical plays to be enacted during a
biographical festival.

The Perfection of Storytelling 155
such as the ones painted on toraṇa and enacted in village plays. Thus although
the festival is only explicitly concerned with the Buddha’s birth, enlightenment
and death, the rest of his biography is also celebrated as what allowed and led to
the final moment of awakening.
it is not only the long biography of the great being who eventually became
the Buddha that is celebrated. Since Vesak is a celebration of the Buddha, and
the reason Buddhists celebrate the Buddha is because of his dhamma, the latter
is of central importance during the festival. i have already noted the strong
emphasis on the perfections during Vesak week, especially sīla and dāna, which
are of course part of the dhamma teachings. The perfections, as a rationale for
the Buddhist path, form a link between jātakas and the final life of the Buddha.
They also provide a link between a Buddhist’s own aspirations and the personal
biography of the Buddha/Bodhisatta. This link is sometimes made explicit, for
example in a special Vesak feature article in The Sunday Times that lays out each of
the perfections we should be striving towards with examples from the Buddha’s
own previous lives.12 Such aspirations are not wholly towards the perfections
or buddhahood, though; an article two pages later lays out some instructions
on how to achieve rebirth as the king of the gods,13 and this attitude is amply
reflected in the preoccupation with the acquisition of merit through worship
of the Buddha during the festival. as always, these two types of aspiration and
preoccupation happily co-exist, and both – as we have seen in earlier chapters
– find legitimation and inspiration in jātaka stories.
Whether helping the devotee towards nibbāna or rebirth in the heavenly
realms, many other aspects of the dhamma also form part of the rationale for
Vesak activities. The newspapers try to educate readers about Buddhist ideals, as
well as the history and significance of the festival. in addition they contain sundry
small articles about Vesak activities around the island, as well as reviews of the
major Vesak annuals. There are several of the latter in circulation, containing
a great variety of articles, including scholarly examinations of Buddhist
philosophy by some of the island’s top professors, devotional poetry by leading
authors, and news articles about Buddhism worldwide. in addition there are
special programmes on Sri lankan television channels, including debates about
Buddhist issues, devotional songs, and live broadcasts of Vesak celebrations.
everywhere Buddhist ideals are being promoted to the general public. Jātakas,
of course, are a traditional method of imparting Buddhist teachings in Sri lanka.
Their link to Vesak is therefore not just through their association with the
biography of the person whose life is being celebrated; jātakas have a practical
role to play in communicating the dhamma, and as such demonstrate the great
teaching ability of the Buddha.
12 Upali Salgado, ‘a Stepping Stone to end Suffering’ in The Sunday Times Plus, 29
april 2007, p. 4.
13 Chandrani Fernando, ‘How to be Reborn as Sakka, King of Gods’ in The Sunday
Times Plus, 29 april 2007, p. 6.

156 Jātaka Stories in Theravāda Buddhism
We can now see that there are several layers of interaction between jātakas
and Vesak. The Bodhisatta set an example through, amongst other things, his
great dāna. This is emulated by devotees during Vesak (both explicitly and
implicitly associated with jātaka stories), as well as taught as a virtue through
toraṇa, plays, newspaper and annual articles and so on. as is commonly said by
Buddhists, the greatest gift of all is the gift of the dhamma, and this great gift
given by the Buddha forms the focus for much of Vesak. once again, these two
biographical elements of jātaka stories – their association with the Buddha as
teacher and Bodhisatta as celebrated example – allow them to operate on these
different levels during the festival, linked by the 10 perfections.
as with many festivals, the religious significance of Vesak is caught up in
a carnival-like atmosphere. it is difficult to say what religious significance is
intended by the grand parade of fire-spinners, Kandyan drummers and dancers,
and finely attired elephants, put on by the Temple of the Tooth in Kandy. There is
also an underlying spirit of rivalry, most clearly seen in the lantern competitions
held in every major town. as noted above, the symbolism of the lantern is
clear, and in addition many of the more elaborate lanterns take a biographical
theme, yet the intense secrecy during preparation and the displays of large,
commercially-sponsored creations in roadside booths with proud-looking
creators standing nearby betray a more competitive, rather than devotional,
spirit. in addition, the large toraṇa on display in Kandy market in 2007 declared
itself to be the work of a local electrical company, whose business address and
telephone number were provided in large letters on the base of the display; one
assumes this was not to make delivery of the merit easier, but rather to turn
admirers into customers. The variety of levels on which the festival operates, of
course, are not mutually exclusive. Vesak is a time of celebration, of community
spirit and being with the family. The bright lights, competitions and elephants
do not prevent engagement with the religious meaning of the festival; rather
they make it more accessible and enduring. There is therefore one final link to
be made between Vesak and jātaka stories: both serve as a vehicle for Buddhist
teachings, yet through containing many layers of meaning and engagement,
they remain accessible and appealing to all.
a PeRFeCT BioGRaPHY: HaPPY eNDiNGS
Throughout my examination of the texts and practices involving jātaka stories
the recurring themes are clear: as Bodhisatta biography jātakas demonstrate the
magnificence of the Buddha and of his path, and in this way jātaka images and
texts can function as icons or objects of worship that equate with the person of
the Buddha and embody his perfection (with its associated power). However,
jātakas also provide Buddhists with the possibility of sharing in such a biography,
and so the interaction between jātaka image/text/words and Buddhist is a
dynamic one. as dhamma teachings jātakas demonstrate the Buddha’s great skill

The Perfection of Storytelling 157
as teacher, which allows an appreciation of both the contents of the teachings
and the person of the teacher. The twofold association with Buddha’s dhamma and
Bodhisatta’s biography thus plays a central part in practices involving jātakas,
and helps to explain the enduring popularity of the stories. all these aspects
are linked by the perfections that give the Buddha his power, the Bodhisatta his
purpose, the dhamma its rationale, and Buddhists their ideals.
These ideals are varied, reflecting the various levels on which Buddhist
doctrine and practice operate. on one level the stories encourage worldly
wisdom, simple good conduct, or the sort of generous acts that lead to a
happier rebirth. Doubtless the interaction between many Buddhists and jātaka
stories is limited to this level, but there are also other possibilities. Ultimately,
of course, the Buddha’s narration of the tiresome repetition in his and other
people’s past lives, and his own escape from this painful cycle, should inspire the
audience to pursue the path to liberation themselves. Thus the goal is not to live
happily ever after, since this possibility is denied by the three characteristics of
existence – impermanence, suffering and not-self. Rather, the happy ending for
the Buddhist jātaka audience is escape from their own biography, through the
help of the Buddha’s.
